# PROJECT_STATE

Portable state of the guided build. Updated at every meaningful milestone so the
project can be continued in a new conversation, or by a different AI assistant,
without relying on model memory.

On 2026-08-23 it was the *only* signal in an otherwise perfectly green 337-test build that three of 16.3's production files had been applied into `src/test/java`, so the deployable jar was missing `ResendEmailProvider` entirely. It was first misread here as an Eclipse artifact — Eclipse and Maven do share `target/classes`, and a mid-build recompile can produce the same warning — which is exactly why the warning deserves a check rather than an explanation. **`git status` is the check**, and it took five seconds. Count the classes as well as the tests: `ls target/surefire-reports/*.txt \| wc -l`. A class's elapsed time also reveals whether it started a container of its own — under a second means it reused a cached context, twenty-odd seconds means a fifth container appeared |

### What 4.5b delivers

`POST /api/v1/auth/refresh` and `/logout` accept the refresh token **either** from
the `garajul_meu_refresh` cookie **or** from a `{"refreshToken": "..."}` body, and
answer on whichever channel the request used. No client-type header is needed,
as specification section 14 requires: the request itself reveals how the client
works.

Login always sets the `HttpOnly` `Secure` `SameSite=Strict` cookie scoped to
`/api/v1/auth`, and additionally returns the token in the body only when the
caller sends `"refreshTokenInBody": true` — so a browser's JavaScript never sees
it, while a native client, which has no cookie jar, can ask for it. Only `/login`
needs that flag: at login the client has presented nothing yet, so the backend
cannot infer the channel. On `/refresh` and `/logout` it can, and does.

`AuthService.login` is no longer `@Transactional(readOnly = true)`: issuing a
refresh token writes a row.

Logout is idempotent — logging out twice, or with no token at all, answers 204
and clears the cookie regardless.

## How this project is built — working agreement

This is a **guided build**, not code generation. The cycle is: explain → give the
exact command or file → the developer applies it → run → verify → fix → continue.
Stop at each meaningful milestone and wait for the developer's actual output
before going further.

| Rule | Detail |
|---|---|
| Who writes files | **The developer.** Provide the exact path, whether it is new or modified, the full content, and the reasoning. Do not write source files unless explicitly asked. **Never say "replace the second test" or "change lines 22–24"** — on 2026-08-15 a replacement test landed in the wrong `describe` block, silently deleting a different test and leaving the original in place, and the suite failed for a reason that looked nothing like the cause. Whole files, or a single unambiguous line with enough surrounding text to be unmistakable. |
| This document | Maintained by the assistant, not the developer. Update it at every milestone. |
| Language | Explanations in Romanian; technical terms, code, comments and identifiers in English. |
| Verification | Read the log, not just `BUILD SUCCESS`. **Always check the total test count, and on the frontend the test-file count too** — a test lost to a bad edit leaves the build green and the number smaller, and a whole file that never reached disk removes its entire block at once. Both have now happened, on 2026-08-13 and 2026-08-14. A milestone is not done until the predicted number is the observed number. |
| Versions | Verify current documentation before giving version-specific instructions. Spring Boot 4 renamed many artifacts and packages, and most material online targets 3.x. |
| Paste fragility | Long files and nested XML have repeatedly lost lines in transit — and on 2026-08-15 a single step lost **three** files: one absent, two created but **empty**. An empty source file is valid TypeScript and raises nothing, and a file nothing imports yet raises nothing either, so neither the compiler nor the test count reveals it. **Standing procedure from 2026-08-15: after the developer applies a multi-file step, the assistant verifies on disk — existence, non-zero size, expected exports — before anything is run.** It costs seconds and removes the entire class of failure. If a paste fails twice on the same content, ask before switching approach. |
| Scope | Implement only V1 features from the specification. Surface any deviation and wait for approval rather than deciding alone. |

Narrowing it means a service account created in IAM with only the messaging role, and costs nothing later: the provider reads a variable and does not care which account is behind it. `google-services.json` can be downloaded again at any time; the private key cannot, and generating a replacement means revoking the first |

## Flyway migrations

Flyway runs at startup over the direct (non-pooled) endpoint. Migrations live in
`backend/src/main/resources/db/migration`.

| Version | File | Applied to |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `V1__create_users_table.sql` | Neon `development`, and every Testcontainers run |
| 2 | `V2__create_verification_tokens_table.sql` | Neon `development`, and every Testcontainers run |
| 3 | `V3__create_refresh_tokens_table.sql` | Neon `development`, and every Testcontainers run |
| 4 | `V4__create_vehicles_and_registration_certificates.sql` | Neon `development` (2026-08-16), and every Testcontainers run |
| 5 | `V5__create_ocr_usage_table.sql` | every Testcontainers run; **not yet applied to Neon `development`** |
| 6 | `V6__create_vehicle_documents_table.sql` | every Testcontainers run, and Neon `development` (2026-08-17, by the running backend) |
| 7 | `V7__create_notification_preferences_table.sql` | every Testcontainers run; **not yet applied to Neon `development`** |
| 8 | `V8__create_reminders_table.sql` | every Testcontainers run; **not yet applied to Neon `development`** |
| 9 | `V9__create_user_devices_table.sql` | every Testcontainers run; **not yet applied to Neon `development`** |
| 10 | `V10__create_notification_deliveries_table.sql` | every Testcontainers run; **not yet applied to Neon `development`**. The **tenth and last table of section 10** — the data dictionary is complete |
| 11 | `V11__encrypt_push_tokens.sql` | every Testcontainers run; **not yet applied to Neon `development`**. **Adds no table** — the data dictionary is still ten. It deletes every row of `user_devices`, adds `push_token_hash VARCHAR(64) NOT NULL`, and swaps the unique index off the token onto the hash |

**Phase 12 needed no migration either, and for the same reason.** `V4` created
`image_object_key`, `image_content_type` and `image_size_bytes` on `vehicles`
back when Phase 7 mapped six columns of the table; 12.3 mapped them and added
nothing to the schema. Four phases passed with those three columns present and
unmapped, which `ddl-auto: validate` permits — it checks that every mapped
property has a column, not that every column has a property.

**Phase 8 needed no migration at all.** `V4` created all thirty-seven columns
while Phase 7 mapped only six of them, so the certificate's other twenty-eight
fields arrived as entity mappings and nothing else — no `V5`, no checksum risk,
nothing to apply to Neon. That was the reason for creating the whole table in
one migration, and it paid off exactly as intended.

`V1` creates `users` per specification section 10.1, with `pk_users`, a
`ck_users_preferred_language` CHECK limiting the column to `ro`/`en`, and the
unique index `ux_users_email`.

`V2` creates `verification_tokens` per section 10.10, with a
`ON DELETE CASCADE` foreign key to `users` — which is how section 24 account
deletion reaches this table — a CHECK on the three token types, and the index
`ix_verification_tokens_user_type`.

`V4` creates **both** `vehicles` (section 10.2) and `registration_certificates`
(section 10.3) in one migration, because section 9 leaves a vehicle with no
identity of its own: the certificate is the source of truth for registration
number, VIN, make and commercial description, so a `vehicles` table on its own
would hold rows nobody could name. All thirty-five certificate columns are
created now even though Phase 7 maps only four — adding a column later is a new
migration, and there is no reason to spend four of them on a schema already
known in full. `ix_vehicles_user` serves the garage listing, which runs on every
load; `ux_registration_certificates_vehicle` enforces the one-to-one rather than
trusting it; and `ux_registration_certificates_user_vin` is the section 9 rule
that one account cannot hold the same VIN twice.

**Migrations live only in `src/main/resources/db/migration`.** A copy under
`src/test/resources` makes tests pass while the application fails to start:
tests see both `target/test-classes` and `target/classes` on the classpath, but
a running application sees only the latter. This happened once, on 2026-08-13,
and was caught by `ddl-auto: validate` refusing to boot against Neon with a
missing table.

**An applied migration is never edited.** Flyway stores its checksum; changing
the file afterwards fails the next startup with a checksum mismatch. Every
schema change from here is a new `V2__`, `V3__` file.

**`V11` was edited in place on 2026-08-23, hours after it was written, and the
exception is worth stating precisely so it does not become a habit.** The rule
protects databases that already hold the migration; V11 had run against nothing
but throwaway Testcontainers, so there was no checksum anywhere to mismatch. The
edit was one word — `CHAR(64)` to `VARCHAR(64)` — and it had to happen because
PostgreSQL implements `CHAR` as `bpchar`, which Hibernate reports as a type
mismatch against a plain `String` field: with `ddl-auto: validate` the
SessionFactory refused to build and **the application could not start at all**.
The safe alternative, a corrective `V12` doing `ALTER COLUMN … TYPE`, was
weighed and declined only because the migration was provably unapplied. **If
`V11` had ever run against Neon, the answer would have been `V12`.** Should a
checksum mismatch on version 11 ever appear, it means exactly that — the local
database did run it — and the recovery is `mvnw flyway:repair` against the
direct endpoint.

## Implemented modules and endpoints

### backend — Spring Boot 4.1.1, Java 21, `ro.garajulmeu`

| Item | State |
|---|---|
| `BackendApplication` | boots on Tomcat 11.0.22, port 8080 |
| `GET /actuator/health` | `UP`, including `db: UP` (PostgreSQL); liveness and readiness groups exposed |
| Runtime dependencies | `spring-boot-starter-webmvc`, `-actuator`, `-data-jpa`, `-flyway`, `org.flywaydb:flyway-database-postgresql`, `org.postgresql:postgresql` (runtime), `spring-boot-devtools` (runtime, optional) |
| Test dependencies | `spring-boot-starter-webmvc-test`, `spring-boot-starter-actuator-test` |
| ORM | Hibernate ORM 7.4.1.Final (Spring Boot 4 baseline) |
| Configuration | `src/main/resources/application.yml`, committed, placeholders only |
| Local secrets | `backend/application-local.yml`, gitignored, loaded via `spring.config.import: optional:file:./application-local.yml` |
| Artifact | `target/backend-0.0.1-SNAPSHOT.jar`, repackaged as an executable jar |

The `/api/v1` surface implemented so far is the authentication block, the
account-management endpoints — `GET`, `PATCH` and `DELETE /api/v1/users/me`, and
`POST /me/change-password`, `/me/change-email` and `/me/confirm-email-change`,
which is the whole of Phase 4 — plus the whole vehicle surface from Phase 7:
`GET`, `POST /api/v1/vehicles`, and `GET`, `PATCH`, `DELETE
/api/v1/vehicles/{vehicleId}`. See the package tables below.

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.exception`

| Class | Role |
|---|---|
| `ErrorCode` | The canonical catalogue from specification section 17, each code carrying its HTTP status so the same failure cannot answer differently in two endpoints. `AUTHENTICATION_REQUIRED` covers both a missing bearer token and one that fails verification — one code, because the client's next move is the same either way |
| `ApiException` | Thrown for expected business failures; carries an `ErrorCode`, never user-facing text |
| `ApiErrorResponse` | The single JSON shape for every failure: `code`, `status`, `path`, `timestamp`, `fieldErrors`. Deliberately has **no message field**, so the frontend cannot display untranslated server English |
| `GlobalExceptionHandler` | `@RestControllerAdvice` mapping every exception to that shape. Handles `MethodArgumentTypeMismatchException` since 7.1 — a path or query value that will not convert, in practice an identifier that is not a UUID. Without it the catch-all answered 500 and logged at ERROR, so from Phase 15 a mistyped URL would have raised a Sentry alert for a fault entirely the caller's. It went unnoticed until Phase 7 because no endpoint before it had a path parameter |

Log levels follow specification section 27: expected 4xx outcomes log at INFO,
only unhandled exceptions log at ERROR — so Sentry, from Phase 15, will not
raise alerts for ordinary business failures.

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.user`

| Class | Role |
|---|---|
| `User` | `@Entity` on `users`. Id is `@GeneratedValue` UUID; timestamps come from Hibernate `@CreationTimestamp` / `@UpdateTimestamp`. `equals` uses the id alone and `hashCode` is constant, so an entity stays findable in a `HashSet` after Hibernate assigns its id |
| `Language` | `RO("ro")`, `EN("en")` — the code is the same IETF tag i18next and the email templates use |
| `LanguageConverter` | `@Converter(autoApply = true)`. `@Enumerated(STRING)` would write `RO`/`EN` and break the CHECK constraint |
| `UserRepository` | `JpaRepository<User, UUID>` with `findByEmail` and `existsByEmail`; both expect an already-normalised address |
| `UserService` | `profileOf`, `updateProfile`, `changePassword`, `deleteAccount`. Injects `PasswordEncoder` and `RefreshTokenService` — the deliberate `user` → `auth` edge recorded in the decisions table. `validZone` checks the timezone against `ZoneId.getAvailableZoneIds()`, because membership of a set resolved at runtime cannot be expressed as a Bean Validation annotation without a custom constraint. `deleteAccount` is a hard delete followed by an explicit `flush()`, so the foreign-key cascade runs inside the method rather than at commit |
| `UserController` | `GET /api/v1/users/me` — the first protected route; `PATCH /me` → 200 with the updated profile; `POST /me/change-password` → **204**; `POST /me/change-email` → **204** (nothing has changed yet, a code has been sent); `POST /me/confirm-email-change` → **200 with the profile**, because the account has just changed in two ways at once and the client must react to both; `DELETE /me` → **204**, carrying a body and clearing the refresh cookie on the way out. For all six the identity comes from the verified token's `sub`, never from a path or query parameter, which is what makes another account unreachable by editing a URL. Injects `UserService`, `AuthService` — the email change is a verification-code flow and belongs to `auth`, while the URL belongs to the account — and `RefreshCookies`, solely to clear the cookie on deletion |
| `dto.UserProfileResponse` | Response DTO rather than the entity, so `passwordHash` is never one Jackson change away from the wire |
| `dto.UpdateProfileRequest` | Partial update: every component optional, absent means unchanged. Deliberately carries **no** `@NotBlank` — that would forbid absence, which is the whole point of PATCH |
| `dto.ChangePasswordRequest` | `currentPassword` `@NotBlank` with no length bound; `newPassword` 12–128, the same bound as registration and reset |
| `dto.ChangeEmailRequest` | `newEmail` `@Email @Size(max = 320)`, matching the column; `currentPassword` with no length bound, for the same reason as above |
| `dto.ConfirmEmailChangeRequest` | The code alone, `@Pattern("\\d{6}")`. The requested address is **not** repeated here — it rides on the token, so a confirmation cannot name a different address from the one the owner was shown |
| `dto.DeleteAccountRequest` | The current password alone. Of the three endpoints that ask for it this is the one that most needs it: no support process can restore what this removes |

Email normalisation — trim and lower-case — is the service layer's
responsibility. The entity stores what it is given, and the unique index
enforces one account per address.

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.auth`

| Class | Role |
|---|---|
| `VerificationTokenType` | `EMAIL_VERIFICATION`, `PASSWORD_RESET`, `EMAIL_CHANGE`. Codes are never interchangeable between purposes, so the type is part of every lookup |
| `VerificationToken` | `@Entity` on `verification_tokens`. Holds the owner as a plain `userId`, not a `@ManyToOne` — the auth module never needs the `User` object from a token, and the foreign key still enforces integrity. `isUsable(now)` requires unused **and** not superseded **and** unexpired |
| `VerificationTokenRepository` | `findFirstByUserIdAndTypeOrderByCreatedAtDesc` for the newest code, and `invalidateOutstandingCodes` — a `@Modifying` bulk update so a double "resend" cannot leave two codes both valid |
| `VerificationCodeGenerator` | `SecureRandom` plus `%06d` padding. Padding matters: returning "42" instead of "000042" would shrink the effective code space |
| `AuthProperties` | `@ConfigurationProperties("garajul-meu.auth")`: 15 minutes code validity, 5 attempts, 30 days refresh-token validity |
| `RefreshToken` | `@Entity` on `refresh_tokens`. Rows are **never deleted on rotation** — a spent token must stay findable, because that is exactly how a replay is detected |
| `RefreshTokenRepository` | `findByTokenHash` on the unique index, plus two `@Modifying(flushAutomatically, clearAutomatically)` bulk updates: `revokeFamily` for one session and `revokeAllForUser` for every session an account has anywhere, which section 14 requires after a password reset |
| `RefreshTokenService` | `startFamily`, `rotate`, `revokeSessionOf`, `revokeAllSessionsOf`. 32 random bytes, base64url; only the SHA-256 hex is stored |
| `RefreshCookies` | Builds the `garajul_meu_refresh` cookie: `HttpOnly`, `Secure`, `SameSite=Strict`, path `/api/v1/auth`, lifetime from `AuthProperties`. `clear()` returns the same cookie empty with a zero max-age, which is how a cookie is deleted |
| `AuthService` | `register`, `verifyEmail`, `resendVerificationCode`, `forgotPassword`, `resetPassword`, `login`, `refresh`, `logout`, and — although the URLs live under `/users/me` — **`requestEmailChange(accountId, newEmail, currentPassword)`** and **`confirmEmailChange(accountId, code)`**, because the single implementation of the code check is private here. Login returns `LoginResult`; the controller decides how the token travels. Two private helpers carry the shared machinery: `issueCode(user, type)` supersedes any outstanding code and returns a new one, with a three-argument overload that attaches a `targetValue` for the one type that needs it; and **`consumeCode(userId, type, code, now)` is the single place a six-digit code is checked** — spent, expired, too many attempts, wrong. Three flows need the identical sequence, and writing it three times is how three flows end up with quietly different rules. It **returns the spent token**, so email change can read the address off it. The type is always part of the lookup, so a code issued for one purpose cannot open another |
| `AuthController` | `POST /api/v1/auth/register` → 201; `/verify-email` and `/resend-verification` → 204; `/forgot-password` and `/reset-password` → 204; `/login` → 200 with the access token and the refresh cookie; `/refresh` → 200 on the channel the caller used; `/logout` → 204, idempotent |
| `dto.ForgotPasswordRequest` / `dto.ResetPasswordRequest` | The reset pair. The code is `@Pattern("\\d{6}")`, so malformed input is rejected before it costs an Argon2 comparison, and the new password carries the same 12–128 bound as registration — a length policy applies to new passwords, and this is one |
| `dto.VerifyEmailRequest` | `@Pattern("\\d{6}")` on the code, so malformed input is rejected before it costs an Argon2 comparison |
| `dto.RegisterRequest` | Bean Validation constraints; password 12–128 characters; language is a `@Pattern("ro\|en")` **string**, not the enum, because JSON carries the lower-case tag while Jackson would expect the constant name |
| `dto.LoginRequest` | `refreshTokenInBody` is a boxed `Boolean`, not a primitive — see the Jackson 3 decision below. `wantsRefreshTokenInBody()` is the single place that decides absent means false |
| `dto.RefreshRequest` / `dto.RefreshResponse` | The explicit-token channel used by native clients. `RefreshResponse.refreshToken` is null when the caller arrived by cookie |
| `AuthRateLimit` | Applies the policies in the controller, where the network address lives, and turns an exhausted budget into `RATE_LIMITED`. **Two keys per request**, address and email address, because either alone is avoidable: address-only punishes a whole NAT for one person, email-only lets an attacker rotate addresses and never spend a budget. The network address is checked **first**, and an exhausted one returns before the email key is ever created — that ordering is what bounds the limiter's memory. Reuses `AuthService.normalise`, now package-private, so that `A@B.com` and `a@b.com` cannot hold separate budgets |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.email`

`EmailProvider` (specification section 32) with `sendVerificationCode` and
`sendPasswordResetCode`, both `(recipient, code, language)`, and
`sendEmailChangeCode(recipient, newEmail, code, language)`. Separate methods
rather than one with a purpose argument: the templates say different things, and
a reader who receives the wrong wording learns something false about their
account. The email-change method takes **four** arguments because its recipient
and its subject are two different addresses — the code goes to the address on
file, and naming the requested one in the body is what lets somebody who did not
ask for the change recognise it and refuse.
`LoggingEmailProvider` writes the message to the log instead of sending it, gated
by `@ConditionalOnProperty(garajul-meu.email.provider=logging)` with **no**
`matchIfMissing`: if the property is absent there is no `EmailProvider` bean and
the application refuses to start, rather than silently writing codes to a
production log. `EmailProperties` declares `provider`, `apiKey`, `from` and `baseUrl`.

`EmailMessages` (2026-08-23) holds the wording of all three messages in both
languages section 6 requires. **Plain text, and that is a security decision
rather than a stylistic one**: the email-change message interpolates an address
chosen by whoever requested the change — which, in the case the message exists
to catch, is an attacker — and in HTML that value is an injection point inside
somebody else's mail client. The stated validity is read from `AuthProperties`
rather than typed into the sentence, because a template saying "15 minutes"
beside a setting saying thirty is a small lie told to every user that nothing
would ever catch.

`ResendEmailProvider` (2026-08-23) is section 32's seam filled in, gated by
`@ConditionalOnProperty(garajul-meu.email.provider=resend)`. It **builds its own
`RestClient`** because Spring Boot 4 on this classpath auto-configures no
`RestClient.Builder` — verified against the jars, not assumed — and takes one on
a second constructor so a test can bind `MockRestServiceServer` to it. It
**refuses to start** without `api-key` or `from`, treating an empty string as
missing, because the shipped configuration writes `${RESEND_API_KEY:}` and an
unset variable would otherwise surface hours later as a rejected request nobody
would trace back to a missing environment variable. **Nothing it logs contains a
code, an address or a body** — only the purpose and the message id Resend
returns, which is what somebody chasing "the email never arrived" needs and
which identifies no one. A send failure is deliberately **not** caught: it
rolls the registration back rather than leaving somebody with an account they
can never verify, and it reaches Sentry as the outage it is.

Only the Argon2 hash of the six-digit code is stored. Argon2 rather than a fast
hash because a six-digit code has only a million possibilities and would be
trivially reversible from a leaked database. The cost is roughly 50 ms and 16 MB
per verification, which makes rate limiting on the auth endpoints a requirement
rather than a nicety.

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.security`

`SecurityConfig` provides two beans:

- `PasswordEncoder` — `Argon2PasswordEncoder.defaultsForSpringSecurity_v5_8()`: 16 MB memory, two iterations, parallelism one, sixteen byte salt. Chosen over `Argon2Password4jPasswordEncoder` (new in Security 7) because the documentation recommends neither, and the built-in one avoids a further third-party library. **Requires BouncyCastle**, which Spring Boot's BOM does not manage, so `org.bouncycastle:bcprov-jdk18on` is pinned explicitly in `pom.xml`.
- `SecurityFilterChain` — stateless, no session; form login, HTTP Basic and Spring's logout endpoint disabled; `/actuator/health` public, everything else `authenticated()`; `ApiErrorAuthenticationEntryPoint` wired in **two** places, so a credential-less request answers 401 rather than the default 403, and every 401 carries a body.

`CorsProperties` (`garajul-meu.cors.allowed-origins`) holds the browser origins
allowed to call the API, per section 21. Exact origins only — the CORS
specification forbids pairing a wildcard with credentials, and every request
this API answers is credentialed. An empty list is valid and safe: no browser
origin is permitted and native clients, which CORS does not bind, still work.

`SecurityConfig` registers the matching `CorsConfigurationSource` on `/api/**`
and enables `.cors(...)` **first** in the chain, so a preflight — which by
definition carries no `Authorization` header — is answered rather than refused
with a 401 the browser would report as a CORS failure. It exposes
`X-Request-Id`, which is not decoration: a browser hides every cross-origin
response header from JavaScript bar a short standard set, so without that line
the correlation id never reaches the frontend and the request id on an error
screen would always be blank, wasting the whole mechanism.

`ApiErrorAuthenticationEntryPoint` answers an unauthenticated request with the
same `ApiErrorResponse` shape as everything else. It has to exist because
authentication fails inside the filter chain, before any controller, where
`@RestControllerAdvice` cannot reach. It serialises with the injected
`tools.jackson.databind.ObjectMapper` — the bean Spring MVC itself uses, not a
fresh instance — so the JSON matches every other error byte for byte, and it
reads the correlation id from the MDC exactly as `GlobalExceptionHandler` does.

JWT is handled entirely by Spring Security — no third-party library, no custom
filter:

| Bean / class | Role |
|---|---|
| `JwtProperties` | `garajul-meu.jwt`. `secret` has **no default** and is `@Validated @NotBlank @Size(min = 32)`, so a missing or too-short key stops startup rather than silently weakening the signature |
| `JwtEncoder` | `NimbusJwtEncoder` over an `ImmutableSecret`. One symmetric HS256 key signs and verifies, because this application both issues and consumes its own tokens |
| `JwtDecoder` | `NimbusJwtDecoder.withSecretKey(...)`. **Declaring it also removed Spring Boot's development user** — `UserDetailsServiceAutoConfiguration` lists `JwtDecoder` among the beans it backs off for, so `Using generated security password` no longer appears |
| `AccessTokenService` | Issues the token. Claims are exactly `iss`, `iat`, `exp`, `sub` — the account id and nothing personal, since a JWT is only base64 and lives on the client |
| `oauth2ResourceServer(jwt())` | Spring's own `BearerTokenAuthenticationFilter` reads `Authorization: Bearer`, verifies signature and expiry, and populates the security context |

Every path except `/actuator/health**` and `/api/v1/auth/**` answers 401 without a
valid bearer token. All six endpoints under `/api/v1/auth/**` are legitimately
pre-authentication, so the wholesale `permitAll` is correct **today** — see the
standing rule in the deferred-work table before adding anything else there.

#### Packages `ro.garajulmeu.vehicle` and `ro.garajulmeu.registrationcertificate`

| Class | Role |
|---|---|
| `Vehicle` | Deliberately thin, per section 9: who owns it, what its owner chose to call it, timestamps. No VIN, no registration number, no make. **The three image columns are mapped as of 12.3**, after four phases of sitting in the table unmapped — Hibernate's `validate` checks that mapped properties have columns and not the reverse. They hold **metadata and never bytes**, per section 22: a BLOB column would put megabytes of photograph into every row a query touches. The three move together or not at all, which is why `setImage` and `clearImage` replace three setters — a key with no content type is an image nothing can serve |
| `RegistrationCertificate` | The source of truth for what a vehicle is. Since 8.1 it maps **all thirty-two certificate fields** — four required, twenty-eight optional — plus `userId`, denormalised so the database can enforce one VIN per account. Its `toString` deliberately names nothing but the id: the default would print the owner's name and home address, which section 8 forbids logging, the first time anyone interpolated the entity into a message. **There is no ITP field and there must never be one** — section 8 makes X an ingestion source that becomes a `VehicleDocument`, and a copy here would be a second expiry competing with the first |
| `CertificateValues` | VIN and registration-number normalisation, shared rather than duplicated. Both paths that write a VIN — adding a vehicle and correcting its certificate — must agree exactly, and two copies of this logic would be the one thing that lets the same car into the same garage twice |
| `CertificateData` | All thirty-two fields in the order they are read on the document, with the four required ones constrained and `@Digits` on the two decimals so a number too large for its column is a 400 rather than a 500. Carries its own `of` and `applyTo`, so entity mapping lives in one place |
| `RegistrationCertificateService` | `of` and `correct`. **Correction re-checks VIN uniqueness**, because a `PATCH` can change the VIN and section 9 has to hold after the front door as well as at it — skipped when the VIN has not changed, or every correction of a colour would be refused as a duplicate of itself |
| `RegistrationCertificateController` | `GET` and `PATCH /api/v1/vehicles/{vehicleId}/registration-certificate`, the path and verbs fixed by the section 16 contract |
| `VehicleRepository` | Constructor-expression queries returning DTOs directly. **Every finder declared here takes the account id and matches it in SQL**, so ownership is a `where` clause rather than a check somebody has to remember to write. To be precise about the limit of that: `JpaRepository` still inherits an unscoped `findById`, so this is a convention the services follow, not something the type system forbids — narrowing to a bare `Repository` would enforce it, at the cost of losing `saveAndFlush` and diverging from `UserRepository`. Judged not worth it; recorded so the guarantee is not overstated. The join is explicit (`join RegistrationCertificate c on c.vehicleId = v.id`) rather than a mapped `@OneToOne`, which avoids both an association between two feature packages and a second query per row. `detailsOf` projects **`case when v.imageObjectKey is not null then true else false end`** — the portable way JPQL says "is there one" without selecting the key itself, since a bare predicate is not allowed in the select list of a constructor expression. Executed for the first time on 2026-08-18 rather than merely parsed: it had been written, compiled and shipped through a failed build before anything ran it |
| `RegistrationCertificateRepository` | `existsByUserIdAndVin`, which gives an ordinary duplicate a clean answer. It is **not** the guarantee — two simultaneous requests can both pass it — which is what the unique index is for |
| `VehicleService` | `garageOf`, `detailsOf`, `create`, `rename`, `delete`. A vehicle belonging to somebody else answers exactly as one that does not exist. `create` writes both rows in one transaction, and **flushes the vehicle before inserting the certificate**: the two are not linked by a mapped association, so Hibernate has nothing telling it which insert depends on the other and is free to order them by entity type. Also owns the normalisation — VIN uppercased with all whitespace removed, registration number uppercased with runs of spaces collapsed, make and description trimmed only |
| `VehicleController` | `GET` and `POST /api/v1/vehicles`; `GET`, `PATCH` and `DELETE /api/v1/vehicles/{vehicleId}`. The account always comes from the token and can never be asked for. `POST` answers 201 with the vehicle in the body and **no `Location` header**: a browser cannot read one cross-origin unless it is added to the CORS exposed headers, so it would be configuration carrying a value the body already has |
| `VehicleImageService` | 12.3. `replace`, `remove`, `read`, all scoped by account. **The two writes use opposite orderings and each is right for its case.** Replace writes the object, then the row, then deletes the old object: a failure leaves either an orphaned object or the previous photograph still working, and never a row pointing at nothing. Delete writes the row first, then the object: a failed object delete leaves the row saying "no photograph", which is what was asked for. **A replacement gets a new key rather than overwriting** — the client-visible address is stable, so overwriting would let a cache serve the previous photograph with nothing to invalidate it. The read deliberately **does not repair** a row pointing at a missing object: it logs and answers 404, because a GET that quietly writes is a surprise waiting for whoever debugs it later |
| `VehicleImageController` | 12.3. `GET`, `PUT` and `DELETE /api/v1/vehicles/{vehicleId}/image`. **The upload is a raw body, not multipart**, and that is forced rather than chosen: Tomcat parses `multipart/form-data` for POST only, so on a `PUT` `getParts()` returns nothing and `@RequestPart` cannot work. The cost is that multipart's free size limit disappears, so `readBounded` asks the stream for one byte past the limit and refuses if it gets it — `Content-Length` is not trusted, because a sender can lie and a chunked request declares nothing, the same reasoning that makes the validator read the format from the bytes. The GET sets `Cache-Control: no-store`: the key changes on every replacement, and the image is personal data |
| `VehicleSummary`, `VehicleDetails`, `VehicleImageContent`, `CreateVehicleRequest`, `UpdateVehicleRequest` | In `vehicle.dto`. `VehicleDetails` carries **`hasImage` rather than a URL**, and that is a decision with a date on it: section 16 allows either a signed URL or an application endpoint, the endpoint is derivable from the vehicle's own id, and it could not be used as an `<img src>` anyway because a browser sends no Authorization header on an image request. The object key is deliberately absent — nothing outside the backend has a use for it, and a key in a response is a key in a log somewhere later. `VehicleImageContent` is kept **separate from `storage.VehicleImage`** on purpose: that record's whole point is to exist only as the output of validation, and reusing it for reads would weaken the claim. The summary deliberately omits the VIN: a list does not need it, and section 24 argues for carrying identifying data only where it is used. `CreateVehicleRequest` carries the four fields section 8 names as the minimum to save a vehicle, and `@Size` bounds equal to the column widths so nothing can be truncated silently |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.push`

| Type | What it is |
|---|---|
| `UserDevice` | Section 10.7. **The only secret in this schema that must be readable again** — every other one is one-way because we only ever compare, while FCM needs the value itself to deliver. **Refuses to print itself**: a default `toString` reaching a log line would put a live token in it, which section 27 forbids by name. `fingerprint()` is the last six characters, which the word "full" in that prohibition is what permits |
| `DevicePlatform` | IOS and ANDROID, and **no WEB**: section 18 says V1 implements no web push and this table holds native registrations only. A third constant would invite storing something the delivery path cannot reach |
| `PushTokenCipher` | 2026-08-23. **AES-256-GCM**, twelve-byte random IV stored in front of the ciphertext, key derived by SHA-256 from a passphrase of at least thirty-two characters. Authenticated, so a row edited in the database fails to decrypt rather than decrypting to something plausible. The key is `garajul-meu.push.token-key`, **with no default** — the same shape as `jwt.secret`, because a missing key must be a refusal to start and never a silently weak one |
| `PushTokenConverter` | A JPA `AttributeConverter`, which is the whole design: every reader above it — repository, service, dispatcher — goes on seeing a usable token, and nothing has to remember to decrypt. A service call would have had to be remembered in each place |
| `common/Sha256Hex` | The **blind index**. A randomised IV means two encryptions of one token differ, so the column can no longer be searched and a unique index on it could never collide — which would let one handset register a thousand times. `push_token_hash` restores both, and `UserDevice` recomputes it in the constructor and in `setPushToken` so it cannot go stale. **`RefreshTokenService.sha256` is the same eight lines and could adopt this** — noted, not done |
| `UserDeviceService` | **Registration moves a token between accounts rather than refusing it** — see the decision below. Idempotent, because the native client calls it at every launch |
| `PushNotificationProvider` | The seam section 36 names, third of its kind after `EmailProvider` and `OcrProvider`. Takes the **token**, not the device, so an implementation needs no repository and whatever protects the token at rest can change without this interface noticing |
| `LoggingPushNotificationProvider` | Active for the whole of V1 web rather than only in development, since there is nothing to deliver to until phases 17 and 18. Selected by an explicit property, so production cannot fall back to it silently |
| `PushProperties` | Declares `garajul-meu.push` so the key is a known property. Nothing reads it — `EmailProperties` says why in its own javadoc — and **leaving it out was caught by an IDE warning rather than by any test** |
| `dto/DeviceView` | **No token, in any form.** The client that registered it already has it; sending it back would put a live credential in a response body and every cache between, for nothing |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.reminder`

| Type | What it is |
|---|---|
| `ReminderSchedule` | Section 12, pure and Spring-free. **Three of its rules are one comparison** — "generate only future timestamps", "skip a reminder falling today whose send time has passed", and "an expired document creates none" all reduce to *compute the instant, keep it if it is after now*. Written as three checks they would be three chances to disagree. The instant comes from the account's own zone, the **third** place this project takes that decision |
| `ReminderStatus` | PENDING / PROCESSING / SENT / FAILED / CANCELLED. **Cancelled, never deleted**: a row explaining why nothing arrived is worth more than an absence explaining nothing |
| `Reminder` | Section 10.6. Stores an **instant**, not a local time, which is what lets the scheduler ask one question of the whole table rather than one per account. `last_error_code` is a code and never a payload, per section 27 |
| `ReminderService` | `reconcile` cancels what a document had pending and schedules what it should have now — **cancel-then-regenerate rather than a difference**, because working out which of six offsets survived a date change is more code than discarding them. `cancelFor` is the renewal clause: the superseded record keeps its row and its dates but loses its warnings. `reconcileGarage` answers section 12's preference rule |
| `ReminderRepository` | **No account id, unlike every other repository here** — nothing reachable from the API reads this table yet. The view section 16 describes arrives in 11.5 and will be scoped through the document that owns the reminder. `findDue` is the scheduler's only question and answers it as a **projection across five tables in one round trip**: at nine in the morning the whole user base is due at once, and loading entities to navigate would be five queries per reminder |
| `DueReminder` | The projection: reminder, offset, account, vehicle, document, type, expiry, label and language. `vehicleLabel` is `coalesce(displayName, registrationNumber)` — **never the VIN**, which section 18 keeps off a lock screen |
| `ReminderMessage` | The words themselves, pure and Spring-free. **The one place the backend composes user-facing text** — section 17's ban is about API errors, where a frontend is running to translate a code, and a notification arrives when the application is not running at all. Romanian's own arithmetic is honoured: "în 3 zile" but **"în 30 de zile"**, written as the language's rule rather than as a special case for the one offset that needs it today |
| `NotificationDelivery` / `DeliveryStatus` | Section 10.8, one row per reminder × device. **One state short of `ReminderStatus`, and the missing one is PROCESSING**: a delivery is never claimed on its own, so giving it an in-flight state would be a second thing to strand after a crash. The row is written **before** the provider is called, which is what makes `UNIQUE(reminder_id, user_device_id)` mean anything on a retry |
| `ReminderDispatcher` | Three public methods, three transactions: release, claim, dispatch. **The orchestration deliberately lives elsewhere** — a `@Transactional` method called from inside its own class never reaches the proxy, and the three boundaries would collapse into none without a compiler saying so |
| `ReminderScheduler` | `runOnce` and a `@Scheduled` tick. **The bean exists even when scheduling is off**, because `@Scheduled` is inert without `@EnableScheduling` — conditioning the bean itself would have made the one method worth testing unreachable from a test |
| `ReminderSchedulingConfig` | `@EnableScheduling` behind `garajul-meu.reminder.scheduler-enabled`, and **off in tests**: with it on, every Spring test in the project would dispatch real reminders against its own container while it ran |
| `ReminderController` / `ReminderView` | `GET /api/v1/vehicles/{vehicleId}/documents/{documentId}/reminders`, section 16's one reminder endpoint. **Read-only, and deliberately nothing else**: a reminder is not something a person creates or deletes — it exists because a document has a date and an account has preferences, both changed elsewhere. Four fields of the entity's eleven; `attempt_count` and `last_error_code` explain a failure to whoever fixes it and mean nothing to the owner of the car |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.notification`

| Type | What it is |
|---|---|
| `NotificationPreferences` | Section 10.5. **The defaults exist twice on purpose** — as column defaults for a row the database inserts, and as field initialisers for `defaultsFor`, which builds preferences for an account that has no row. A row that does not exist cannot have a column default, so both are needed and a test asserts they agree |
| `NotificationPreferencesService` | **The row is created on first save, never on read.** Creating one when somebody merely opens a settings screen would make every read a write, storing exactly what the absence already meant. `preferencesOf` is package-private so 11.2's generation reads the same answer the endpoint reports |
| `NotificationPreferencesController` | `GET` and `PUT` under `/users/me/…`, so the only account either can describe is the token's |
| `dto/SaveNotificationPreferencesRequest` | **Boxed booleans with `@NotNull`, not primitives.** A primitive reads a field the client forgot as `false` and accepts it as an instruction to switch that reminder off — the one failure a preferences screen must not have |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.dashboard`

| Type | What it is |
|---|---|
| `DashboardController` | `GET /api/v1/dashboard`. Takes **no parameters at all** — not even a vehicle — because the only garage it can describe is the token's |
| `DashboardService` | Section 11's projection. **Three queries whatever the garage holds**: the vehicles, every document of every one of them, the account's timezone. Grouping by vehicle and by type happens over rows already in hand, because asking per vehicle per type would be four round trips per car on a screen whose whole job is to load at once |
| `dto/DashboardView` | An object rather than a bare array: a resource named `dashboard` that answered with a list of something else would have nowhere to put a second fact when one arrives |
| `dto/DashboardVehicle` | One vehicle, named as the garage names it, followed by its four lines in the order `DocumentType` declares them |
| `dto/DocumentStatusLine` | **All four types for every vehicle**, configured or not — which of them to show is the screen's decision, as the label is. The status answers one question, *are you covered today*; everything nullable exists to explain the answer rather than change it |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.vehicledocument`

| Type | What it is |
|---|---|
| `DocumentType` | RCA, CASCO, ITP, ROVINIETA. Stored as text, so a constant's name is a database value and renaming one is a migration rather than a refactoring |
| `DocumentStatus` | ACTIVE, EXPIRING_SOON, URGENT, EXPIRES_TODAY, EXPIRED, NOT_CONFIGURED. **None of them is stored.** Every one is a statement about a document *and a date*, so the same row changes status overnight without anything being written; section 11 says NOT_CONFIGURED in particular is presentation-only |
| `VehicleDocument` | One period of cover, or one inspection. A renewal is a new row rather than an edit — which is what makes the history in 10.5 possible at all — and editing exists only for correcting a record entered wrongly. Holds the vehicle by id rather than by association, as elsewhere |
| `VehicleDocumentRepository` | Every finder takes the account id and matches it in SQL. The documents table carries no owner of its own, so ownership is reached by **joining to the vehicle**; the certificate's denormalised owner serves a uniqueness constraint that has no counterpart here. `ofGarage` returns every document of every vehicle in one account **in one round trip**, which is what keeps the dashboard at three queries whatever the garage holds. **There is deliberately no "covers today" query** — there was one, briefly; see the 2026-08-17 decision |
| `DocumentCoverage` | Section 11 entire, pure and Spring-free: `daysRemaining`, `statusOn`, `todayFor(clock, zone)` — the one place the timezone decision is taken — and the **selection rule**: `coveringOn`, `upcomingAfter`, `lastToExpire`. Both comparator keys tolerate null, `valid_from` because the column does and `created_at` because an unpersisted entity has none and a comparator must not explode on one |
| `VehicleDocumentService` | Section 16's five operations. Loads the account for one column — the IANA timezone — because a status is a statement about a date and cannot be stored; **asks for the vehicle even when the answer is unused**, so documents of a vehicle the caller does not own answer 404 rather than an empty list, which would be indistinguishable from a vehicle that simply has none |
| `VehicleDocumentController` | `/api/v1/vehicles/{vehicleId}/documents`. Two identifiers in the path, both matched against the account **in SQL** rather than compared afterwards. 201 with the created document and no `Location` header, as vehicles decided |
| `VehicleHistoryController` | `/api/v1/vehicles/{vehicleId}/history`. A class of its own rather than a sixth method: section 16 puts history **beside** `/documents` rather than under it, and a class-level mapping can only say one of those |
| `dto/DocumentDetails` | One shape for the list and the detail, unlike vehicles: a document has no field the configure screen does not already show, so a second narrower record would be duplication. Carries `status` and `daysRemaining`, computed for the request |
| `dto/SaveDocumentRequest` | Used by both `POST` and `PATCH`, because **a correction replaces the record rather than patching it** — the certificate's decision, for the same reason. `type` is text rather than the enum so an unrecognised value can be refused by name |
| `dto/RenewDocumentRequest` | The next period, with **no type**: a renewal takes it from the record it supersedes, and that is what distinguishes renewing from adding |
| `dto/DocumentPeriod` | The interface both write requests implement, so section 12's rule about the pair of dates lives in **one** method. It applied to two shapes the moment renewal existed, and a rule stated twice is a rule that can drift |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.ocr`

| Class | Role |
|---|---|
| `OcrUsage` | One account's OCR requests on one day. Surrogate key with a unique index on (user_id, usage_date), so it looks like every other entity here while the index enforces what matters |
| `OcrUsageRepository` | `ensureRowFor` is an `insert … on conflict do nothing`, so two simultaneous first requests of the day cannot turn an ordinary call into a unique-index failure. The read is `PESSIMISTIC_WRITE`, so two requests cannot both see the same count and both write count + 1 — which is how an allowance of ten quietly becomes eleven |
| `CertificateCode` | The printed codes and the field each labels, with the kind of value to expect. Four fields have no code and are therefore never proposed: observations and the certificate number are printed without one, and the two C2=C1 boxes are ticks rather than text |
| `RomanianRegistrationCertificateMapper` | Named by section 13 and provider-independent as it requires — it sees only `OcrDocument`. **Anchors on the printed codes, not on the Phase 8 coordinates**, and proposes only values the field can actually hold |
| `OcrScan`, `FieldStatus` | What a scan proposes, one entry per coded field including the ones nothing was found for, in the three states section 7 puts on the overlay |
| `OcrProperties` | Ten a day, thirty a month, per section 13, plus the upload bounds, the active provider and a **provisional** confidence threshold that says so in its own javadoc. A monthly limit below the daily one is refused at startup rather than looking like a working system until somebody hits it |
| `OcrProvider` | The section 32 seam. One method, taking a checked image and returning a document that names no provider — which is what lets the mapper and the screen be built with no Google account |
| `OcrDocument` | Blocks of text with a confidence and a box, boxes in fractions of the page rather than pixels. Provider-neutral by construction: Document AI's own types stop at the adapter |
| `OcrImage` | An upload that has passed validation, carrying the content type **found in the bytes**. Constructible only by the validator, so no path reaches a provider unchecked |
| `OcrImageValidator` | Since 12.1, only the half that decides what a finding *means*: `ImageInspection` does the measuring and this **flattens all seven refusals into `OCR_FILE_INVALID`**, because a caller who sent the wrong thing gets the same advice either way. Logs the reason and never the image, per section 24 |
| `StubOcrProvider` | A canned reading, on the `LoggingEmailProvider` pattern. Shaped the way Document AI reports — **one block per word**, since 9.3b — so the mapper cannot be written against a convenience the real provider does not offer. Widths are derived from the number of characters and gaps from the distances measured on a real certificate, so the geometry is generated rather than typed. The plate deliberately begins with `B`, which is also a printed code: that is the ordinary case for a Bucharest certificate, and `OcrFlowTest` asserting `B 100 ABC` end to end is what proves the join survives it. One confidence is deliberately poor, so 9.4's review states have something to show. **The geometry has to be possible, not merely present**: codes are narrow, values start clear of them, and rows are spaced well beyond the mapper's line tolerance — see the decisions table for what the first version did |
| `OcrQuota` | Spends one request or refuses. **Counted in the database, not in memory** — see the decisions table for why this is not the rate limiter. Owns `allowanceDay(clock)`, the single definition of which day an allowance is spent from, called by the service and by its tests so the two cannot drift apart |
| `OcrService` | One scan, in the order the order matters: validate, then spend, then call the provider, then map. Deliberately **not** transactional, so the quota's pessimistic lock is released before the external call rather than held across it |
| `OcrController` | `POST /api/v1/ocr/registration-certificate`, multipart in and an `OcrScan` out. Not nested under a vehicle, and that is the contract rather than an oversight: a scan belongs to nothing until the person has reviewed it and saved it through the certificate's own `PATCH`. The part's declared filename and content type are ignored entirely |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.ocr.google`

Everything Document AI knows about, and nothing above it does. Provisioned
2026-08-17 in project `garajul-meu-505722`, processor `7f0ef1c4beff0c0f`, region
`eu` — identifiers rather than secrets, so they live in the configuration file;
the credentials come from the environment and never from the repository.

| Class | Role |
|---|---|
| `DocumentAiTranslation` | `Document` → `OcrDocument`. **Pure, static, and tested against a hand-built response** — no client, no network, no page charged, which is what let the whole risk of the adapter be settled before the account existed. Reads **tokens**, because a token is the smallest thing with a position and a confidence and anything coarser fuses a printed code to the value beside it. First page only: boxes are normalised per page and section 22 accepts nothing multi-page anyway. A token with no position is dropped rather than placed at the origin, where it would silently become the leftmost thing on the page |
| `DocumentAiProperties` | The three identifiers, checked **in the methods rather than the constructor** so a missing value fails the google path loudly and leaves the stub path alone. Lower-cases the region because the console displays `EU` and the API wants `eu` — a mismatch that answers `NOT_FOUND`, which reads as "no such processor" when it means "wrong building" |
| `GoogleDocumentAiOcrProvider` | The seam filled in. Client built once at startup, so a misconfigured deployment **fails to start** rather than failing every scan. Unavailable, timed out or throttled becomes `OCR_PROVIDER_UNAVAILABLE` at WARN; anything else becomes `OCR_PROCESSING_FAILED` at ERROR, because it is almost always our own configuration and Sentry should see it from Phase 15. Logs the status code and never the image or what was read from it, per section 24 |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.storage`

Phase 12.2. Object storage and the checks an upload passes before it reaches
any of it. Section 32 names the pair directly:
`FileStorageProvider → CloudflareR2FileStorageProvider`.

| Class | Role |
|---|---|
| `FileStorageProvider` | The seam. **Keys in, bytes out, and deliberately no URLs.** A signed URL is a Cloudflare concept a filesystem cannot produce, so putting `urlFor` here would make the development provider a lie — and a seam whose two implementations mean different things by the same method is worse than no seam. The cost is that every read passes through the backend; for a garage of a few vehicles that buys a bucket which stays private with no expiry machinery, and `urlFor` can arrive the day traffic argues for it. The content type is given on write, because R2 stores it on the object, and **not returned on read**: section 22 puts metadata in PostgreSQL, so `vehicles.image_content_type` is the authority and a second copy could only disagree with it. `get` answers `Optional.empty` rather than throwing — an object removed outside the application is a fact, not a failure. `delete` is idempotent |
| `LocalFileStorageProvider` | Development only, active on `provider: local`. Writes under `garajul-meu.storage.local-directory`, which is **gitignored: it holds real photographs of real cars** |
| `StorageProperties` | Five megabytes — a photograph from a phone rather than from a camera — and the same 200-pixel smallest edge OCR uses, because a picture of a car below that is a favicon whichever feature receives it. The ceiling is lower than OCR's because an upload here is **kept** rather than read once and discarded |
| `VehicleImage` | An upload that has passed validation, carrying the content type found **in the bytes**. Constructible only by the validator, so no path reaches the bucket unchecked — the same arrangement as `OcrImage`. No width or height: dimensions were measured only to refuse the file, and a stored number nothing reads is a column waiting to go stale |
| `VehicleImageValidator` | Delegates the facts to `ImageInspection` and decides only what they mean. **Separates `IMAGE_TOO_LARGE` from `IMAGE_INVALID_TYPE`**, where OCR flattens everything into one code — here one is worth retrying with a smaller file and the other is not |

#### Package `ro.garajulmeu.common`

| Class | Role |
|---|---|
| `ImageInspection` | Phase 12.1. Whether a pile of bytes is an image we accept, and what kind. **It answers facts and never decides what they mean** — it throws nothing and knows no error codes, because OCR wants every refusal flattened into one answer while a vehicle image wants them told apart, and putting the codes in here would have forced one of those to be wrong. Returns a sealed `Accepted`/`Refused` with **seven** named reasons; the reason is the only thing a caller cannot work out for itself. **The declared content type is ignored** — a declaration is an assertion, not evidence — so a PNG uploaded as `image/jpeg` is stored correctly labelled and an executable uploaded as `image/png` is not stored at all. Dimensions come **from the header before anything is decoded**: a few kilobytes of PNG can legitimately declare a canvas tens of thousands of pixels a side, and decoding one to find out how big it is turns the sender's file into our memory problem. **JPEG and PNG only, stated rather than inherited** from whatever the runtime happens to read — GIF and BMP are refused as policy, not by accident. Logs nothing at all, per section 24 |
| `RateLimiter` | The seam. One method, `tryConsume(key, policy)`, returning a boolean rather than throwing — which error code an exhausted budget produces belongs to the layer that knows what the caller was attempting |
| `InMemoryRateLimiter` | A fixed-window counter per key in a `ConcurrentHashMap`, using `compute` so two threads on one key cannot both write `count + 1`. The counter is capped one past the limit, and hammering never pushes the window forward — otherwise a refused caller could keep their own lockout alive by retrying. Expired entries are swept at most once a minute, because a map that keeps every key ever seen is itself a way to exhaust memory |
| `RateLimitProperties` | Policies grouped by what a request costs: `credentialCheck` 10/15m (login, verify-email — each pays an Argon2 comparison), `emailDispatch` 5/1h (register, resend — each sends mail), `tokenRefresh` 60/1h. A policy with a limit and no window is refused at startup rather than binding a null and failing mid-attack |
| `TimeConfig` | A `Clock` bean. `Instant.now()` cannot be moved forward in a test, so elapsed-time logic becomes either untestable or slow. Phase 11 will need this more than the limiter does |
| `PageResponse<T>` | **The shape every paginated endpoint answers with**, from 10.5 onwards. Deliberately not Spring Data's `Page`: serialising `PageImpl` produces a JSON contract Spring itself warns is unstable across versions, and a mobile client built against one shape cannot be updated as fast as a server. `page` is zero-based; `totalElements` is a `long`, because a count is |

`RequestIdFilter` (`@Order(HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE)`) gives each request a
correlation identifier, places it in the SLF4J MDC under `requestId`, returns it
in the `X-Request-Id` response header, and clears the MDC in a `finally` block
because servlet threads are pooled.

A caller-supplied `X-Request-Id` is honoured only when it is at most 64
characters of `[A-Za-z0-9._-]`; anything else is replaced with a fresh UUID.
An unvalidated header would be a log-injection vector — newlines let a caller
forge log entries and an unbounded value lets them flood log storage.

`logging.pattern.correlation` in `application.yml` fills the slot Spring Boot
reserves inside its default console pattern, so the identifier appears on every
line without replacing the standard format or losing colours. Lines produced
outside a request show `[system]`.

**Codes added beyond the section 17 examples**, for HTTP-level failures that
belong to no domain: `MALFORMED_REQUEST`, `RESOURCE_NOT_FOUND`,
`METHOD_NOT_ALLOWED`. Without them an unparseable request body would be
reported as `INTERNAL_ERROR`, blaming us for a client mistake. Section 17
explicitly allows extending the canonical catalogue.

### frontend — React 19.2.8, TypeScript ~6.0.2, Vite 8.2.0

| Item | State |
|---|---|
| Template | `react-ts` from create-vite 9.1.2 |
| Linter | ESLint 10.8.0 with `typescript-eslint`, `eslint-plugin-react-hooks`, `eslint-plugin-react-refresh` |
| `npm run dev` | serves on port 5173 |
| `npm run build` | `tsc -b && vite build`, output in `frontend/dist/` |
| `npm run test:run` | Vitest 4.1.10 over jsdom 30.0.1, with Testing Library 16.3.2 |
| Routing | react-router **8.3.0**, declarative API |
| i18n | i18next **26.3.6** with react-i18next **17.0.11** |

The Vite starter page is gone. Structure:

| Path | Role |
|---|---|
| `src/routes/paths.ts` | Every URL named once, so a route and the links pointing at it cannot drift apart by a typo |
| `src/routes/AppRoutes.tsx` | The route table, **deliberately separate from the Router**. `App.tsx` wraps it in a `BrowserRouter` that reads the real address bar; tests wrap the same table in a `MemoryRouter` and hand it a path, so every route is reachable without navigating through the UI |
| `src/layouts/RootLayout.tsx` | One `<header>`, one `<main>`, and it is a **layout route with no path**, so it wraps the not-found page too — which is exactly where somebody lost needs a way home |
| `src/pages/` | Screens 1–5 of section 5 plus not-found on the public side, and screens 6, 7, 8, 12 and 15 behind the gate. Welcome is the public landing and redirects anyone signed in to the dashboard; the garage lists real vehicles and offers the way to add one; the vehicle screen shows its certificate identity, renames and deletes; the dashboard still carries an empty state, because `GET /api/v1/dashboard` waits for Phase 10; the profile shows the account and ends the session |
| `src/components/TextField.tsx` | Label, input and message wired together — `aria-invalid`, `aria-describedby`, a generated id. **The accessibility wiring exists once** rather than six times, which matters because `eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y` cannot be installed to check it. Renders a `<textarea>` when asked, because a textarea needs exactly the same wiring |
| `src/components/CheckboxField.tsx` | The same idea for a checkbox, with the label after the box — the reading order for one, and how the certificate itself prints C2=C1. No message slot: the two booleans on a certificate have nothing to get wrong, and an unused affordance invites a use |
| `src/certificate/fields.ts` | **All thirty-two fields described once** — semantic name, the code printed on the document, kind, group, whether section 8 requires it, the backend's column width. Rendering, validation and conversion all read this instead of repeating themselves, and **Phase 8.3 attaches the template's normalised coordinates to these same entries** rather than keeping a second list that can drift |
| `src/certificate/coordinates.ts` | Where each field sits on the template, in fractions of its width and height. **Measured, not guessed** — see the decisions table for how. A list rather than a map, because "Numărul certificatului" is printed twice and stored once |
| `src/certificate/CertificateOverlay.tsx` | The template with editable fields laid over it, which is the UI model section 7 fixes. Positioning is inline because it is data. The image is `alt=""` — everything it says is also said by the labelled fields on top of it — and every field keeps a visually hidden label, since the printed code beside a box means nothing to a screen reader. Carries section 7's three scan states as **line styles plus words in each field's accessible description**, never as colour alone; a validation problem outranks the scan state on the same box |
| `src/certificate/scan.ts` | Lays a scan over **whatever set of fields a screen holds** — thirty-two on the certificate, four on Add Vehicle — so the rule lives once rather than twice. **A proposal with no value leaves what is there alone**: "not detected" means the photograph did not show it, never "empty the field". Values are taken exactly as they arrive, because the backend already proposes only what the field can hold. A name the screen did not list, including one a newer backend knows, is skipped rather than written. `ScanTally` is a **type alias and not an interface**, which is load-bearing: i18next's interpolation parameter needs an index signature, and TypeScript grants one to object type aliases but not to interfaces |
| `src/api/endpoints/documents.ts` | The document surface: paths for reads, functions for writes, as `vehicles.ts` explains. `DocumentStatus` carries all six states including `NOT_CONFIGURED`, which never arrives on a stored document — it is what the dashboard reports for a type nothing was entered for — because **one presentation function reads both shapes** |
| `src/documents/status.ts` | Turns a state into a sentence. **`tone` is for styling and is never the sole carrier of meaning** — a colour is invisible to a screen reader and ambiguous to a good part of everybody else, the same reason the scan outlines got three line styles rather than three colours. `DocumentStateKey` is a **union of the nine literal keys, not `string`**: i18next types `t` over the keys that exist, so a key assembled at runtime silently resolves to the overload whose second argument is a default string and stops compiling. The date formatter is **passed in rather than reached for**, so the function stays pure and testable without a hook |
| `src/components/SelectField.tsx` | A select with its label, its message and its `aria-invalid`/`aria-describedby` wired once — the bargain `TextField` makes, for the identical reason. Added in 10.6a rather than inlining a `<select>`, because inlining is what the wiring exists to prevent |
| `src/api/page.ts` | `PageResponse<T>`, mirroring the backend's. The shape every paginated endpoint answers with, and ours rather than Spring's for the reason the server gives |
| `src/api/endpoints/dashboard.ts` | The projection's types. `DocumentStatusLine`'s nullable fields **explain** the status rather than change it: no `documentId` means never configured, `upcomingFrom` without `validUntil` is a policy bought and not started, both together are a lapse with cover arranged |
| `src/pages/DashboardPage.tsx` | Screen 6 with the content section 27 asks of it. **All four types for every vehicle**, which for a new account is four "not configured" lines per car — the point rather than noise, since it is the only place the application says what it is for, and each vehicle carries a link to the screen that answers it |
| `src/pages/VehicleHistoryPage.tsx` | Screen 14. The filter and the page are folded into the path, so `useResource` reloads on either with no further wiring; changing the filter returns to page one, because page three of one filter is likely past the end of another and an empty screen would read as "no history". **Where you are and how much there is are two paragraphs**, not one — two interpolated sentences in a single element read as one run of text to anything matching on content |
| `src/documents/fields.ts` | The five inputs a period carries, their rules, and the conversion to a request body — shared by adding, correcting and renewing. An empty input becomes **null rather than `""`**, which on a correction is the difference between clearing a note and storing a blank one |
| `src/documents/DocumentFields.tsx` | Those five inputs as markup, written once because three forms need them. The same separation the backend makes with `DocumentPeriod`, and for the same reason: what differs between the three is the type |
| `src/pages/VehicleDocumentsPage.tsx` | Screen 11. Lists, adds and removes; correcting and renewing are screen 13, reached from each entry. Deletion confirms in place, not through `window.confirm`, which speaks the browser's language rather than the application's |
| `src/pages/VehicleDocumentDetailsPage.tsx` | Screen 13. **Two forms rather than one with a mode**, because correcting replaces this record and renewing creates another and leaves this one alone. **Keyed on the document id** — React Router keeps a component mounted when only a path parameter changes, so without it a renewal lands the person on the new record with the form that created it still filled in. Async results are held in **refs, not locals**: TypeScript cannot see an assignment made inside the async callback, so a local narrows to `never` after its null check, which fails loudly when a property is read and passes silently when one is not |
| `src/certificate/values.ts` | Form state is strings, because that is what an input holds; this converts both ways. Reads a decimal written with a comma, which is how it is written in Romanian — `Number('66,5')` is NaN, and without this the field would empty itself on save with nothing said |
| `src/components/FormError.tsx` | The form-level failure, in `role="alert"` so it is announced, with the request id when the code is one nobody can act on |
| `src/forms/rules.ts` | The rule vocabulary, each rule carrying the Bean Validation constraint name the backend would report for it |
| `src/forms/validate.ts` | `validate` before sending; `fieldMessagesFrom` turns the backend's field errors into **this form's own wording** |
| `src/forms/useSubmission.ts` | Pending state and error capture for one submission. Returns the failure rather than a boolean, because the caller needs it to place field errors |
| `src/routes/carriedEmail.ts` | Readers for route state: `carriedEmail` for an address handed between screens — never a query parameter, which would put it in history and in every proxy log — and `returnTo` for the address a protected route turned somebody away from. `returnTo` accepts only paths beginning with `/`, which is what stops it becoming an open redirect performed while holding a live session |
| `src/auth/RequireAuth.tsx` | The gate. `unknown` renders a visible `role="status"` wait rather than nothing or a redirect; `anonymous` redirects to sign-in with `replace` and records where it came from |
| `src/components/SiteHeader.tsx` | The header both layouts share, so it exists once |
| `src/layouts/AppLayout.tsx` | The authenticated frame: `SiteHeader`, then the primary navigation as a **sibling landmark of `<main>`** rather than inside it, then the content. `NavLink` rather than `Link` because it sets `aria-current="page"` by itself |
| `src/i18n/language.ts` | Pure functions: what was remembered, what the browser asks for, what wins. No detector plugin — see the decisions table |
| `src/i18n/locales/ro.ts` | The reference locale. `en.ts` is typed as `typeof ro`, so a forgotten key is a compile error rather than a string that renders in the wrong language |
| `src/i18n/i18next.d.ts` | Augments `CustomTypeOptions`, so a mistyped translation key stops the build instead of rendering itself to the reader |
| `src/i18n/errorKey.ts` | Backend error code → translation key, with an explicit unknown branch. The frontend half of section 17 |
| `src/components/LanguageSwitcher.tsx` | Label translated, option names **not** — each language named in itself |
| `src/api/tokenStore.ts` | The access token in a module variable, never in storage, plus a subscription so the session ending in the background is noticed rather than discovered on the next click |
| `src/api/refresh.ts` | **One refresh in flight, shared by every caller.** The module exists for that single variable |
| `src/api/client.ts` | `apiFetch`: bearer header, `credentials: 'include'`, one refresh and one retry on 401, and every failure turned into `ApiError`. JSON is the default for a body but **never for `FormData`**: a multipart request is only readable if its header carries the boundary the browser generated, so declaring a type there would leave the backend unable to find any part |
| `src/api/ApiError.ts` | `code`, `status`, `requestId`, `fieldErrors`. No message from the server, because the server sends none |
| `src/api/endpoints/` | `auth.ts` — `login`, `logout`; `users.ts` — `getProfile`; `vehicles.ts` — the vehicle types, `createVehicle`, `renameVehicle`, `deleteVehicle`, `vehicleLabel`, and the **paths** for reads; `certificate.ts` — the thirty-two-field payload, its path and `saveCertificate`; `ocr.ts` — `scanCertificate`, which posts one photograph as multipart and gets proposals back. Its `field` is typed as a plain string on purpose: the backend is a separate deployment and may be a version ahead, so narrowing it here would be an assertion the compiler cannot check, and it is checked against the field table instead. Reads are exposed as paths and writes as functions, which looks inconsistent and is not: `useResource` keys its effect on a string, and a fetcher would be a new value every render, while a write is called once from an event handler and has no such problem |
| `src/api/useResource.ts` | One GET, reported as loading / failed / answered. **Loading is derived from which request the stored answer belongs to, never set at the top of the effect** — doing that re-renders before the request is even sent, which `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect` rejects. A rejection that is not an `ApiError` becomes `UNKNOWN`, because `fetch` throws a `TypeError` when the connection drops and a screen reading `error.code` would otherwise show nothing |
| `src/auth/` | `AuthContext.ts`, `AuthProvider.tsx`, `useAuth.ts`. Three-state status, silent restore on load, and the account's language adopted on sign-in |

No design system, no CSS beyond the untouched `index.css`. Nothing in section 30
places one in Phase 5, and inventing tokens before the first real screen exists
would be guessing at what they have to support.

## Tests currently passing

| Test | Scope |
|---|---|
| `BackendApplicationTests.contextLoads` | Spring context boots against a throwaway PostgreSQL container |
| `BackendApplicationTests.usesThrowawayContainerAndNeverTheHostedDatabase` | Asserts the live JDBC URL contains `localhost` and not `neon.tech` |
| `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest.businessFailureAnswersWithItsOwnCodeAndStatus` | `ApiException(VEHICLE_NOT_FOUND)` produces 404 with that code |
| `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest.unexpectedFailureNeverLeaksInternalDetail` | An exception whose message contains an internal host and a database user yields only `INTERNAL_ERROR`; neither string nor the exception class name appears in the body |
| `RequestIdFilterTest.generatesAnIdentifierWhenTheClientSendsNone` | MDC is populated during the chain and the value matches the response header |
| `RequestIdFilterTest.reusesAnIdentifierTheClientSuppliedSoOneRequestCanBeTracedEndToEnd` | A safe caller-supplied identifier is preserved |
| `RequestIdFilterTest.replacesAnUnsafeClientIdentifier...` | A header containing a newline is discarded rather than written to the log |
| `RequestIdFilterTest.clearsTheMdcOnceTheRequestIsFinished` | No identifier leaks to the next request served by the same pooled thread |
| `UserRepositoryTest` (5 tests) | Migration defaults are applied, a new account is unverified, lookup by email works, a duplicate email is rejected by `ux_users_email`, and the language round-trips as its lower-case code |
| `PasswordEncoderTest` (3 tests) | The configured encoder produces `$argon2id$` output, never the plain password; the same password hashes differently each time thanks to a per-password salt; only the exact original password matches. Tests the bean `SecurityConfig` actually provides, so hashing cannot be weakened unnoticed |
| `RefreshTokenServiceTest` (9 tests) | Only the digest is stored; rotation stays in the family and links the chain; replaying a spent token revokes the whole family and takes the honest holder's current token with it; unknown and expired tokens are refused; logout ends every token in the family; two logins get independent families, so signing out on the phone leaves the laptop alone. **Two added 2026-08-31 with the reuse grace window**: a replay whose replacement was never collected is forgiven and re-issued in the same family, and a replay is refused the moment the replacement *has* been used — the condition that keeps the alarm working. **The theft test now stages a third rotation**, because replaying the instant after a rotation is the forgiven case: stopping at two would have asserted the window while claiming to assert the alarm |
| `RefreshTokenGraceWindowTest` (1 test) | That the window is a window. Its own Spring context with `refresh-reuse-grace=0s`, because the size of the window is the whole safety margin and the class above cannot vary it. The alternative was a setter on `revokedAt` existing for no other reason than to let a test reach past the entity's vocabulary |
| `PasswordResetServiceTest` (9 tests) | A request for an unknown address sends nothing; the emailed code replaces the password; a wrong code is counted and leaves the password alone; an expired code answers its own error; a spent code cannot be used twice; asking again invalidates the earlier code; **resetting ends every session on every device**; resetting verifies an address that was never verified, and leaves an already verified one verified |
| `AuthFlowTest` (20 tests) | Full HTTP surface. Session: a token from login opens `/users/me`; no token and a forged token both answer 401 with `AUTHENTICATION_REQUIRED`, and the unauthenticated error's `requestId` matches the `X-Request-Id` header; the profile body contains neither `argon2` nor `passwordHash`; a wrong password and an unknown address answer the identical `INVALID_CREDENTIALS`; an unverified account answers `EMAIL_NOT_VERIFIED`.
