Transport: login always sets the `HttpOnly` `Secure` cookie and omits the token from the body; a caller asking for it in the body receives it; refresh on the cookie channel answers on the cookie channel and on the body channel answers in the body; a replayed token answers `REFRESH_TOKEN_REUSED`; refresh with no token at all answers `REFRESH_TOKEN_INVALID`; logout clears the cookie and kills the session; logout with no token is not an error. Password reset: `forgot-password` answers identically for a known and an unknown address; the whole flow ends with the new password working and the old one refused; a malformed code is rejected as `VALIDATION_ERROR` on the `code` field before it costs a hash; and resetting ends a session that was already open |
| `InMemoryRateLimiterTest` (5 tests) | No Spring and no sleeping — the clock is moved by hand, so a fifteen-minute window is verified in microseconds. Exactly the configured number of attempts is allowed; the window reopens once it has elapsed; hammering does not extend the lockout; two keys do not share a budget; expired entries are swept so the map does not grow forever |
| `AuthRateLimitTest` (4 tests) | The wiring, at two attempts per policy so the boundary arrives in three requests. One address cannot keep guessing by changing the email; another address is unaffected by a noisy one; one account cannot be attacked from many addresses — **the test that justifies the second key**; and spending the login budget leaves the resend budget intact |
| `AccessTokenServiceTest` (4 tests) | Our own decoder accepts the issued token and reads the account id back; expiry lands inside the configured window; the token carries **only** `iss`, `iat`, `exp`, `sub`; a token signed with a different key is rejected. Builds the beans directly rather than starting Spring, so it runs in about a tenth of a second while still exercising the real configuration |
| `VerificationTokenRepositoryTest` (5 tests) | A fresh code is usable; an expired one is not; a spent one cannot be reused; a resend supersedes every outstanding code; codes of another purpose are untouched |
| `VerificationCodeGeneratorTest` (2 tests) | Always exactly six digits over a thousand draws, which also proves the zero padding; two hundred draws are almost all distinct |
| `AuthServiceTest` (10 tests) | Registration: address normalised, password `$argon2id$`, emailed code matches the stored hash, duplicate address rejected in any case, language defaults to Romanian. Verification: a correct code verifies the account, a wrong one is counted, a spent code cannot be reused, an expired one answers `VERIFICATION_CODE_EXPIRED`, a resend marks the earlier token invalidated and the old code stops working, and a resend for an unknown address sends nothing |
| `UserProfileFlowTest` (8 tests) | The two account-management endpoints over HTTP. PATCH leaves every field the body does not mention and trims the name it is given; it accepts a language and a real timezone; it rejects a whitespace-only name as `VALIDATION_ERROR` on `fullName`, and `Europe/Bucuresti` — plausible, but not a zone — as `VALIDATION_ERROR`. **It changes only the account the token belongs to**: a second account exists, the first account's token renames itself, and the second row is read back untouched. That is the ownership property in the only shape this endpoint can have, since there is no path parameter to tamper with. Change-password refuses a wrong `currentPassword` with `INVALID_CURRENT_PASSWORD` and the old password still logs in afterwards; a correct one answers 204, **kills a refresh token issued before the change**, and the new password logs in. Both endpoints, and `GET /me`, answer 401 `AUTHENTICATION_REQUIRED` with no token |
| `EmailChangeFlowTest` (9 tests) | The email change end to end. **The code is sent to the old address and names the new one**, and never to the new address — the property the whole design rests on, asserted positively and negatively. Nothing changes until the code is confirmed. Confirming moves the address and leaves it **unverified**, which is the honest consequence of a code that proved control of the *old* inbox only. The full round trip then works: confirm, receive a fresh verification code at the new address, verify, log in with it. **The session survives the change**, verified by refreshing a token issued beforehand — that is the only route back from a mistyped address, so it is asserted rather than assumed. A wrong `currentPassword` answers `INVALID_CURRENT_PASSWORD` and sends no email at all; an address somebody else already holds answers `EMAIL_ALREADY_EXISTS`; a code of another type cannot confirm a change; both endpoints answer 401 without a token |
| `AccountDeletionTest` (9 tests) | Deletion, and what it must not leave behind. The account row goes; **every verification code and refresh token goes with it**, counted in the database rather than assumed, because neither child is mapped as an association and the foreign key is the only thing that removes them. **The address is free for a new registration afterwards** — the test that would catch the day somebody turns this into a soft delete or an anonymised tombstone, since either would keep `ux_users_email` occupied. A wrong password deletes nothing; the refresh token stops working at once; **the access token outlives the account and opens nothing**, answering `USER_NOT_FOUND`, which is the honest consequence of a stateless JWT that cannot be recalled; the response clears the refresh cookie; deleting one account leaves another intact; and a caller with no token is refused |
| `CorsTest` (8 tests) | **Four more arrived on 2026-08-23 with 16.2**, and they are what closed the CSRF entry: a forged refresh from a *sibling subdomain* is refused before it reaches the handler — `www.cyber-half.com` is same-site with `api.cyber-half.com`, so `SameSite=Strict` attaches the cookie and **only the origin check distinguishes them**; and, derived from the route table, **nothing reads the refresh cookie on a method browsers send without an `Origin` header**, because a GET endpoint reading that cookie would be forgeable by an `<img>` tag with neither CORS nor SameSite in the way. **The fourth is 2026-08-22's, and it is the one that would have prevented the whole incident**: every HTTP method the controllers serve under `/api/` must survive a preflight, with the method list **read from Spring's route table** rather than written out. A test naming the methods would have needed the same remembering that failed; this one fails on the day an endpoint with an unlisted method is added. The handler mapping is qualified by bean name, because Actuator contributes a second `RequestMappingHandlerMapping` and the wrong one would have checked the management endpoints while claiming to check ours. Then: a preflight from the application origin is allowed to carry credentials; one from anywhere else is refused; and **an unauthenticated API call still carries the CORS headers and exposes `X-Request-Id`** — asserted on a response that fails, deliberately, because without those headers on error responses a browser hides the body and the frontend could not read the code or the request id from precisely the answers that need explaining |

| `VehicleReadTest` (6 tests) | The two vehicle reads over HTTP. The garage lists only the caller's vehicles and is empty for an account with none; details carry the identity held on the **certificate**, not on the vehicle row. **Somebody else's vehicle and an identifier nobody owns produce the identical 404 `VEHICLE_NOT_FOUND`** — asserted together in one test, because the property being protected is that the two are indistinguishable, and two separate tests would each pass while the pair diverged. An identifier that is not a UUID answers `VALIDATION_ERROR` rather than 500. Both endpoints refuse a caller with no token. Tokens are issued directly through `AccessTokenService` rather than by logging in: the token is real and the security filter verifies it exactly as it would any other, so nothing is bypassed — it only avoids paying for an Argon2 comparison in every test of a feature that has nothing to do with passwords |

| `VehicleWriteTest` (10 tests) | Creation, renaming and deletion over HTTP. A created vehicle comes back with the identity held on its certificate and appears in the garage. **The same VIN twice in one account is refused; the same VIN in two accounts is allowed** — the two halves of the section 9 rule, and the second is why the index is on `(user_id, vin)` rather than on `vin`. **Normalisation is asserted through the constraint it protects**: a VIN typed with a space and in lower case is stored uppercased and unspaced, and the tidy version then collides with it. That assertion is made *before* the duplicate attempt on purpose — a constraint violation aborts the transaction, so anything read after it would fail for a reason unrelated to the test. A missing mandatory field answers `VALIDATION_ERROR` naming `vin`. A nickname can be set, cleared with an empty string, and left alone by a body that omits it. **Deleting a vehicle removes its certificate, counted in the database** rather than assumed, because nothing in Java removes it — only the foreign key does, and a certificate outliving its vehicle would keep the VIN occupied and refuse the owner's next attempt to add the same car. Deleting and renaming somebody else's vehicle both answer 404 **and are then verified to have changed nothing**. All three writes refuse a caller with no token |

| `RegistrationCertificateRepositoryTest` (4 tests) | **Every one of the thirty-two fields is written, the persistence context cleared, and all of them read back from the database.** Long on purpose: a field that was never mapped raises nothing anywhere, because `ddl-auto: validate` checks that mapped properties have columns and not the reverse — so an omission is silent in the build, silent in the tests, and visible only as a value that will not save. Twenty-eight fields arrived in one edit and this is what proves they all landed. Also: a certificate with only the required four leaves the rest null; a certificate is found only through its owner; and **the entity refuses to print itself**, which is the section 8 logging ban made structural |
| `RegistrationCertificateFlowTest` (10 tests) | The two endpoints over HTTP. The whole certificate comes back; a correction is stored and read back. **A body that omits an optional field clears it** — the replacement semantics asserted rather than described, so the day it becomes a real merge this test says so. A blank required field is refused by name. A number too large for its `DECIMAL(8,2)` column is a `VALIDATION_ERROR`, not a 500. **Correcting a VIN into one already in the garage is refused, and keeping the same VIN is not a duplicate of itself** — the two halves of the rule that only exists because `PATCH` can change a VIN. Somebody else's certificate is not found. And **the owner's address is stored but never logged**, asserted by attaching a `ListAppender` to the root logger for the length of the request, because a ban nobody checks lasts until the next helpful debug line |

| `RomanianRegistrationCertificateMapperTest` (16 tests) | No Spring; the mapper takes its threshold from a record. **Seven of them are 9.3b's**: a value printed as several words becomes one value; words printed touching are joined without a space between them, so a plate stays `CT-92` rather than `CT - 92`; the join stops at the gap that separates two cells; a joined value is only as certain as its worst word; a stray letter with empty page beside it does not take a field, even when it is higher on the page than the printed code; **a plate that begins with a code letter is still read whole, and no date is invented out of the rest of it**; and the next cell's code ends a value even when it is printed close. A code and the value beside it become a proposal; **every coded field is answered for exactly once**, so the screen never has to guess which state it is in; a code with nothing beside it, and a value on another line, are both not detected. An uncertain reading is proposed but flagged. **A confident reading that cannot be true still needs review** — a VIN ending in I is a perfectly confident reading of something excluded from the alphabet precisely because it looks like a 1, which is why section 13 asks for confidence *and* semantics. A Romanian date arrives as ISO; one nobody can parse proposes nothing; a number read with its unit is still a number |
| `ImageInspectionTest` (8 tests) | 12.1, and where the measuring is now tested. No Spring; limits come from a record, so every case runs in microseconds. A photograph is accepted and measured; **the format comes from the bytes and not from any declaration**; something that is not an image is told apart from **a real image in a format we do not keep** — two different refusals that a boolean would have merged; an empty upload and one over the size limit are refused on their own reasons; one below the smallest useful edge is refused. And **a valid PNG header declaring a canvas of twenty thousand pixels a side is refused before decoding** — the difference between rejecting a few bytes and allocating 1.6 gigabytes to discover we should have |
| `OcrImageValidatorTest` (3 tests) | What is left after 12.1 moved the measuring out: that the validator carries the limits from its **own** properties rather than a constant, and that **four genuinely different findings are flattened into `OCR_FILE_INVALID`** — rubbish, an empty part, an unkept format and a thumbnail. A flattening nobody asserts is indistinguishable from a flattening nobody noticed |
| `VehicleImageValidatorTest` (4 tests) | The other half of the same split: an accepted photograph keeps the type **measured from its bytes**, and **every one of the seven refusal reasons maps onto one of the two codes section 17 provides** — asserted over the whole enum, so a reason added later cannot quietly fall through. Too big and not-a-photograph are **different answers**, which is the entire reason this validator exists separately from OCR's |
| `LocalFileStorageProviderTest` (5 tests) | What is written comes back **byte for byte**; a key containing slashes creates the directories it needs; a key never written is **empty rather than a failure**; deleting removes it and deleting again is not an error. And **a key that climbs out of the storage root is refused** — the one test here that is about safety rather than behaviour, since a key is not user input today but the provider cannot know that |
| `VehicleImageFlowTest` (12 tests) | 12.3 and 12.3b, over HTTP. An uploaded photograph comes back with **the type read from its bytes**, and a declared type is ignored entirely. `hasImage` flips true and false on the vehicle's own endpoint, and **the object key never appears in the body**. Replacing writes a **new** key and removes the old object; deleting removes the object and **deleting again still succeeds**; a vehicle with no photograph answers 404 rather than an empty body. **Another account is refused on all three verbs.** Rubbish is `IMAGE_INVALID_TYPE`; a 70 KB body against the 64 KB test limit is `IMAGE_TOO_LARGE`, which is how the raw-body bound is exercised without sending five megabytes; no token is 401. Annotations copied **verbatim** from `AuthFlowTest`, unused `EmailProvider` mock included, and the real `FileStorageProvider` autowired rather than mocked — a `@MockitoBean` would change the context key and start a fifth container. It ran in **1.03 s**, which is what context reuse looks like. **Two are 12.3b's**: deleting the vehicle takes its photograph, and deleting the account takes every photograph in its garage — both asserted on the store, because the database looks identical either way. The second failed first for a reason worth keeping: **rows removed by a database cascade are invisible to the persistence context**, so `findById` answered from the first-level cache and reported a vehicle that was already gone; the test now clears the `EntityManager` before reading. Uses `isContentTooLarge`, not the `isPayloadTooLarge` deprecated in Spring 7.0 — RFC 9110 renamed 413, and `ErrorCode` had already been declaring `CONTENT_TOO_LARGE` all along |
| `ApplicationConfigurationTest` (1 test) | Reads the shipped `application.yml` **as text** and asserts every key at column zero is a root Spring or this application recognises. No Spring context on purpose — loading one would read the test configuration, which shadows the very file this is about. Exists because the whole `ocr:` block spent two days one indent too far left and nothing noticed: the properties record substitutes a default for every zero, so a block that never bound looked exactly like one that did |
| `VehicleDocumentFlowTest` (27 tests) | **Five are 11.2's**: adding a document schedules the six offsets as PENDING; one expiring in two days schedules only the two still ahead, not six at once; correcting the expiry cancels the old set and generates a new one; **renewing cancels the superseded record's reminders while its own dates stay untouched**, which is 10.3 and section 12 asserted together; deleting the document takes its reminders by foreign key. |
| `VehicleDocumentFlowTest` (first 22 tests) | **Six are 10.5's**: the history keeps superseded records newest-entry-first, which is 10.3's assertion read from the other end — what renewal declines to touch is exactly what history shows; it narrows to one type; an unrecognised filter is `DOCUMENT_TYPE_INVALID` rather than silently everything; **an oversized page is clamped to 100 and a negative page to 0** rather than refused; the second page carries the rest and the totals agree; another account's history is not found. **Six are 10.3's**: renewing creates a new row and the superseded one is read back **unchanged, both dates**; the type carries over and nothing else does, asserted against an original that had a provider, a policy number and a note; an overlapping renewal is accepted, because section 11 resolves overlaps rather than forbidding them; the date rule fires through the renewal shape too, which is what the shared `DocumentPeriod` exists to guarantee; renewing another account's document is not found **and creates nothing**. And the one that finally ran `coveringOn` — three records covering the same day, expected back as renewal, older, undated: **`desc nulls last` translated as intended, the first time it was executed rather than merely parsed**. |
| `VehicleDocumentFlowTest` (first 10 tests) | Annotations identical to `AuthFlowTest`, including the unused `EmailProvider` mock — **0.68 s and no fifth container**, measured. A document is added and comes back in the list; **the status and the day count are computed rather than stored**, so one five days out is URGENT without anything saying so; a period ending before it starts is refused by name and writes nothing; an unrecognised type is `DOCUMENT_TYPE_INVALID` rather than `MALFORMED_REQUEST`, which is the whole reason the request carries text; any case is the same type. **A correction clears a note the second body does not mention** — the only way screen 13 can delete one. Another account's vehicle and another vehicle's document both answer as things that do not exist, and the row survives; **the pair of identifiers is matched together**, so the caller's own document under the wrong vehicle is still not found. Deleting the vehicle takes its documents by foreign key. All five endpoints refuse a caller with no token |
| `PushTokenCipherTest` (5 tests) | No Spring. A token survives the round trip; two encryptions of the same token differ, which is the property the blind index exists to work around; ciphertext altered by one character fails to decrypt rather than decrypting to rubbish, which is what "authenticated" buys; a passphrase under thirty-two characters is refused at construction |
| `EmailMessagesTest` (5 tests) | No Spring — the templates are a pure function of a code, a language and one duration. **Derived over `Language.values()` rather than written out**, so adding a third language fails here on the day it is added instead of silently sending Romanian: every purpose in every language has a non-empty subject and body, carries the code it was given, and **differs between languages** — that last one catches the quiet failure where a branch was copied and never translated, which produces a perfectly valid message in the wrong language. The email-change body names the requested address. The stated validity follows `AuthProperties` rather than a typed number, asserted by building a second instance at thirty minutes |
| `ResendEmailProviderTest` (4 tests) | `MockRestServiceServer` bound to the builder, so the assertions are about the exact HTTP request Resend would have received — no Spring context and no network. The documented shape: POST `/emails`, bearer key, JSON, `from`, `to[0]`, subject and text. **The email change goes to the address on file and names the requested one in the body.** A 500 from Resend reaches the caller rather than being swallowed, because that exception is what rolls the registration back. And the provider **refuses to construct** with an empty key or a null sender, naming the property in the message |
| `UserDeviceFlowTest` (10 tests) | Annotations identical to `AuthFlowTest` — 0.63 s, no fifth container. **The tenth, from 2026-08-23, is the one that proves the encryption is applied rather than merely working**: a native query reads `push_token` straight out of the table and asserts it is neither equal to nor contains the token, then reads the same row back through the repository and asserts the application still sees the real value — which is section 10.7's requirement in one test, since FCM needs the value itself. Registration stores the device and **the token appears nowhere in the response**, asserted against the whole body rather than one field name, so a refactoring that renamed it could not slip past; the same token twice keeps one device; **a token registered by another account moves to the new one** rather than being refused; unregistering removes it and another account's device is not found and survives; a platform outside the two is refused by the deserialiser, which is the argument for taking the enum here; **the entity refuses to print its token**; deleting the account takes its registrations |
| `ReminderViewFlowTest` (7 tests) | Annotations identical to `AuthFlowTest` — 0.479 s, no fifth container. The schedule comes back **in firing order**, not the order it was written. Cancelled rows are **kept in the table and left out of the view**, asserted on both sides so a filter that had become a deletion would fail. A sent reminder carries its instant; a document with none answers an empty list rather than a 404. Then the two the ownership check exists for: **another account's document is NOT_FOUND rather than empty** — without it, "not yours", "no reminders" and "wrong UUID" would all answer the same thing — and a document asked for under the wrong vehicle is refused too, because the path's vehicle is matched and not merely carried |
| `ReminderMessageTest` (6 tests) | **20 milliseconds, no Spring.** The rule that has nothing to do with software: Romanian needs "de" above nineteen, asserted next to an offset that does not so the general rule is what is being tested. "Azi" and "mâine" are words rather than numbers in both languages; only ROVINIETA translates; the date is day-first in both and month-named in English so `03/12` cannot be misread. And the one that guards a lock screen: **the payload's key set is asserted whole**, so a fourth key has to be argued for here before it can be added |
| `ReminderDispatcherFlowTest` (9 tests) | Annotations identical to `AuthFlowTest` — **0.775 s, no fifth container**. The dispatcher is **built by hand rather than injected**: a `@MockitoBean PushNotificationProvider` would have changed the context configuration and started that fifth container, and building it here puts the retry limit in the file that asserts about the third attempt. What it costs is stated in the class: a hand-built instance has no proxy, so these verify the state machine and not the commit boundaries. **An account with no device finishes SENT with zero delivery rows** — every reminder in V1 web. Every enabled device gets its own row; a silenced device is skipped; a refusal leaves PENDING with `scheduled_at` untouched; three failures end FAILED. Then the two that pay for the design: **a retry reaches only the device that failed** — one phone attempted once, the broken one three times, ending SENT because somebody was told — and **a reminder abandoned in PROCESSING is invisible until released**, which is section 12's "a short restart loses nothing" made real |
| `ReminderScheduleTest` (7 tests) | **18 milliseconds, no Spring and no database** — section 12's rules are arithmetic on dates and a zone. The six offsets in firing order; a switch turned off removes only its own; **nine in the morning is nine where the person is**, asserted against both Bucharest and UTC so a server-zone mistake could not pass. Then the three rules that are one comparison, checked from each side: offsets already past are dropped rather than fired at once, today-after-the-send-time and an already-expired document both schedule nothing, and today one minute *before* the hour still schedules. The seventh arrived with 11.4a: **the account-level switch turned off schedules nothing at all**, which was true of nothing until then |
| `NotificationPreferencesFlowTest` (9 tests) | Annotations identical to `AuthFlowTest`. **An account that has never saved anything reads the defaults and stays unwritten** — asserted on the repository, not only on the response. Saving creates the row and reads back; saving twice keeps one row; **a body missing one switch is refused rather than read as off**, which is what the boxed booleans buy; one account cannot see or change another's; deleting the account takes its preferences by foreign key. And the one guarding a duplication that had to exist: **the migration's column defaults and the entity's field initialisers agree**, checked by inserting a bare row and comparing it against `defaultsFor` |
| `DashboardFlowTest` (7 tests) | Annotations identical to `AuthFlowTest` — **0.55 s, no fifth container**. Every vehicle carries all four types whether configured or not; a type nothing was ever entered for is NOT_CONFIGURED with every other field absent; **a renewal decides the status without anything having been marked**, asserted by identifier and not merely by date. Then section 11's two hard cases: a lapse with cover already bought **reads as the gap and names when it resumes**, and a policy that has not started is not reported as active. The dashboard describes only the token's garage |
| `DocumentCoverageTest` (6 tests) | No Spring, no database: section 11's rules are arithmetic on two dates, so a container would buy nothing. **Every boundary in the status table and only the boundaries** — 31, 30, 8, 7, 1, 0, −1 — because a status asserted in the middle of a band proves nothing an off-by-one would break. `valid_until` is inclusive, asserted from both sides: not expired on the day it expires, expired the day after. And **the day belongs to the reader**: at one fixed instant it is already tomorrow in Bucharest and still today in London, and the same row reads EXPIRED to one and EXPIRES_TODAY to the other. That last one exists because the identical rule, left implicit in `OcrQuota`, disagreed with itself between midnight and three in the morning |
| `OcrQuotaTest` (7 tests) | The first request is counted; the daily allowance runs out; **being refused costs nothing**, so tomorrow is not shorter for having been told no today; a new day brings a fresh allowance; the monthly total can run out on an untouched day, which is the whole reason it is not the daily figure multiplied; **deleting the account takes its usage with it**, per section 24; two accounts do not share. **The clock is never moved** — every case is set up by writing rows for the days it needs, because a mutable `Clock` bean would change the context cache key and start a fifth container for the sake of a date. **Every date comes from `OcrQuota.allowanceDay(clock)`, the same call the service makes**, never from a bare `LocalDate.now()`: see the 2026-08-17 decision on which day an allowance belongs to |
| `OcrFlowTest` (8 tests) | The endpoint over HTTP, against the stub provider. A photograph comes back as proposals, one per coded field; a reading that cannot be true comes back for review with **no value at all**; **nothing is saved**, counted in the database; a successful scan spends exactly one request. **A broken upload costs no allowance** — the ordering decision asserted rather than described, and the test that speaks up the day validation and metering are swapped. The exhausted allowance answers `OCR_RATE_LIMITED` through the endpoint and not merely through the quota; a request with no file answers 400 rather than 500; a caller with no token is refused. **This class earned its place twice over on the run that introduced it**: it found that the stub had never been executed by the mapper and was describing a physically impossible certificate, and that a missing multipart part was answering `INTERNAL_ERROR`. Neither is reachable from a unit test — the mapper's own test builds its blocks, and the missing part never exists outside a real dispatch |

Four test classes replace `EmailProvider` with `@MockitoBean` — `AuthServiceTest`,
`PasswordResetServiceTest`, `AuthFlowTest` and `EmailChangeFlowTest` — because
it is the only way to read a code that is stored as an Argon2 hash and therefore
irreversible by design. Each *distinct* context configuration buys a separate
Spring context and PostgreSQL container, so the build starts **four**:
`AuthFlowTest`, `AuthRateLimitTest` (which overrides the rate limit properties),
`AuthServiceTest`, and `RefreshTokenServiceTest`. **Ten** classes reuse an existing
context rather than adding a fifth container: `PasswordResetServiceTest` matches
`AuthServiceTest`; `UserProfileFlowTest`, `EmailChangeFlowTest`,
`AccountDeletionTest`, `VehicleReadTest`, `VehicleWriteTest`,
`RegistrationCertificateFlowTest` and `OcrFlowTest` match `AuthFlowTest`; and
`RegistrationCertificateRepositoryTest` and `OcrQuotaTest` match
`RefreshTokenServiceTest` — three
annotations and no MockMvc. Six of those — `UserProfileFlowTest`, `AccountDeletionTest`,
`VehicleReadTest`, `VehicleWriteTest`, `RegistrationCertificateFlowTest` and
`OcrFlowTest` — do so **with an `EmailProvider` mock
they never use**, because `@MockitoBean` joins the cache key through
`BeanOverrideContextCustomizer` and dropping it would have split the context.
All six run in about a second with no container of their own, and the build
log proves it: none prints a Spring banner. **Matching an existing class's
annotations exactly is what keeps the container count from growing with every
new test class**, and an unused `@MockitoBean` is a legitimate way to match —
documented in the test as intentional, or the next reader deletes it as debris.

**The rate limiter is a singleton and its counters are not transactional**, so
they survive between test methods and between test classes sharing a context.
`src/test/resources/application.yml` therefore sets every limit to 10000.
Realistic limits there would make unrelated tests fail depending on the order
they happened to run in — `AuthFlowTest` alone logs in eleven times, which is
exactly how this was found.

The second test guards specification section 20. Configuration changes are
frequent and a Neon URL leaking into the test context would be invisible — tests
would pass while writing to the real database. This makes that mistake loud.

Test infrastructure: `TestcontainersConfiguration` supplies a
`PostgreSQLContainer` annotated `@ServiceConnection`, pinned to
`postgres:18.4-alpine` to match the Neon server version. `src/test/resources/application.yml`
deliberately shadows the main configuration file so `application-local.yml` can
never reach a test.

**Because it shadows rather than merges, every `@ConditionalOnProperty` seam has
to be repeated in it.** `@DefaultValue` on the properties record does not help:
it applies when the record is bound, while the condition reads the raw
environment, so an absent property means an absent bean however the default is
written. Both seams are listed there today — `email.provider: logging` and
`ocr.provider: stub`. Anything else conditioned on a property must be added at
the moment the condition is written, because the omission stays invisible until
some bean finally injects the seam, and then it fails everywhere at once.

### End-to-end — 1 Playwright journey

`frontend/e2e/journey.spec.ts`, run with `npm run e2e` from `frontend/` while
`mvnw spring-boot:test-run` holds a backend open from `backend/`. Green in 9.4 s
on 2026-08-22.

One test, not six, because it is one journey and each step depends on the last:
register → verify with the code that was actually sent → sign in → add a vehicle
→ open its certificate → add a document. What it covers that nothing else does is
the **seam** — two applications, two processes, a real browser and a real
database, with CORS, cookies, tokens and navigation all live at once. Every one
of those has already broken something this project's unit tests called green,
most recently the missing PUT method that cost two phases.

**Match navigation links with `exact: true`.** Playwright matches an accessible
name by substring, and the product is called *Garajul Meu*, so a query for
*Garaj* resolves to both the header link and the navigation one. That is not a
quirk of one test; it applies to any query for that destination for as long as
the product keeps its name.

`tsconfig.e2e.json` type-checks these files - added the same day, once the gap
showed up as red squiggles in the editor rather than as a failing build. See the
decision log.

### Coverage — what the first report actually said

Run with `mvnw clean verify` (report at `backend/target/site/jacoco/index.html`)
and `npm run coverage`. First measured 2026-08-23.

| | Instructions / statements | Branches |
|---|---|---|
| Backend | 90.33% | 69.94% |
| Frontend | 95.45% | 86.47% |

**The branch figure on the backend is low for one reason, and it is not a gap.**
Of 162 missed branches, roughly 90 are the hand-written `equals`/`hashCode` that
every entity carries — `RefreshToken`, `Vehicle`, `User`, `Reminder`,
`RegistrationCertificate`, `OcrUsage`, `NotificationPreferences`,
`NotificationDelivery`, `VehicleDocument`, `UserDevice`, `VerificationToken`,
about five decision points each. Nothing calls them because nothing needs to.
Tests for them would move the number and prove nothing.

**Checked while there, and clean:** the entity `hashCode` returns
`getClass().hashCode()`, which differs between an entity and its Hibernate proxy
and would put both copies in the same `HashSet`. Inert here — every collection of
entities in the codebase is a `List`, and no entity maps a collection of its own.
Worth re-checking if a `Set<SomeEntity>` is ever introduced.

**Two backend classes sit at 0%, both expected.**
`GoogleDocumentAiOcrProvider` (88 instructions) is the adapter blocked on GCP
billing, never selected in tests — the provider seam working as designed.
`ReminderSchedulingConfig` is three instructions of configuration.

**Two frontend files were excluded rather than left at 0%,** because a row that
can never improve teaches people to ignore 0% rows. `src/api/page.ts` declares
only an interface, which TypeScript erases — 0% there is 0 of 0, not untested
code. `src/App.tsx` is six lines wrapping `AppRoutes` in a `BrowserRouter`, and
its own comment says why nothing tests it: `BrowserRouter` reads the real address
bar and cannot be told to start elsewhere. Same category as `main.tsx`.

The next honest questions the report raises, for whenever they are worth it:
`AuthController` (9 of 26 branches missed), `NotificationPreferences.tsx` (66%
branch), `DocumentFields.tsx` (57% statements), `useVehicleImage.ts` (70%
branch). None is a known defect; all are simply less exercised than their
neighbours.

### Frontend tests — 272 in 48 files

| File | Scope |
|---|---|
| `App.test.tsx` (8) | Each of the six routes renders its own page, an unregistered address falls back to not-found, and the shell with its way home survives even there. Asserted against the Romanian resource rather than literals, so rewording a title stays a one-file change |
| `i18n/language.test.ts` (5) | A remembered language is read back; a stored value that is not a supported language is ignored; the remembered choice outranks the browser; the browser's first supported language wins otherwise; Romanian is the fallback. All pure — the candidate list is a parameter, so nothing rewrites a global the whole environment shares |
| `documents/status.test.ts` (6) | The nine sentences, pure and Intl-free — the formatter is a parameter, so the assertions are about the branching. Days left while cover holds; **soon and urgent differ in the sentence and not only in the tone**, since two bands that read identically would leave the colour carrying the meaning alone; elapsed days counted as a positive number. Then the one that justifies the whole file: **the three situations section 11 hands over as a single EXPIRED are told apart by their dates** and produce three distinct keys — a lapse, a lapse with cover already arranged, and a policy that has never begun |
| `pages/VehicleDocumentsPage.test.tsx` (8) | Says plainly when a vehicle has none; shows a stored document with its period and its state; **refuses an empty expiry without asking the backend**; sends untouched optional fields as `null` rather than `""`, asserted on the exact body; reloads the list after adding; confirms before deleting and reloads after, and changing your mind deletes nothing; a backend refusal is translated and the list is not re-fetched. **One of these was written wrong first**: it waited for a heading its own single-page stub could never produce, and now waits for the form clearing itself — which is what a success actually looks like when the list is empty by design |
| `pages/DashboardPage.test.tsx` (5) | A way in when the garage is empty; each vehicle named with how long its cover has left; **what is not configured, beside the screen that configures it**. Then section 11's two hard cases read from the screen rather than from a unit test: a lapse reads as the gap **and still names when cover resumes**, and a policy that has not started is told apart from one that ran out |
| `pages/VehicleHistoryPage.test.tsx` (6) | Nothing yet; what was kept, newest entry first; **narrowing to one type asks the server for that type**, asserted on the address rather than on the rendered list, since the filter living in the path is the whole design; turning the page asks for the next one and says where you are; the ends of the list are not clickable; **changing the filter goes back to page one**, asserted by the absence of `page=` in the last address asked for |
| `documents/reminderState.test.ts` (8) | Pure, no DOM. **The split where Romanian splits, above nineteen** — "cu 14 zile" but "cu 30 de zile" — asserted at 19 and 20 so the boundary is the thing tested rather than one convenient case. One day and the day itself are words, not numbers. **A sent reminder is reported by when it was sent, not when it was due**, and asserted against both instants so a copy-paste between them would fail; a sent reminder with no instant still gets a sentence. Then the guard, in **both directions**: every status produces wording the locale holds, *and* the locale holds no wording no status produces — the shape `errorKey.test.ts` arrived at after Phase 9's five OCR codes sat outside their guard for two phases |
| `pages/VehicleDocumentDetailsPage.test.tsx` (8) | The stored document and its state; **the reminder list and the sentence that keeps it honest**, asserted together — a screen showing "marcat ca trimis" without the standing note that push is native-only would mislead every current user; **a correction sends the whole record with an emptied note as null**, asserted on the exact body, because that is the only way a note can ever be removed; a renewal sends a period and **no type at all**; the renewal form refuses an empty expiry without asking the backend; a refusal is translated and the correction form keeps its values; a document that is not there says so. And the one that earned its keep: **renewing opens the record it created, with both forms fresh** — the second half of that assertion found a real defect, since React Router had been keeping the screen mounted across the identifier change. The five period labels appear twice, so two named helpers read the correction form and the renewal form by position rather than scattering `getAllByLabelText` indices through the file |
| `i18n/errorKey.test.ts` (3) | A known code maps to its key, an unknown one to the generic message, and **every code the backend can currently send has wording** — if one of those ever resolved to UNKNOWN, a real failure would be shown as "something went wrong" while the backend had said exactly what happened. **The list grows with each phase that makes a code reachable** — and on 2026-08-17 it was found to have stopped growing: it held **fifteen** codes while the locale held **twenty-six**, so Phase 9's five OCR codes had been translated, shown on real screens, and left outside this guard for two whole phases. Fixed in both senses: the list is complete, and **the test now also asserts the reverse**, that every translated code is listed — a code nobody lists is a code nobody is checking, which is exactly how the gap opened. **Three** codes remain untranslated, for the same honest reason: **nothing throws them.** The two registration-certificate codes and `VEHICLE_ACCESS_DENIED` are declared in the enum and raised from nowhere. **It was five until 2026-08-18, and this test is what noticed the difference.** Phase 12.2 made `IMAGE_INVALID_TYPE` and `STORAGE_PROVIDER_UNAVAILABLE` reachable — `VehicleImageValidator` throws the first, `FileStorageProvider.put` declares the second — so the note claiming nothing threw them had quietly become false. Translating them for the Phase 12 frontend without adding them to the list failed the reverse assertion, which is the half added after the OCR gap and the first time it has caught anything |
| `vehicles/VehicleImage.test.tsx` (7) | The component directly rather than through the route table, since the parent screen contributes only two props — the cost being that no sign-in happens, so the token is put into the store by hand. **The assertion the whole hook exists for**: an existing photograph is fetched from the vehicle's own image address, **with the Authorization header**, and shown from an object URL — an `<img src>` pointing at that endpoint would arrive unauthenticated and answer 401. A vehicle with no photograph **asks the server nothing**, because the 404 would be correct and a predictable console error on every such screen. Uploading shows it and turns the control into replace; **replacing fetches again and revokes the object URL it drops**, which is the only cheap place a leak-per-replacement can be caught. A refused type is explained and nothing is shown. Deleting asks first, and confirming returns the control to add. **jsdom implements neither `createObjectURL` nor `revokeObjectURL`**, so both are stubbed — which is what makes the revoke assertable and also what leaves the real round trip to a browser walkthrough |
| `certificate/values.test.ts` (8) | **The field table covers exactly the keys of `CertificateData`** — the guard against a field that exists in the type and not in the table, which nothing else would notice: the screen would never render it and every save would clear it. A full certificate survives a round trip; absent values become empty strings and come back as nulls; numbers go as numbers; nonsense becomes null rather than NaN; an untouched checkbox is false. And two about how a person actually writes a number here — **a decimal with a comma**, and a whole number however it is grouped |
| `components/CheckboxField.test.tsx` (3) | The label reaches the box, the state shown is the state given, and a change reports what it became rather than that it happened |
| `certificate/CertificateOverlay.test.tsx` (6) | Zoom moves in both directions, one button returns to the original size, and the controls disable at their limits — a control that does nothing when pressed says so before it is pressed. **What these cannot cover is the panning**: `overflow: auto` produces no scrollable area in jsdom, so what is asserted is the zoom level, not the scroll. That part is only visible in a browser at phone width. Then the three scan states, **asserted as words rather than as borders**: a filled field says where its value came from, an uncertain one asks to be checked, and **a validation problem outranks the scan state on the same field** — a box can say one thing, and it must be the one the person has to act on |
| `certificate/coordinates.test.ts` (3) | **Every field has a position and every position belongs to a field** — checked in both directions, because a field with no place would simply never appear on the template and a position left behind by a rename would sit there forever, and neither raises anything by itself. Every box lies inside the template, in fractions rather than pixels |
| `pages/CertificatePage.test.tsx` (8) | What is stored is shown over the template, with empty fields empty rather than the word "null". **Saving sends the whole certificate** — asserted by counting the keys against the field table, because a field this screen omitted would be cleared on the server. A decimal typed with a comma survives the trip. Emptying an optional field clears it instead of sending an empty string. A required field left blank is refused with no request sent. A certificate that is not there says so — the same 404 that answers for somebody else's. And **the way back names the place it actually leads to**, asserting the link's name and its destination together, because the label said "back to the garage" while leading to the vehicle. And **the certificate number is one value shown in two places**, asked for with `getAllByLabelText` because a label appearing twice is precisely the shape the singular query refuses. Then five about the scan: a photograph fills what it could read; **a field the photograph could not show keeps what was already there**, the no-erase property asserted through the screen and not only through `applyScan`; the result names how many fields landed in each state; an exhausted allowance is explained and changes nothing; and saving after a scan sends the scanned values, because a scan proposes and the ordinary Save button is still what stores. **The fetch stub checks the OCR path before the certificate path on purpose** — `/ocr/registration-certificate` contains `/registration-certificate`, so the obvious order would have sent every scan to the certificate stub and passed for the wrong reason |
| `components/LanguageSwitcher.test.tsx` (3) | Both languages named in themselves; switching re-renders the page in the chosen language; the choice is written to storage |
| `api/refresh.test.ts` (5) | The new token is stored; **one request no matter how many callers ask at once** — the test that justifies the module; a later expiry may refresh again rather than reusing the first result; a refused refresh clears the token; so does an outright network failure |
| `api/client.test.ts` (9) | The bearer header appears only when there is a token; a 401 refreshes once and retries once; **two simultaneous 401s produce exactly one refresh**; a 401 from `/auth/login` refreshes nothing; a failure becomes `ApiError` with the code and request id intact; a response with no JSON at all still becomes one; a 204 parses to nothing; and **a multipart upload is sent with no `Content-Type` at all**, so the browser sets the one carrying the boundary |
| `auth/AuthProvider.test.tsx` (6) | A session the cookie still supports is restored; with none, the status settles on anonymous; **the status is `unknown` until the attempt settles**; the account's language is adopted; signing in loads the profile; signing out returns to anonymous |
| `forms/validate.test.ts` (5) | The first broken rule per field and no more; **a server field error answers with the form's own precise wording**; when two rules share the `Size` constraint, the one the value actually breaks wins; an unrecognised constraint falls back to a generic message |
| `forms/useSubmission.test.tsx` (3) | An `ApiError` is captured and returned; success returns null; anything that never reached the backend becomes `UNKNOWN` |
| `components/FormError.test.tsx` (3) | Nothing renders without an error; a backend code is translated; an opaque failure offers the request id |
| `routes/carriedEmail.test.ts` (6) | An address handed over is read back; absent state and a non-string value both yield nothing — the reload case every screen has to survive. For `returnTo`: a recorded path is read, absent state yields nothing, and **anything that is not a path on this site is refused**, including an absolute URL and a `javascript:` scheme |
| `auth/RequireAuth.test.tsx` (6) | Somebody without a session is sent to sign in; **the wait is visible and shows neither the page nor the sign-in form while the status is unknown**, then resolves; a signed-in visitor is let through. The primary navigation offers exactly the three destinations section 5 fixes, marks the one currently open with `aria-current`, and is absent on the public side |
| `api/endpoints/auth.test.ts` (3) | Register, verify and reset each send exactly the body the backend declares |
| `pages/LoginPage.test.tsx` (7) | An empty form is refused without a request; signing in lands on the dashboard; **signing in returns to the address that turned the person away**, asserted on that screen's heading rather than on its content, so the test stays about routing; refused credentials are translated; a server field error lands on its own field via `aria-describedby`; the inputs carry the autocomplete hints a password manager needs; the address handed over by verification or reset pre-fills |
| `pages/WelcomePage.test.tsx` (3) | Both ways in when signed out; **a signed-in visitor is sent to the dashboard**; neither offer while the session is still being restored, then the anonymous one appears |
| `pages/DashboardPage.test.tsx` (1) | Says it has nothing to show. Nothing is fetched: `GET /api/v1/dashboard` waits for Phase 10, when there will be something to project |
| `pages/GaragePage.test.tsx` (4) | The vehicles are listed, **by nickname where there is one and by make and description where there is not** — both labelling rules in one test, because the rule *is* the choice between them. The empty garage says so and offers the way to add one. A refused request shows translated wording rather than nothing, and retrying brings the list back — recovery, not merely a second call |
| `certificate/scan.test.ts` (11) | Applying a scan to a form, with no React. A detected value is written; **a proposal with no value leaves what is there alone**, for both "not detected" and "needs review" — the rule that protects a VIN somebody typed before photographing a creased certificate; a status is recorded for every field the scan mentions and for no others; a field name this build does not know is ignored rather than turned into a key no input reads. **The values a scan produces are accepted by the save endpoint with no conversion in between**, asserted end to end through `fromForm` on a date and a decimal, which are the two that would break if either side started converting. The counts behind the message are tallied once. Then three on the general form: only the fields a screen lists as accepted are written, an accepted field with no value is left alone, and statuses are recorded only for accepted names — the same rule, proved on the shape Add Vehicle passes rather than only on the certificate's |
| `pages/AddVehiclePage.test.tsx` (8) | An incomplete form is refused with no request sent at all, asserted per field. A filled one creates the vehicle and lands on the garage, with the submitted body checked rather than assumed. **A blank nickname is sent as typed**, which is the decision that keeps the meaning of "no nickname" on the backend only. **A duplicate VIN lands in the banner and not on the VIN input** — it is about the vehicle, not about one value being malformed, and the test asserts the field is *not* marked invalid. A server field error on `vin` is answered in this form's own wording, which is the case client-side rules exist for and cannot cover: the bounds are duplicated between halves with nothing linking them. Reads in its stub answer with a **canonical** vehicle rather than echoing the request, because what this screen owns is the request it sends and where it goes next; what the vehicle's own screen renders is that screen's test. Then three on section 3's other entrance: **a photograph fills the four fields printed on the certificate and creates nothing until the button is pressed**; a field the photograph could not be read for is **named** rather than decorated, because on a form of four inputs the useful thing is which one to look at; and **a scan leaves the nickname alone**, since it is not on the document at all |
| `pages/VehicleDetailsPage.test.tsx` (7) | The identity from the certificate is shown. **A vehicle that is not there says so** — the same 404 the backend gives for somebody else's vehicle, which is where that security decision becomes visible. Renaming shows the new name, and clearing the nickname returns the vehicle to its certificate name, which is the only way to remove one. Then the three halves of deletion: **one click is not enough**, asserted by counting that no DELETE was sent; confirming deletes and lands on the garage; cancelling deletes nothing and stays. The stub keeps the vehicle in memory so a PATCH is visible to the GET after it — one that always answered the same thing could not tell a rename that worked from one silently discarded |
| `api/useResource.test.tsx` (4) | Data arrives and loading clears; a refused request is reported as its code instead of thrown; **a rejection that is not an `ApiError` becomes `UNKNOWN`**, which is the branch that keeps a dropped connection from rendering an empty message; reloading asks again |
| `pages/ProfilePage.test.tsx` (2) | The account is shown, **queried inside `<main>`** because the language name legitimately appears again in the header switcher; signing out leaves the protected area, which the gate does rather than the page |
| `pages/RegisterPage.test.tsx` (4) | A too-short password is refused locally; registration carries the address to verification; a taken address is reported; **the account is created in the language the switcher currently shows** |
| `pages/VerifyEmailPage.test.tsx` (5) | The handed-over address pre-fills, and its absence leaves a usable empty field; confirming moves on to sign-in; a refused code is reported; a resend says so |
| `pages/ForgotPasswordPage.test.tsx` (3) | An empty address is refused locally; the address is carried to the reset screen; the rate limit is reported |
| `pages/ResetPasswordPage.test.tsx` (4) | The handed-over address pre-fills; a short new password is refused locally; a successful reset ends at sign-in; an expired code is reported as its own outcome |

`src/test/setup.ts` pins the language to Romanian before every test and clears
storage afterwards. jsdom reports `en-US`, so without that pin every component
test would run in English and a Romanian assertion would fail for a reason that
has nothing to do with the component under test.

Playwright and end-to-end coverage arrive with Phase 14.

## External integrations configured

None yet. Just-in-time schedule: Neon at Phase 2, Resend at Phase 4,
Google Document AI at Phase 9, Firebase at Phase 11, Cloudflare R2 at Phase 12,
Sentry at Phase 15.

## Decisions taken during the build

| Date | Decision |
|---|---|
| 2026-08-12 | IDE split: backend in Eclipse, frontend in VS Code. `pom.xml` and `package.json` remain the source of truth for project structure, so IDE metadata is gitignored and regenerable. |
| 2026-08-12 | Line endings handled by a committed root `.gitattributes` (`* text=auto eol=lf`, `*.cmd`/`*.bat`/`*.ps1` CRLF) rather than per-machine `core.autocrlf`, so Linux CI and the macOS iOS build behave identically. |
| 2026-08-12 | Default branch `main`. GitHub repository is **public**; secret hygiene is therefore release-critical — an exposed secret must be rotated, not merely deleted from a later commit. |
| 2026-08-12 | Single root `.gitignore` for the whole monorepo. The `.gitignore` and `.gitattributes` files generated by Spring Initializr and by Vite were deleted; their genuinely new rules (`*.local`, `logs/`) were merged into the root file. |
| 2026-08-12 | Base package `ro.garajulmeu`, artifact `backend`. Package name field on Initializr was overridden so packages are `ro.garajulmeu.<feature>`, matching specification section 4. |
| 2026-08-12 | Spring Boot 4.1.0 — the only stable line Initializr still offers. Note that Boot 4 renamed starters: `spring-boot-starter-webmvc` (not `-web`) and per-module test starters (no `spring-boot-starter-test`). Spring Boot 3.x tutorials will give dependency names that no longer exist. |
| 2026-08-12 | Frontend uses ESLint rather than the create-vite 9 default of Oxlint, so that `eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y` is available. **Corrected 2026-08-14:** this was recorded as satisfying "the accessibility requirement in specification section 36", and no such requirement exists — section 36 is the definition of V1 success. The specification says almost nothing about accessibility; its only line is in section 7, that colour is never the sole accessibility signal for certificate field states. The tooling choice still stands on its own merits, but it is ours rather than mandated. |
| 2026-08-12 | Template `react-ts` rather than `react-compiler-ts`. React Compiler is a reversible one-line addition later if performance requires it. |
| 2026-08-12 | Node upgraded 22.19.0 → 24.19.0 because Node 22 is Maintenance LTS and 24 is Active LTS; the version has to be declared for CI and Cloudflare Pages anyway. |
| 2026-08-12 | Neon Auth left disabled at project creation. It would store its own users and sessions and displace the authentication design in specification section 14 and the `users` / `refresh_tokens` / `verification_tokens` tables in section 10. It can still be enabled later from project settings if that ever changes. |
| 2026-08-12 | Two datasources: application runtime uses the Neon **pooled** endpoint, Flyway uses the **direct** endpoint via `spring.flyway.url`. The pooler runs in transaction mode and cannot hold the session-level lock Flyway relies on. Verified in the startup log — the two hostnames differ by the `-pooler` suffix. |
| 2026-08-12 | Configuration moved from `application.properties` to `application.yml`, since the datasource, Hikari, JPA and Flyway trees are deeply nested. |
| 2026-08-12 | `spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto: validate` and `spring.jpa.open-in-view: false`. Flyway is the only thing allowed to change the schema; Hibernate may only verify mappings. |
| 2026-08-12 | Local secrets pattern: committed `application.yml` contains only `${PLACEHOLDER}` references; real values come from gitignored `backend/application-local.yml` locally, and from real environment variables in production. An undefined placeholder fails startup loudly rather than failing at connection time. |
| 2026-08-12 | Spring Boot 4 uses a `spring-boot-starter-flyway` starter rather than depending on `org.flywaydb:flyway-core` directly, as Boot 3.x did. |
| 2026-08-12 | JDBC URLs use `channelBinding` (camelCase). Neon's native connection string uses the libpq spelling `channel_binding`, which the PostgreSQL JDBC driver rejects. |
| 2026-08-13 | Testcontainers 2.0.5: container classes are **no longer generic**. `new PostgreSQLContainer<>(...)` from every 1.x example fails to compile; the canonical class is now `org.testcontainers.postgresql.PostgreSQLContainer`, with a legacy copy left in `org.testcontainers.containers`. Artifact ids also changed to `testcontainers-junit-jupiter` and `testcontainers-postgresql`. |
| 2026-08-13 | The Mockito agent path resolves only because `maven-dependency-plugin`'s `properties` goal runs first; it publishes each dependency's jar path as a property named `groupId:artifactId:type`. Without that plugin, `${org.mockito:mockito-core:jar}` reaches the JVM as literal text and the forked test VM fails to start. |
| 2026-08-13 | Test isolation is enforced by a real assertion, not only by configuration: `usesThrowawayContainerAndNeverTheHostedDatabase` fails if the live JDBC URL ever points at Neon. |
| 2026-08-13 | Error responses use a small project-specific JSON shape rather than RFC 9457 Problem Details. Problem Details carries a human-readable `detail` field, which conflicts with the section 6 requirement that all user-facing wording is chosen and translated by the frontend. |
| 2026-08-13 | `ErrorCode` owns its HTTP status. Keeping the mapping in the catalogue rather than at each throw site prevents the same failure answering 404 in one endpoint and 400 in another. |
| 2026-08-13 | Boot 4 moved the test slices: `@WebMvcTest` and `@AutoConfigureMockMvc` are now in `org.springframework.boot.webmvc.test.autoconfigure`, not `org.springframework.boot.test.autoconfigure.web.servlet`. |
| 2026-08-13 | Handler tests use the `@WebMvcTest` slice with a throwaway controller, so they need no database and run in about one second rather than twenty. Slice tests are the default; full `@SpringBootTest` is reserved for cases that genuinely need persistence. |
| 2026-08-13 | Correlation uses a plain MDC entry and `logging.pattern.correlation`, not Micrometer Tracing. Distributed tracing solves a problem a single-instance modular monolith does not have (specification section 19). If tracing is introduced later, the same pattern slot is where its trace and span ids belong. |
| 2026-08-13 | `ApiErrorResponse` carries `requestId`, so a user can quote the identifier from an error screen and the exact request can be found in the logs without knowing their account or the time. |
| 2026-08-13 | Verification codes are hashed with the same Argon2 encoder as passwords. A six-digit code has only a million possibilities and a fast hash would be trivially reversible from a leaked database. |
| 2026-08-13 | Registration answers `EMAIL_ALREADY_EXISTS` rather than hiding whether an address is taken. Specification section 17 defines that code and section 14 requires non-disclosure only for forgot-password. |
| 2026-08-13 | `existsByEmail` is backed up by catching `DataIntegrityViolationException` and re-throwing the same code. Two simultaneous registrations can both pass the check and collide only at the unique index; without the catch the caller would get `INTERNAL_ERROR` for an ordinary conflict. |
| 2026-08-13 | Password policy: 12–128 characters, no composition rules. Current guidance favours length over forced symbols, which mostly produce predictable substitutions. The maximum bounds the Argon2 work per request. |
| 2026-08-13 | `ExceptionHandlerExceptionResolver` is pinned to ERROR level. At its default WARN it logs every resolved exception including rejected field values, which put an attempted password into the log during a manual registration test — forbidden by specification section 27, which names passwords, tokens and verification codes explicitly. Our own handler logs field names only. Found by reading the log during manual verification; no automated test asserts on framework logging, so this class of leak needs a human eye. |
| 2026-08-13 | Refresh tokens are hashed with **SHA-256, not Argon2** — the opposite of passwords and verification codes, for two reasons. The token holds 256 bits of entropy, so brute force from a leaked database is irrelevant. And Argon2's per-hash salt makes lookup by value impossible: every refresh would have to load every token and compare each at ~50 ms. **The hash choice follows the entropy of the secret, not habit.** |
| 2026-08-13 | A `familyId` is one login on one device. Rotation keeps the family; reuse revokes it entirely; logout revokes it. Signing out on one device therefore leaves other devices signed in. |
| 2026-08-13 | Detected reuse revokes the family, which also invalidates the honest holder's current token. That is deliberate: the alternative is letting a thief refresh indefinitely. A stolen token buys at most one refresh before it becomes an alarm. |
| 2026-08-13 | A token revoked by logout is indistinguishable from one revoked by rotation, so logging out and then replaying answers `REFRESH_TOKEN_REUSED` rather than something more precise. Distinguishing them would need another column; noted as an observation, not a defect. |
| 2026-08-13 | Login hashes a throwaway value when the address is unknown, so a missing account costs the same as a wrong password. The database lookup is microseconds and an Argon2 comparison is tens of milliseconds; without the dummy hash, response time alone reveals which addresses hold accounts. |
| 2026-08-13 | Login checks the password **before** `isEmailVerified`. The other order would tell anyone who guesses an address that it exists and is unverified, without knowing the password. As written, `EMAIL_NOT_VERIFIED` only reaches someone who has already proved they know it. |
| 2026-08-13 | `LoginResponse` returns `expiresInSeconds`, not an absolute timestamp. A phone with a wrong clock would misjudge an absolute expiry; a duration is immune to clock skew. This is also the OAuth2 convention. |
| 2026-08-13 | `LoginRequest` does **not** carry the 12-character minimum that `RegisterRequest` does. A length policy applies to new passwords; enforcing it at login would lock existing users out of their own accounts the day the policy tightens. |
| 2026-08-13 | JWT uses Spring Security's own Nimbus-backed `JwtEncoder`/`JwtDecoder` rather than `jjwt`. It adds no third-party library, and `oauth2ResourceServer(jwt())` supplies the bearer-token filter, so no authentication filter is written by hand. |
| 2026-08-13 | Symmetric HS256 with a shared secret, not an RSA key pair. An asymmetric key only earns its complexity when a separate service must verify without being able to sign; here one application does both. |
| 2026-08-13 | The issuer claim is the plain string `garajul-meu`, not a URL. RFC 7519 defines `iss` as StringOrURI, so this is valid — but `Jwt.getIssuer()` insists on a URL because it comes from OAuth2, so read `getClaimAsString("iss")` instead. A URL issuer would also bake the still-undecided production domain into every token. |
| 2026-08-13 | `verifyEmail` is annotated `@Transactional(noRollbackFor = ApiException.class)`. A business exception normally rolls the transaction back, which would discard the failed-attempt counter recorded immediately before it — the attempt limit that makes a six-digit code safe would silently never fire. |
| 2026-08-13 | Verification answers `VERIFICATION_CODE_INVALID` for an unknown address, so the endpoint cannot be used to discover which addresses hold accounts. An expired code answers `VERIFICATION_CODE_EXPIRED` distinctly: the caller already held a valid code for that address, so nothing is disclosed, and the client needs the distinction to offer "resend" rather than "retry". |
| 2026-08-13 | `resend-verification` answers 204 identically for an unknown address, an already verified one and a real reissue. |
| 2026-08-13 | Because verification always reads the **newest** token for the account, `invalidateOutstandingCodes` is defence in depth rather than the active mechanism — an older token is never consulted. Its test asserts the `invalidatedAt` column directly, so it cannot pass merely because a newer code shadows the old one. |
| 2026-08-13 | `invalidateOutstandingCodes` is annotated `@Modifying(flushAutomatically = true, clearAutomatically = true)`. A bulk JPQL update bypasses the persistence context entirely: without `flushAutomatically` a freshly persisted but unflushed token escapes the UPDATE unnoticed, and without `clearAutomatically` every already-loaded entity keeps its stale values. **After any bulk update, previously loaded entities must not be trusted.** The defect was invisible until a test asserted the column instead of the outcome. |
| 2026-08-13 | Slice tests must name what they load. `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` uses `@WebMvcTest(controllers = …)`: the slice instantiates every `@RestController` but excludes every `@Service`, so the first real controller broke an unrelated test. Scoping the slice is the fix, not mocking each new controller's dependencies. |
| 2026-08-14 | **No primitive component in any request DTO.** Spring Boot 4 ships **Jackson 3** (3.1.4), which enables `DeserializationFeature.FAIL_ON_NULL_FOR_PRIMITIVES` by default — the opposite of Jackson 2. An absent JSON property reaches a record's canonical constructor as null, and `null → boolean` now throws before the constructor runs, so the *whole request* becomes unreadable rather than defaulting to false. `LoginRequest.refreshTokenInBody` was a primitive `boolean`, so any login that omitted the flag — every browser login — answered `400 MALFORMED_REQUEST`. Six `AuthFlowTest` failures caught it. Fix: box the component and let `wantsRefreshTokenInBody()` own the meaning of absent. A compact constructor cannot rescue this; the failure is earlier. |
| 2026-08-14 | The global escape hatch `spring.jackson.deserialization.fail-on-null-for-primitives: false` was **rejected**. It would weaken deserialization for every DTO in the project permanently. For a missing boolean, false is plausible; for a missing `int`, 0 is a meaningful and usually wrong value — an absent `offset_days` or `engine_capacity_cc` would become a silent zero instead of a rejected request. Jackson 3 turned this on deliberately. Corrections are paid at the defect, not by disabling a protection project-wide. |
| 2026-08-14 | Only `/login` carries a channel flag. At login the client has presented nothing, so the backend cannot infer whether it has a cookie jar; `refreshTokenInBody` is an explicit opt-in whose default is the safe one. On `/refresh` and `/logout` no flag exists or is needed — the presence of a cookie or a body token *is* the answer. Section 14's ban on an `X-Client-Type` header is therefore satisfied in substance, not just in spelling. |
| 2026-08-14 | **A successful password reset also marks the address verified.** Entering a code that arrived by email proves exactly what email verification asks for, so treating the two differently would be an inconsistency rather than a precaution. It also removes a real dead end: register, never verify, forget the password, reset successfully — and still be refused at login with `EMAIL_NOT_VERIFIED`, with nothing on screen explaining why. |
| 2026-08-14 | The six-digit code check was extracted into one private `consumeCode`. The same security-sensitive sequence written in two flows is how they quietly drift apart. The refactor touched working code, and the ten existing `AuthServiceTest` tests were the safety net that made it safe — they stayed 10/10. |
| 2026-08-14 | Rate limiting is **written by hand**, not Bucket4j. One algorithm — a fixed-window counter — for a single-instance backend is roughly fifty testable lines, and specification section 19 says any move to several instances needs shared storage designed first, so a distributed token-bucket library would be carrying machinery we would replace anyway. Section 4 of the Master Prompt does not list Bucket4j, so adding it would have needed an approved architecture change. |
| 2026-08-14 | Rate limit policies are grouped by **what a request costs**, not by endpoint, so the grouping explains itself. `/login` and `/verify-email` share `credentialCheck` because each pays an Argon2 comparison; `/register` and `/resend-verification` share `emailDispatch` because each sends mail to a real inbox. The policy name is part of the key, so the groups never draw on one another's counter. |
| 2026-08-14 | Two keys per request, address **and** email, checked in that order. Either key alone is avoidable: counting only by address punishes everyone behind one NAT for one person's behaviour, and counting only by email lets a distributed attacker rotate emails and never spend a budget. The order is not cosmetic — an exhausted address returns before an email key is created, so flooding with distinct emails cannot grow the map without first spending the address budget. A distributed attack still grows it; that residual is bounded by the number of addresses and is what shared storage fixes at scaling time. |
| 2026-08-14 | `/logout` is deliberately **not** rate limited. Refusing a logout is worse than allowing one: it leaves a user who asked to end their session with a live session. The endpoint costs a SHA-256 lookup, and revoking anything requires already holding a valid 256-bit token. |
| 2026-08-14 | Configuration metadata for `@ConfigurationProperties` records comes from the annotation processor, which emits correctly prefixed and correctly typed entries into `spring-configuration-metadata.json`. **Never accept Eclipse's "create metadata" quick fix.** On 2026-08-14 it wrote six entries into `additional-spring-configuration-metadata.json` missing the `garajul-meu.` prefix and typed `java.lang.String` instead of `Integer` and `Duration`; the two files merge, so the result was six phantom keys binding to nothing. **It happened again on 2026-08-15**, and that time the fabricated metadata produced a false error: `garajul-meu.cors.allowed-origins` was declared `java.lang.String`, so Eclipse reported "Expecting a 'String' but got a 'Sequence'" against a list that was entirely correct. The trap has a shape worth recognising — **writing the YAML before the properties class exists** is what makes Eclipse offer the quick fix at all. Write the record first. The same file also held five entries for `DB_URL`, `DB_USERNAME`, `DB_PASSWORD`, `DB_MIGRATION_URL` and `JWT_SECRET`, which are `${}` placeholder names resolved from the environment and not configuration properties of this application at all. The whole file was deleted; nothing in this project needs it. |
| 2026-08-14 | **There are two paths to a 401, and `exceptionHandling()` governs only one.** A request with *no* token is refused later by `AuthorizationFilter` and handled by `ExceptionTranslationFilter`, which uses the global entry point. A request whose bearer token *fails to decode* is refused by `BearerTokenAuthenticationFilter`, which calls **its own** entry point on the spot and never reaches `ExceptionTranslationFilter`. The custom entry point must therefore be set on `oauth2ResourceServer(...)` as well as on `exceptionHandling(...)`; neither replaces the other. Wiring only the first left forged tokens on Spring's default, and `AuthFlowTest` caught it. |
| 2026-08-14 | Replacing the resource server's default entry point also removes its `WWW-Authenticate` header, which is a gain rather than a loss. That header carried `error_description="An error occurred while attempting to decode the Jwt: ..."` — English prose the frontend must own per section 6 — and `resource_metadata` naming the server host, which is internal detail a response has no place to carry: section 17 gives a failure one stable code and nothing else. RFC 6750 recommends the header; our only client reads the JSON code, so the recommendation buys nothing and costs a leak. |
| 2026-08-14 | **CSRF stays disabled through Phase 15; the token mechanism arrives at Phase 16.** The refresh cookie is `SameSite=Strict`, `HttpOnly`, `Secure` and path-scoped to `/api/v1/auth`, so a browser attaches it to no cross-site request at all — that is the primary defence against exactly the attack CSRF tokens prevent, not a secondary one. A CSRF token on top is genuine defence in depth, but it needs a frontend that reads and replays it, and that frontend does not exist yet; nor can it be tested convincingly on localhost, where there are no two real origins. It lands with `app.<domain>` and `api.<domain>`. Residual risk accepted until then: an XSS or subdomain takeover on any same-site host could issue same-site requests, which a token would still block. |
| 2026-08-14 | **PATCH means absent-is-unchanged, and that decides the validation.** `UpdateProfileRequest` carries no `@NotBlank`: it rejects an absent value, which would turn PATCH into PUT. `@Pattern(".*\\S.*")` does the work `@NotBlank` would have done without forbidding absence — Bean Validation skips a null, so only a value that is actually present has to contain a non-whitespace character. |
| 2026-08-14 | The timezone is checked in the service rather than declaratively. Membership of `ZoneId.getAvailableZoneIds()` cannot be expressed as an annotation without a custom constraint, and the price of not writing one is that the response carries no field name — acceptable while this endpoint has exactly one field that can produce a `VALIDATION_ERROR` without one. **Revisit the moment a second appears.** The check itself is not pedantry: Phase 11 computes reminder day boundaries from this column, and `Europe/Bucuresti` looks entirely plausible until something tries to resolve it. |
| 2026-08-14 | `ChangePasswordRequest.currentPassword` carries no length bound, for the same reason `LoginRequest` does not: a length policy applies to **new** passwords. Enforcing today's twelve-character minimum on the existing one would refuse exactly the people whose password is too short and who are trying to fix it. |
| 2026-08-14 | **Changing the password ends every session, including the caller's own, and the endpoint answers 204 rather than a fresh token pair.** Section 14 requires this after a reset and the reasoning applies at least as strongly here: if the password is being changed because somebody else learned it, their sessions must not outlive it. Sparing the caller's own session would require the access token to carry its refresh-token family, which it deliberately does not — the token holds `iss`, `iat`, `exp`, `sub` and nothing more. 204 is the honest answer: log in again with the password you just chose. |
| 2026-08-14 | **`revokeAllSessionsOf` is the last statement in `changePassword`, and the ordering is load-bearing.** It is a `@Modifying(flushAutomatically = true, clearAutomatically = true)` bulk update: it flushes the new hash and then detaches every loaded entity, so anything touching `user` after that line would read or write a stale copy. This is the same trap as `invalidateOutstandingCodes` on 2026-08-13, met a second time in a different flow — which is why the constraint is written as a comment at the call site and not only here. |
| 2026-08-14 | **The `user` → `auth` package edge is accepted deliberately.** `UserService` injects `RefreshTokenService` while `auth` already depends on `user`, so in the modular monolith of section 4 this is a cycle between packages.
