The alternatives — an application event, or a third "session" package — buy indirection without buying independence, since the two modules are one deployable and one transaction. Recorded so that it is a decision rather than an accident. **Trigger to extract the seam: a third module needing to end sessions.** |
| 2026-08-14 | **An unused `@MockitoBean` can be a legitimate context-cache tool.** `UserProfileFlowTest` declares an `EmailProvider` mock it never touches, purely so its annotation stack matches `AuthFlowTest` exactly; `@MockitoBean` joins the Spring context cache key, so omitting it would have started a fifth context and a fifth PostgreSQL container for eight tests that need neither. Verified in the build log — the class prints no banner and creates no container. The field carries a comment saying why, because an unused mock otherwise reads as leftover debris and gets deleted by the next reader, silently costing several seconds on every build from then on. |
| 2026-08-14 | **The email change code goes only to the address already on the account, never to the requested one.** That single choice is what makes a stolen access token insufficient to hijack an account: the thief can provoke the request, but the answer lands in an inbox they do not hold. It also makes the message useful to the person who did *not* ask — the requested address is named in the body, so an unexpected change is recognisable and refusable rather than merely alarming. |
| 2026-08-14 | **Confirming the change resets `email_verified_at` to null and issues a fresh verification code to the new address.** The code proved control of the old inbox, which is what authorises the move, and proved nothing whatever about the new one. The alternative — marking the new address verified — would record a claim nobody checked, and a mistyped address would then sit permanently "verified" while silently receiving nothing, including the Phase 11 reminders this application exists to send. A visible unverified state is a recoverable error; a silent one is not. |
| 2026-08-14 | **An email change deliberately does not revoke sessions**, unlike a password change or reset. No credential changed, so there is nothing to outrun. More importantly the live session is the *only* route back from a mistyped address: login refuses unverified accounts and `forgot-password` writes to the account address, so revoking here would convert a typo into an unrecoverable account. **Residual risk accepted knowingly:** a user who mistypes and then loses the session is still locked out. Closing that gap needs either a confirmation step at the new address — rejected in favour of a single code at the old one — or an operator-side recovery path, which does not exist yet. |
| 2026-08-14 | The requested address rides on the token in `target_value` rather than being sent again at confirmation time. Asking the client to repeat it would let the confirmation name a different address from the one the owner was shown and approved in the email — precisely the substitution this flow exists to prevent. |
| 2026-08-14 | `consumeCode` now **returns the spent token** rather than `void`, and `issueCode` gained a three-argument overload carrying `targetValue`. Both were preferred to giving email change its own copy of the code check, per the 2026-08-13 decision that one security-sensitive sequence written twice is how two flows quietly drift apart — now three flows. The change touched working code, and the ninety existing tests were the net that made it safe. |
| 2026-08-14 | **Account deletion is a hard delete.** No soft delete, no anonymised tombstone row: specification section 24 says permanent, and the address becomes free again immediately. That is both what "delete my account" means to the person asking and what a data protection request means legally. `theAddressIsFreeForANewAccountAfterwards` is the test that would catch the day somebody decides deletion should merely hide the account — a tombstone would keep `ux_users_email` occupied and the re-registration would answer `EMAIL_ALREADY_EXISTS`. |
| 2026-08-14 | **The foreign key is the only thing that removes a deleted account's child rows.** Neither `VerificationToken` nor `RefreshToken` is mapped as an association — both hold a plain `userId` — so JPA cascades nothing, and `ON DELETE CASCADE` in `V2` and `V3` does all the work. A future migration that dropped it would leave orphans in complete silence, which is why the test counts the rows in the database afterwards rather than trusting the mechanism. `deleteAccount` also calls `flush()` explicitly, so the cascade runs inside the method and a broken constraint fails on that line instead of inside Spring's commit machinery. |
| 2026-08-14 | **A deleted account's access token stays cryptographically valid until it expires**, because a stateless JWT cannot be recalled. It opens nothing: every route resolves the account first and finds none, so the answer is `USER_NOT_FOUND`. Recorded and tested rather than left to be discovered, since the alternative — a revocation list checked on every request — would trade the entire benefit of stateless verification for at most fifteen minutes of exposure on an account that no longer holds any data. |
| 2026-08-14 | `DELETE /users/me` carries a request body, which HTTP permits but does not require. The password has to travel somewhere, and a query parameter would put it in access logs and browser history — the two places a password must never be. See the deferred-work table for the proxy risk this creates. |
| 2026-08-14 | The two email-change endpoints live on `UserController` but call `AuthService`, so the controller injects two services. The URL belongs to the account; the flow is a verification-code flow and belongs to `auth`, where the single implementation of the check is private. A controller coordinating two services is a smaller price than either a second copy of that check or a pass-through method on `UserService` that would add a layer without adding meaning. |
| 2026-08-15 | **The route table is separate from the Router that drives it.** `BrowserRouter` reads the real address bar and cannot be told to start anywhere else, so `App.tsx` holds only the Router and `AppRoutes.tsx` holds everything testable. Tests wrap the same table in a `MemoryRouter` with an initial path, which makes every route reachable directly instead of by navigating the UI to get there. `RootLayout` is a pathless layout route so it wraps the not-found page too. |
| 2026-08-15 | **`i18next-browser-languagedetector` was rejected.** The policy needed is three rules — the remembered choice, then the browser, then Romanian — and hand-written they are about twenty **pure** functions' worth of code, with the candidate list passed as a parameter that defaults to `navigator`. The plugin would have made detection a one-time event at initialisation, testable only by rewriting a global the whole environment shares. Same reasoning as the hand-written rate limiter: one narrow policy, fully testable, no dependency. |
| 2026-08-15 | **Two compile-time guards replace a class of silent translation bugs.** `en.ts` is typed `typeof ro`, so a key added to Romanian and forgotten in English fails the build rather than rendering in the wrong language; and `i18next.d.ts` augments `CustomTypeOptions`, so a mistyped key in `t()` fails the build rather than rendering itself to the reader. Romanian is the reference locale because it is the fallback and the backend's default. |
| 2026-08-15 | **Language names are never translated.** `languageNames` lives in `language.ts`, not in the resource files, because somebody who has landed in a language they cannot read must still recognise their own in the list — which they cannot do if "Romanian" is rendered as a Romanian word. The switcher's label is translated; its options are not. |
| 2026-08-15 | **`errorMessageKey` returns a key, not a sentence.** The mapping from a backend code to a translation key is the logic worth testing; translating is i18next's job. Keeping it a plain function rather than a hook keeps it free of React and trivially testable. The unknown branch is the point of the whole module: the backend's catalogue grows every phase, and a code this frontend has never seen must never reach a reader as the literal text `errors.SOMETHING_NEW`. Wording exists only for codes the authentication and account surface can actually raise; inventing Romanian copy for OCR failures that no screen can yet produce would be wording nobody has checked. |
| 2026-08-15 | **A delivered test file silently failed to reach disk.** `i18n/language.test.ts` was never created; the suite reported 11 passed and green, and five assertions simply did not exist. Caught only by comparing the total against the predicted 16. This is the second time paste loss has cost this project real coverage, and it is why the verification rule now says **count the test files as well as the tests** — a missing file removes its whole block at once, which no per-test number reveals. |
| 2026-08-15 | **`rate-limit` was at the wrong nesting level in `application.yml` and bound nothing.** It sat at column zero, a sibling of `garajul-meu` rather than a child, so `RateLimitProperties` fell back to the defaults in its compact constructor — which happen to be **exactly the values written in the file**, 10/15m, 5/1h, 60/1h. Nothing behaved wrongly, and that is what makes it dangerous: anyone tuning those limits in production would have seen no effect at all, silently, and found out mid-attack. Fixed by indenting. **The test suite cannot catch this class of fault**: `src/test/resources/application.yml` deliberately shadows the main file, so no test ever reads its values. A real blind spot, recorded rather than papered over. |
| 2026-08-15 | **CORS was configured now rather than deferred behind a Vite dev proxy.** The proxy would have made the frontend same-origin in development and cost nothing on the backend, but it hides preflight, `credentials: 'include'` and the allowlist until deployment day. This project already carries one "verify against the real proxy at Phase 15" debt — `X-Forwarded-For` — and that one is there because it *cannot* be tested locally. This one can, so it was. |
| 2026-08-15 | **The access token lives in a module variable and never in `localStorage` or `sessionStorage`.** Any XSS can read storage, which is the entire reason the refresh token is an HttpOnly cookie; storing the token that opens every endpoint beside it in plain text would give back exactly what that design bought. The cost is that a reload loses it, which is why the session is restored by a silent refresh at startup rather than read back. |
| 2026-08-15 | **All refreshes share one in-flight promise.** The backend rotates refresh tokens and treats a spent one as theft, revoking the whole family. Two panels expiring together would each call `/auth/refresh`, the second still carrying the token the first had just spent, and the user would be signed out of every device for doing nothing but loading a page with two panels on it. One shared promise means exactly one request reaches the server however many were waiting. It also makes React StrictMode's deliberate double-invocation harmless for free. |
| 2026-08-15 | Refresh is attempted **once** and the request retried **once**, and never at all for paths under `/api/v1/auth/`. A 401 from `/auth/login` means the password was wrong, not that a token expired; refreshing there would be nonsense and would spend the 60-per-hour budget. A second refresh would be arguing with a server that has already refused the first. |
| 2026-08-15 | **Authentication status has three values, and the provider does not withhold its children.** `unknown` covers the gap between load and the silent refresh answering; collapsing it into `anonymous` would show a signed-in person the signed-out view and correct itself a moment later. Blanking the tree during that moment was rejected as the opposite mistake — the public pages are perfectly renderable, so it would trade a brief wrong view for a brief empty one. Gating belongs to the routes that need protecting, which is Phase 6. |
| 2026-08-15 | **Server-side field errors get generic wording; precision belongs to client-side validation.** The backend sends `{field, constraint}` with no parameters, so `Size` arrives without its bounds. Rather than invent a per-field override table, the registration form in 5.4 will enforce and explain the rule before the request is sent — which it must know anyway to validate — and the server's `fieldErrors` become the backstop for a stale client or a direct API call. **Accepted duplication:** the numeric bounds will exist in both halves with nothing linking them. Changing a bound on the backend means changing a form rule by hand. Adding the constraint's parameters to `ApiErrorResponse` would remove the duplication and was rejected as a contract change for messages users should rarely see; **trigger to revisit: evidence that server-side field errors are commonly reaching users.** |
| 2026-08-15 | **Forms are hand-written; no form library was added.** Specification section 3 lists the frontend stack as React, TypeScript, Vite, React Router and a custom CSS/design system — no `react-hook-form`, no `zod` — so adding one would be a stack change needing approval, exactly as Bucket4j would have been on the backend. Five forms of one to three fields share a rule table, an error component and a submission hook, about sixty lines in total. **Trigger to revisit: Phase 8's registration certificate**, which has many fields and OCR review states; that is the moment to propose a library with a real case in hand rather than on a hunch. |
| 2026-08-15 | **One rule table per form drives both client validation and server field errors.** This supersedes the plan recorded earlier the same day, which was generic per-constraint wording for anything the server reported. The form already knows the password minimum — it must, to validate before sending — so when the backend answers `{field: "password", constraint: "Size"}` the form looks up its own rule and reuses that exact message. One wording per problem instead of two, and no second family of translation keys. When two rules share a constraint, the one the submitted value actually breaks is chosen; an unrecognised constraint falls back to a generic message. |
| 2026-08-15 | **`TextField` exists so the accessibility wiring exists once.** Label association, `aria-invalid`, `aria-describedby` and a generated id are the parts no linter is checking — `eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y` still cannot be installed — so repeating them across five forms would have been five chances to get one subtly wrong. `LoginPage` was rewritten onto it in the same step as the new screens, deliberately, so the codebase never carried two patterns for the same thing. |
| 2026-08-15 | **The email travels between screens in route state, never in a query parameter.** An address in a URL lands in browser history and in the access log of every proxy on the path. Route state is invisible to both — and lost on reload, which is why **every screen that receives one still renders an ordinary editable field**, pre-filled only when the address is known. The handover is a convenience, never the only way in. |
| 2026-08-15 | **Signing in lands on `/`, and the welcome screen changes with the authentication status.** No placeholder dashboard route was invented: Phase 6 will replace the authenticated half of that screen, not the route. This is also where the three-state status earns itself — while the silent refresh is in flight the page offers neither a way in nor a greeting, because showing "sign in" for that moment would tell a signed-in person they are not. |
| 2026-08-15 | Every form carries `noValidate`. The browser's own validation messages are written in **the browser's** language, not the application's, so leaving them on would put untranslated text beside translated text on the same form — a direct contradiction of section 6. |
| 2026-08-15 | **A `<p>` nested inside a `<p>` shipped through the type checker and the whole test suite.** The field group was a paragraph and `FieldMessage` renders one; a browser closes the outer paragraph the moment it meets the inner, so the rendered structure stops matching the source. jsdom tolerates it and only React's development warning said anything. Fixed to a `div`. **The lesson is not about paragraphs**: HTML validity is a class of defect this suite cannot catch, and the answer is to look at real pages in a real browser at the end of each screen-building step. |
| 2026-08-15 | Validation messages are phrased as labels — "Lungime minimă: {{min}} caractere" — rather than sentences. Romanian requires "de" before a noun after twenty and above, so "12 caractere" but "120 de caractere", and plain interpolation cannot choose between them. i18next can, through plural forms, and that is worth doing when real copy is written; until then this phrasing is correct for every number instead of wrong for some. |
| 2026-08-15 | **Walking the real flow in a browser found a defect that seventy-nine tests did not.** Registration, verification, sign-in and profile load were driven end to end against the running backend: every endpoint answered, every preflight passed, the six-digit code arrived in the log with its leading zeros intact, and the welcome screen greeted the account by name. It also showed that `LoginPage` ignored the address `VerifyEmailPage` and `ResetPasswordPage` hand it, so somebody who had just proved an address had to type it again. **No test caught it because no test walked two screens in a row** — each rendered a single screen with its route state supplied directly, which is exactly the shape that hides a handover living *between* screens. Fixed and covered. Keep the browser walkthrough at the end of every screen-building step; it is not ceremony. |
| 2026-08-15 | **A refresh that never answered would have wedged the application for the life of the page.** `refresh.ts` keeps one in-flight promise so concurrent callers share it — the whole defence against the backend revoking a token family. But a request that is swallowed rather than refused never settles, `.finally()` never runs, and the slot is never freed: every later expiry would await a promise that cannot resolve, and the session would look frozen rather than expired. Fixed with `AbortSignal.timeout` at twenty seconds — generous because Neon cold starts are slow, the point being that the wait is bounded at all. Scoped to refresh deliberately: one stuck request there disables refreshing for everything, while any other request failing affects only itself. **Found because a test that stubbed a never-settling fetch leaked into the tests after it** — the same shape, at test scale. Tests that hold a request open now release it before finishing. |
| 2026-08-15 | **Phase 6 touches no backend at all.** `GET /api/v1/dashboard` and `GET /api/v1/vehicles` are in the approved contract but belong to Phase 7, and the `vehicles` table does not exist yet. A stub endpoint answering an empty list would be a lie Phase 7 then has to undo, so the skeleton stays a skeleton: protected routes, navigation, and screens whose empty states describe having no data rather than fetching none. The 111 backend tests were not touched. |
| 2026-08-15 | **A protected route waits visibly rather than blanking or redirecting.** The silent refresh reaches the backend, which reaches Neon, and `application.yml` already records that Neon scales to zero and cold starts are slow — so the `unknown` window is seconds, not milliseconds. Rendering nothing would look broken; redirecting to sign-in would tell somebody who is signed in that they are not. `role="status"` so the wait is announced and not merely drawn. |
| 2026-08-15 | Signing in returns the person to the address that turned them away, recorded in route state by `RequireAuth`, and falls back to the dashboard. Without it a link to `/garage` always dumps the recipient somewhere else after signing in, with no explanation. **`returnTo` accepts only values starting with `/`**: without that check, anything able to place a value in route state could redirect a freshly authenticated session to another origin. |
| 2026-08-15 | **Signing out performs no navigation of its own, and the design changed because of it.** The plan said sign-out returns to `/`. Writing it exposed a race: ending the session makes the status anonymous, `RequireAuth` sees that on `/profile` and redirects to sign-in, and a hand-written navigation to `/` arrives after. Doing it in the other order is no better — `WelcomePage` sees a session that still exists and sends the person back to the dashboard. The clean answer was to remove the navigation entirely and let the gate do the departure, which lands on sign-in. Three fewer lines, no race, no flash, and a coherent message: the session ended, here is where to start another. The recorded `from` means signing back in returns to the profile. |
| 2026-08-15 | **`/` is public and `/dashboard` is separate**, rather than one address rendering two different pages by status. It costs a redirect when somebody signed in lands on `/`, and buys routes that can be read, tested and sent to another person as a link. The temporary authenticated branch added to the welcome screen in 5.4b is gone, as planned. |
| 2026-08-15 | **`vehicles` and `registration_certificates` arrive in one migration, and vehicle creation stays inside Phase 7.** Section 9 gives a vehicle no identity of its own — the certificate owns the registration number, VIN, make and commercial description, and forbids duplicating them onto the vehicle for convenience. A vehicle without a certificate is therefore a row nobody can name, which is why the contract specifies `POST /api/v1/vehicles` as creating both atomically and why creation could not be cleanly deferred to Phase 8. The boundary that *was* available is a principled one: **the schema declares exactly four certificate columns NOT NULL**, so Phase 7 asks for those four and Phase 8 adds the remaining thirty optional ones with the field policy in section 8. Flagged as an interpretation of the phase order rather than a contradiction of it, and approved. |
| 2026-08-15 | **`user_id` is denormalised onto `registration_certificates` so `UNIQUE (user_id, vin)` can exist.** Section 9 requires that one account cannot hold the same VIN twice while different accounts may — a constraint spanning two tables, which PostgreSQL cannot index. The alternative was a check in the service, which two simultaneous requests can both pass; registration already learned that lesson and lets `ux_users_email` be the arbiter. The duplication cannot drift because **V1 implements no ownership transfer**, so the value never changes for a given row. Note what is duplicated: the *owner*, not the identity. The VIN stays in one place, which matters because a VIN can be corrected later and an owner cannot. **A deviation from the section 10.3 data dictionary, surfaced and approved rather than decided alone.** |
| 2026-08-15 | **Ownership is a `where` clause, not a check that follows one.** `VehicleRepository` exposes no `findById`; both queries take the account id and match it in SQL. Section 15 says knowing a UUID must never be enough to reach another account's data, and the surest way to honour that across a codebase that will grow many vehicle endpoints is to make the unfiltered read unavailable — a future endpoint cannot forget a check that has no unguarded method to call. |
| 2026-08-15 | **A vehicle somebody else owns answers 404, not 403.** `VEHICLE_ACCESS_DENIED` exists in the section 17 catalogue and was rejected here: a refusal that admits the vehicle is real turns the endpoint into a way of discovering other people's vehicles one identifier at a time, which is exactly what section 15 is guarding. The code stays in the catalogue unused — V1 has no shared ownership to give it a legitimate use. The test asserts the two cases **together**, because the property is that they are indistinguishable and two separate tests would each keep passing while the pair quietly diverged. |
| 2026-08-15 | **`GET /api/v1/dashboard` is deferred to Phase 10.** It is a projection, and the projection worth computing on the server is about documents that expire — which do not exist until Phase 10. Building it now would mean an endpoint that returns a vehicle count, and the garage list already carries that. Same reasoning that kept Phase 6 from stubbing endpoints: an answer invented early is one a later phase has to undo. |
| 2026-08-15 | **The vehicle and its certificate are written in one transaction, and the vehicle is flushed first.** Atomicity is what the contract asks for and what section 9 makes necessary — a vehicle whose certificate insert failed would be a row nobody could name and nobody could find. The flush is a subtler point: the two entities have no mapped association, so **Hibernate has nothing telling it which insert depends on the other** and orders them by entity type, which could put the certificate ahead of the vehicle it points at and break the foreign key. Writing the vehicle explicitly removes the question rather than relying on an ordering that happens to work. |
| 2026-08-15 | **A duplicate VIN is refused twice: by a check, and by the unique index.** The check gives the ordinary case a clean `VEHICLE_DUPLICATE_VIN` instead of a constraint violation surfacing as an internal error; the index catches two simultaneous requests that both passed the check. Exactly the arrangement registration uses around `ux_users_email`. Neither alone is enough — the check is a race and the index alone is a bad error message. **The catch block is untestable by construction**: two concurrent transactions cannot exist inside a `@Transactional` test, so it rests on the same reasoning as its counterpart in registration rather than on a test of its own. |
| 2026-08-15 | **VIN is normalised before it is stored: uppercased, with all whitespace removed.** The unique index compares bytes, so without this one lower-case letter puts the same car in the same garage twice and section 9's rule holds only for people who type consistently. Removing spaces is safe because a VIN contains none by definition. Registration numbers are uppercased and their repeated spaces collapsed — they are uppercase by law, and the garage is **ordered by that column**, so leaving the case alone would sort a row away from its neighbours for no visible reason. Make and description are trimmed only: "Dacia" is not "DACIA". |
| 2026-08-15 | **The VIN's seventeen characters are not enforced.** Section 13 places semantic validation of the VIN inside the OCR review arriving in Phase 9, and refusing a short VIN typed by hand would lock out the vehicles that legitimately have one — anything built before the standard, and some special categories. Presence and a maximum length are checked; the maximum equals the column width, so nothing is truncated silently. **Trigger: Phase 9, where the rule will exist anyway and can be applied to both entry paths at once.** |
| 2026-08-15 | **`POST /api/v1/vehicles` answers 201 with the vehicle in the body and no `Location` header.** A browser cannot read `Location` cross-origin unless it is added to the CORS exposed headers, so shipping one would mean extra configuration carrying a value the body already has and the client already parses. |
| 2026-08-15 | **A nickname is cleared by sending an empty string, not by sending null.** The PATCH convention in this API is that an absent field is left alone, which leaves an optional value with no way to be removed. Overloading null would have broken the convention for every other endpoint; an empty string is explicit, testable, and reads the same way in the request as it does on screen. |
| 2026-08-15 | **A sixth wrong specification citation, caught before it was committed.** `CreateVehicleRequest` cited section 22 for semantic VIN validation; it is section **13, OCR design**. Found by reading the heading above the sentence in the extracted specification rather than trusting the note that recorded it. The section 8 citation in the same file was checked at the same time and is correct — and it turned out to state the phase boundary outright: "Minimum required fields to save a vehicle: A registration number, D.1 make, D.3 commercial description/model, E VIN", which had been inferred from the NOT NULL columns before the sentence was found. **Both halves of that check matter**: one citation was wrong, one was right, and only reading decides which. |
| 2026-08-15 | **The certificate entity maps four of thirty-five columns.** Hibernate's `validate` requires every mapped property to have a column, not every column to have a property, so the unmapped thirty are legal — and all of them are nullable, so Phase 7.2 can insert a row with only the four. The alternative, mapping all thirty-five now, would have put a three-hundred-line entity through a paste channel that has lost files twice in this project, to serve a phase that is not writing them yet. |
| 2026-08-15 | **No data-fetching library; `useResource` is thirty lines.** Section 3 lists the frontend stack without one, so adding TanStack Query would be a stack change needing approval — the same reasoning that kept the forms hand-written and Bucket4j off the backend. Three screens with one GET each do not carry it. **Trigger to revisit: Phase 10**, where deleting a document has to refresh a list on a different screen. That is the problem a cache solves and this hook does not, and it is a real case rather than a hunch. |
| 2026-08-15 | **The hook's dependency is a path, not a fetching function.** A hook taking `() => apiFetch(...)` re-runs on every render, because the caller builds a new closure each time and the effect sees a changed dependency — the screen reloads itself forever. Storing the answer together with a key identifying the request it answers removes the flag entirely, and the three states become mutually exclusive by construction rather than by the screen checking three conditions at once. **The simpler design came out of the lint failure, not despite it.** One casualty: the first version kept old data visible during a reload, and this one does not. Four named blocks in two 130-line locale files met that; anything that says "the second" or "lines 22–24" still does not. | 2026-08-16 | **The OCR allowance is metered in the database; it is not the rate limiter.** It was tempting to call section 13's "ten a day, thirty a month" two more `RateLimitProperties` policies, and that would have been wrong. `InMemoryRateLimiter` exists to make guessing expensive, and a restart forgiving somebody their failed logins costs nothing. **Each OCR request costs money at a provider**, so a restart handing every account a fresh budget is a bill, not a courtesy — and a monthly window in a `ConcurrentHashMap` would mean a process that has to live a month to count correctly and a map that grows with every user who ever appears. Hence `V5`, one row per account per day, with the month as the sum of its days so there is no second figure to keep in step. **Partly corrected on 2026-08-17 once the real price was known — see that day's entry. | 2026-08-16 | **The quota is read under a pessimistic lock, and today's row is created with `on conflict do nothing`.** Two requests arriving together would otherwise both read nine and both write ten, or both insert the first row of the day and have one fail on the unique index — turning an ordinary request into an internal error. Contention is only ever with the same account, so the lock costs nothing in practice. |

Section 13 promises somebody ten scans a day, and the day meant is the one they are living in; in UTC their allowance would reset at three in the morning local time and anybody scanning at one would spend tomorrow's without being told. `OcrQuota.ALLOWANCE_ZONE` is therefore `Europe/Bucharest`, fixed to the audience rather than read from `users.timezone` — which would be more correct, costs a lookup per scan, and hands somebody a second allowance by changing their zone. **Phase 11 schedules reminders per account and has to answer this properly; that constant is what it replaces.** |

Two of the failures were exact inversions of each other, which is the signature. Fixed structurally rather than by patching the tests: `OcrQuota.allowanceDay(clock)` is the one definition and both sides call it, so they cannot disagree again. **Re-run inside the broken window (01:30) to prove it, rather than waiting for a comfortable hour.** |

Two rules keep it a plate. **A word sitting inside another code's value box is that value, not a code** — checked against the same corridor the join uses, and no genuine code among the twenty-seven measured had another cell's content within 0.035 to its left. The gaps separate the two cases cleanly — words printed touching sit 0.001 apart or overlap, words printed apart sit 0.003 to 0.006 — so `SPACE_GAP` is 0.002 and the reconstruction reproduced the printed text on all six multi-word samples. The least-calibrated constant in the mapper, and the cheapest to be wrong about: it decides how a proposal *looks*, never whether it is right. |

What is *not* deferred is the classification: the token is absent from `DeviceView`, absent from `toString`, and written to logs only as its last six characters. |

Implemented as one check and tested from each of the three directions, because a reader will come looking for three rules. |
| 2026-08-17 | **`reminder` and `vehicledocument` depend on each other, and the cycle was chosen rather than tolerated.** A document's period decides its reminders and a preference change reconciles them, so the coupling is real in both directions. A Spring application event would have removed the arrow from a diagram while adding transaction phases, listener ordering, and an indirection that makes "what happens when I save a document" unanswerable by reading the method that saves it. Java has no objection to a package cycle. **The constructor cycle it produced is real**, though — `ReminderService` needs the preferences service and the preferences service needs it back — and is broken at exactly one point with an `ApplicationContext` lookup, rather than by threading a bus through the whole design to avoid admitting the two call each other. |

A renewal is the one case that cancels **without** regenerating: `reconcile` would cancel the superseded record's reminders and immediately schedule the identical set again, when the point is that its expiry has been replaced. Correction reconciles unconditionally rather than only when the date moved — a no-op correction costs six rows rewritten, while a date that moved by a day past code that decided it had not is a reminder nobody ever gets. |

The delivery row is written **before** the provider is called and found again on the retry, so a phone that already has the notification is skipped rather than sent to twice. Delete writes row → object, so a failed object delete leaves the row saying "no photograph", which is what was asked for. Each ordering is wrong for the other's case. The read deliberately **does not repair** a drifted row: it logs and answers 404, because a GET that quietly writes is a surprise waiting for whoever debugs it later. |

The correlation id travels in the MDC, which the base file renders into a `[...]` prefix and the structured format emits as fields instead — same value, still the thread tying an error screen to a log line to a Sentry event. Section 27 forbids exactly that: "expected 4xx business errors are not treated as crashes". Moving it to `exception-resolver-order: 2147483647` makes it inert, because `GlobalExceptionHandler` declares `@ExceptionHandler(Exception.class)` and the resolver chain always stops first. **The filter is then the log level itself**, which `GlobalExceptionHandler` has divided since phase 3 - business failures at INFO, unhandled at ERROR - and whose javadoc said at the time that this was what Sentry would pick up. Since the appender is the entire reporting mechanism, omitting it would have produced the quietest possible failure: the SDK initialises, the configuration looks right, no error appears anywhere, and not one event is ever sent. Verified with `mvnw dependency:tree` rather than assumed, after Sentry's own documentation declined to say. **The same check disproved a worry raised while planning**: no OpenTelemetry artifact is pulled in at all, so the version collision with Spring Boot's managed `opentelemetry.version` cannot happen, and the pre-emptive BOM import that was considered would have been a fix for nothing. Merging them into one line is possible and fragile - it has to be redone correctly every time either provider changes. A separate sending subdomain sidesteps the collision entirely and is the better practice regardless: the reputation of transactional mail stays separate from ordinary correspondence, so a verification code marked as spam cannot damage the address a person actually writes from. |

Both ways of getting it wrong are quiet: coverage reports 0% everywhere while every test passes, or Mockito's agent vanishes. Settled by redirecting JaCoCo to `propertyName=jacocoArgLine` and naming both in Surefire's line. **The syntax is `@{jacocoArgLine}`, not `${...}`** — `${...}` is interpolated when the POM is read, before any plugin has run, so it would resolve to the empty default and the agent would never attach; `@{...}` is Surefire's late replacement, evaluated when Surefire executes, after `prepare-agent` has run in `initialize`. An empty `<jacocoArgLine/>` property is declared as a default so a `-Djacoco.skip=true` build does not hand the JVM the literal text. |

| 2026-08-23 | **Transactional email is plain text, and the reason is injection rather than taste.** The email-change message has to name the address somebody asked to move the account to — that is the whole point of sending it to the *old* inbox, so a person who did not request the change can recognise it. But that value is chosen by whoever made the request, which in the case the message exists to catch is an attacker. In HTML it is an injection point inside somebody else's mail client; in plain text it is text. Everything else follows for free: no images, no tracking pixel, no external stylesheet, nothing that renders differently across clients and nothing that can break. **The stated validity is read from `AuthProperties` rather than written into the sentence** for a related reason — a template saying "15 minutes" beside a setting saying thirty is a lie told to every user, and no test that reads the same template would ever catch it. |

Same argument as `jwt.secret` having no default: **a missing secret must be a refusal to start, never a silently weak one.** The send failure itself is deliberately *not* caught, which is the mirror image — registration sends inside its transaction, so the exception rolls the account back instead of leaving somebody holding an account they can never verify, and it reaches Sentry as the outage it is. |
| 2026-08-23 | **Push tokens are encrypted and retrievable, which is the exact opposite of how refresh tokens are handled two packages away.** Section 10.7 is unusual and worth restating: *"user_devices must retain a retrievable token value … not a one-way hash only"*. FCM needs the value to send anything, so a digest would make the column useless; a refresh token is only ever compared, so a digest is all it gets. AES-256-GCM with a fresh IV per encryption, and **the randomised IV is what forced the rest of the design** — two encryptions of one token differ, so the row can no longer be found by its token and a unique index on the column could never collide, which would let one handset register a thousand times. `push_token_hash`, a SHA-256 blind index, restores both. **That requirement was diagnosed before any crypto was written**, by reading `findByPushToken` and `ux_user_devices_token` first; discovering it afterwards would have meant a second migration. Done at phase 16 rather than the scheduled 17 for one reason: the table is still empty, so the migration could delete every row instead of re-encrypting them. |

A location hint places the data in the Union without touching the endpoint. **The trade is stated rather than hidden**: a hint is placement, not a contractual guarantee, which is defensible for a personal vehicle-management application and would not be for a regulated one. | 2026-08-23 | **A migration wrote `CHAR(64)` for a SHA-256 hex string, and the application would not start at all.** The reasoning behind `CHAR` was sound — the value is *always* exactly sixty-four characters, so the fixed width is the more precise declaration. With `ddl-auto: validate` that is not a warning: **226 test errors, every one of them `ApplicationContext failure threshold (1) exceeded`, from one SessionFactory that never built.** Two things are worth keeping. First, the shape: any backend failure counted in the hundreds is one root cause, the console shows only the cascade, and the answer is always the first `Caused by` in `target/surefire-reports/*.txt`. Second, the fix chose `VARCHAR` over annotating the entity with `columnDefinition`, because `varchar` is what every other text column in this schema uses and `bpchar`'s blank-stripping comparison is a trap on a column that exists to be looked up. |

Overlapping hit areas never fail visibly; they hand the tap to whichever element paints last. The three pixels were the line-box leading that used to sit above and below a 21-pixel inline box and is absorbed once the box fills the line. Accepted with the reason stated rather than waved through — 16 pixels between two 44-pixel targets is comfortable — but a prediction that misses is worth recording, because the next one is trusted on this one's record. 245 tests in 45 files, `tsc` and `lint` silent. |

| 2026-08-25 | **The rotation window a native client has and a browser does not, and why it was accepted rather than closed.** The backend rotates the refresh token on every renewal and `RefreshTokenService.rotate` revokes the entire family when a spent one reappears — a defence that costs nothing on the web, where the browser replaces the cookie atomically from `Set-Cookie`. A native client writes the new token itself, so a process killed between receiving it and storing it comes back holding a spent token and signs the account out of **every** device, having done nothing wrong. Three shapes were weighed. **A grace period on the backend** — accept the immediately previous token for a few seconds and hand back the same successor — closes the window completely and was rejected: it is a change to the security-critical path for a failure nobody has seen, which is the same reasoning that has kept `AccessDeniedHandler` deferred through four phases. **Writing before the request** was rejected outright because it does not work: the token needed is the new one, and in the failure case it never arrived. What was taken is **write before use** — persist, then publish the access token — which shrinks the window to the width of one storage write and is expressible as a test. `SessionChannel.remember` carries the reasoning and `nativeSession.test.ts` asserts the ordering through the token store's own subscription rather than by spying, because that subscription is the moment the new session actually becomes usable to the rest of the application. **Trigger to revisit: the first support report of a sign-out nobody asked for.** |
