| 2026-09-01 | **`FirebasePushNotificationProvider`, and the whole of it worth reviewing is the error mapping.** `firebase-admin` 9.9.0, pinned like the Document AI library and for the same reason — no Google BOM here and exactly one artifact needed. Sending is four lines. Deciding what a refusal *means* is the part with a cost in both directions, which `PushNotificationProvider`'s own contract spells out: a dead handset reported as transient buys retries for ever, and an outage reported as a dead handset unregisters a phone that was working ten seconds ago. **`UNREGISTERED` and `SENDER_ID_MISMATCH` are the only two treated as a dead device.** `INVALID_ARGUMENT` is deliberately not among them, and it is the one that looks like it belongs: FCM returns it for a malformed token, which is permanent, *and* for a payload it will not take, which is our bug — listing it would unregister a working phone every time somebody shipped a bad notification. Left transient it costs `maxAttempts` retries and stops, because `ReminderDispatcher` caps them, and that cap is what makes transient the cheaper mistake. **The mapping takes a `MessagingErrorCode` rather than the exception, and that was forced rather than chosen**: every constructor and factory on `FirebaseMessagingException` is package-private, so one cannot be built outside Google's package and the decision could not have been tested as first written. The result is better than the original — a pure function for the one thing that matters. Guarded in both directions, including a test that pins the seven codes the SDK currently defines, so the next upgrade is one failing test and one deliberate decision rather than a new code silently defaulting to "retry a few times and give up". **`PUSH_PROVIDER_UNAVAILABLE` is a new `ErrorCode` with no translation, on purpose**: no HTTP response carries it: it is written to `notification_deliveries.last_error_code`, and its whole value is telling "Firebase refused" apart from the generic `PROVIDER_ERROR`. `errorKey.test.ts` was run and is content, because nothing sends it to a person. The credential arrangement copies Document AI's exactly — empty means application default credentials, a container supplies the key or the application does not start — with two differences worth naming: the scope is `firebase.messaging` rather than `cloud-platform`, because unlike Document AI, Google publishes a narrow one; and there is no project id beside the key, because a service account key names its own project and a second copy could only ever disagree with it. 363 backend tests. | done |
| 2026-08-31 | **The refusing stub outlived the decision it was standing in for, and shipped.** `unchosenSecureStore` existed so a native build with no plugin would fail with a sentence rather than fall back somewhere unsafe. Once `keystoreSecureStore` existed it was dead code that nothing could reach — and the artifact check found "No secure store is configured" in the native bundle, because it was the default value of a parameter and a default expression is not dead until the parameter is required. Making `store` required on `nativeSessionChannel` deletes it and does something better than deleting it: **the rule the stub enforced at runtime — a native channel must have somewhere safe to put the token — is now enforced by the compiler.** Every test already passed a store, so only the two asserting the stub's refusal were removed with it. | done |
| 2026-08-31 | **The path from registration to the first empty screen was walked for the first time, and it found three things.** Recorded here because the walk itself had been outstanding since the empty states were designed on 2026-08-29 from intercepted API responses — the same components and the same branch, but never the real thing. A throwaway account was registered, verified from the code the logging provider writes to the console, and signed in. **The empty dashboard held up exactly as designed**: it says what the screen is for, says there is no vehicle yet, and offers the one available action as a button. What it also had was 252 pixels of nothing below the footer, and the two findings below. **Nothing about the walk was wasted even though the screens were right** — the defects it found were in the frame around them, which is precisely what a walk sees and a component test cannot. | done |
| 2026-08-31 | **A missing comma had disabled two selectors since the day they were written, and a fix was recorded for one of them that never happened.** `main h2 > a` ended without a comma, so it fused with the line beneath into `main h2 > a [data-form-links] > a` — a single compound selector matching nothing in the application. Both entries went inert. Measured on the rendered page: the login screen's form links at **25** and heading links at **27**, where the footer links above the break sat at **44**. **The 2026-08-29 entry in this document claims those heading links were taken "from 27 to 44"** — and 27 is exactly what was still there two days later. The number had been read before the change and recorded as though it had been read after. `git log -S` finds no revision that ever had the comma. Fixed and re-measured: 25→44, 27→44, footer unchanged at 44 as the control that says the rule did not overreach. **The lesson is about the medium, not the typo: CSS fails silently, a selector list swallows a missing separator without a word, and no test in this suite can see a pixel.** Anything claimed about a measurement has to be measured after the change, on the page. | done |
| 2026-08-31 | **The footer floated in the middle of every short screen, and the shortest screens are the ones a new account sees.** Nothing gave the shell a height, so the document ended where the content did: on the first-day dashboard the footer sat at 497 in a 749-tall window, leaving **252 pixels of empty page** beneath it — a third of the screen. `#root` becomes a column flex box at `100dvh` (with `100vh` beneath it as the fallback) and `main` takes the slack with `flex: 1`. **An id selector, which the no-className rule does not cover and should not**: `#root` is provided by `index.html` and rendered by no component, so a stylesheet is the only thing that can reach it, and the rule names a structure rather than an appearance. Verified at both ends — the gap goes to 0 on a short screen, and the landing page still flows past the fold at 1322 with no horizontal scroll. | done |
| 2026-08-31 | **A lost response was being read as token theft, and it signed you out of every device.** Found by accident while walking the registration path: rapid navigation produced two `REFRESH_TOKEN_REUSED` alarms and killed the session. The mechanism is not the tooling. A rotation reaches the server, the response carrying the replacement does not reach the client — an aborted navigation, a dropped mobile connection, two tabs restored at once — and the client then presents a token the server has already spent. Indistinguishable from a replay under the old rule, which revoked the whole family. **Phase 17 makes it more likely, not less**: a WebView suspended mid-flight aborts requests exactly this way. Fixed with a grace window of `15s` in `AuthProperties`, forgiving a replay only when all three hold: the token was spent *by rotation* (`replacedByTokenId` set — a logout or family revocation writes only `revokedAt`, so the distinction was already in the data), inside the window, and its replacement has never itself been used. **The argument that it does not weaken the alarm**: a thief replaying inside the window does get a token, and the victim's next refresh minutes later lands outside it and revokes the family — the theft is still caught, one step later. What is traded is a few seconds in which an attacker who wins a race gets one extra token. **No migration**: `revoked_at` and `replaced_by_token_id` already existed. **Proven in a real browser as well as in the suite**, because the defect was found there and a test can only assert the rule it was written from: two `/auth/refresh` calls fired together on one cookie — the two-tabs case — both answered 200, the server logged `Rotation replacement was never collected`, and the session survived a navigation that had killed it an hour earlier. That log line is at INFO deliberately: a run of them in production means something is dropping responses systematically, and it is the first sign rather than a mysterious report of people being signed out. 353 backend tests. **Corrected an hour later, because the first write-up claimed more than was true.** Two residuals, both stated rather than discovered later. **The window only helps a client that retries promptly** - a lost response followed by a retry minutes later is still outside it, and still an alarm. That is what bit at 21:49, with several minutes of tsc and lint between an aborted navigation and the next one. No window closes the worst version of it either: a tab closed on an aborted refresh signs the person out on their next visit whenever that is, and only storing the successor's plaintext would help, which is the one thing hashing the tokens exists to avoid. **The concurrent case was feared and then measured, and the fear was wrong.** The grace path revokes the successor, so a client that genuinely holds it is left with a dead token - the premise "unused" is not the same as "never delivered". Measured three times, twice with two simultaneous refreshes and once with three, all survived past the window: the grace path does an extra read and write, so its response lands last and the cookie the browser keeps is the live one. A timing property rather than a guarantee, and recorded as such. **The alternative was considered and refused**: leaving the successor live would give a thief and a victim one live token each, neither would ever present a spent one, and reuse detection would be gone for that family. An occasional false alarm is the right price for an alarm that still works. | done |

## Known issues and open decisions

### Deferred work with a scheduled phase

| Item | Phase |
|---|---|
| **The Railway account is on a trial that ends, and nothing anywhere recorded it.** Seen in the dashboard on **2026-08-29**: *"25 days or $4.13 left — Upgrade to keep your services online."* Whichever runs out first, **`api.cyber-half.com` stops**, which takes the whole application with it since Cloudflare Pages serves a frontend that can then reach nothing. That puts the deadline around **2026-09-23**, and both numbers decay from the day they were read, so treat the date as the softer of the two. Not a code problem and not fixable from here: it needs a plan chosen on the account. **Recorded because it is the one thing that can undo phase 16 without anybody touching the repository**, and because every deployment note in this document was written as though production were permanent. | open |
| ~~**A second Railway project had been failing on every commit since the deployment day.**~~ — **found and deleted 2026-08-29.** Two projects were connected to this repository, not two services in one: `zoological-unity` (`835f31d6…`), which serves `api.cyber-half.com` at 1/1 online, and `proud-dream` (`79f1fa4e…`) at **0/1 — a service that never once came up**. It first reported on the 16.5 deployment day, 2026-08-23, and failed on every commit after it; the likeliest cause is the setting this document already calls the one everything depends on, since without root directory `backend` the build never finds `railway.json` or the `Dockerfile`. **The cost was not the red mark, it was what the red mark destroyed**: the recorded trigger for removing the owner from the branch-protection bypass list is "the first time a red build reaches `main` unnoticed", and nobody can notice a red build on a repository that has been red for six days. Deleted with its GitHub environment, in that order — deleting the environment first only lets the next push recreate it. **The ruleset was checked before deleting and required only the three Actions jobs**, so the failing status had never blocked anything. The Railway GitHub App was deliberately left installed, because production shares that one installation. Commits from `3760cbe` to `0dfd30f` keep the failure permanently: commit statuses are immutable. | done |
| **Standing rule, not a dated task: `/actions/runs` is not "the CI status".** It lists GitHub Actions runs and nothing else. Deployment results from Railway, Cloudflare Pages and anything else arrive as *commit statuses*, which that endpoint never shows — so a repository can report six green Actions runs while the commit itself is red. **This was got wrong on 2026-08-25**, when the Actions API was read, all jobs were green, and the session ended on "CI verde" while `proud-dream` had been failing for two days in a place nobody had looked. The check that answers the question is the combined one: `/repos/{owner}/{repo}/commits/{sha}/status`, which merges every reporter. | standing |
| **Standing rule: `npx tsc --noEmit` type-checks nothing in this repository.** `frontend/tsconfig.json` is a solution file — `"files": []` and three `references` — so the command finds no inputs, exits zero, and says nothing about `src`. The gate is **`npx tsc -b`**, which is what `npm run build` already runs. **This was got wrong on 2026-08-31**: the language switcher was reported as type-clean from `--noEmit`, and VS Code, which resolves the real project references, was showing two errors in the very file at the time. A green check that cannot fail is worse than no check, because it is quoted. | standing |
| **Standing rule: run the frontend suite with `npx vitest run --maxWorkers=4` on this machine.** Unbounded, vitest forks a worker per core and the pool times out starting them — reported as *"Failed to start forks worker"* with several files never running. It looked like flaky tests twice, on 2026-08-29 and 2026-08-31, and both times a re-run was green. Measured on 2026-08-31: **unbounded, 41 of 48 files and 7 pool errors in 168s; capped at four, 48 files and 270 tests in 94s.** The cap is not a workaround that costs speed — it is faster, because the failure is over-subscription rather than the tests. Distinct from the 14.3 `asyncUtilTimeout` change, which was a real timeout inside a running test; if this appears on CI it needs this fix, not that one. **Corrected the same evening: four is not a magic number.** With the backend, an IDE and a dev server running, `--maxWorkers=4` failed the same way — 40 of 48 files, 8 pool errors — and `--maxWorkers=2` was green at 48 files and 272 tests. The rule is therefore *cap it, and lower the cap when workers fail to start*, not *cap it at four*. Anything reporting "Failed to start forks worker" is this, and the answer is always a smaller number rather than a re-run. | standing |
| ~~**RELEASE-BLOCKING: the certificate overlay is invisible, and it is live in production.**~~ — **fixed 2026-08-25, the same hour it was found, and verified by the measurement that found it.** `CertificateOverlay` now sets `color: DOCUMENT_INK` on the field style, a module constant of `#151321`. Re-measured in a real browser against the real template: `CT 10 DOC` at **14.75**, `Chevrolet` at **17.59**, `Aveo` at **15.25**, the VIN at **16.83**, where AA asks 4.5 and all four had been at **1.01**. The spread is the template being paler in some regions than others. **One property fixed two failures**: the scan-state outlines are drawn in `currentColor` and had gone invisible with the text, so section 7's three line styles are back without touching them. **A module constant rather than a token in `:root`, deliberately.** Every colour in that block themes a surface the palette controls; this one paints a photograph of a printed document, which is no more themeable than a scan of a passport. Put among the theme's colours it would invite somebody to make it match them, which is precisely the edit that caused this. **The existing tests passed before and after, unchanged**, which is the point: jsdom has no layout and no image, so no test in the suite could ever have seen this — and that is what a walkthrough is for. 258 tests in 47 files, `tsc` and `lint` silent. Original entry: | ~~17~~ |
| **RELEASE-BLOCKING: the certificate overlay is invisible, and it is live in production.** Found 2026-08-25 by the authenticated walkthrough, measured rather than noticed: the overlay's fields render at a contrast ratio of **1.01:1** against the template behind them, where AA asks 4.5. Not "hard to read" — the values are not there to a reader. Six fields on the walkthrough vehicle carry data, including the registration number, the make, the model and the VIN, and the screen shows a blank certificate. **The cause is exact.** `CertificateOverlay`'s inline styles set `font: inherit` and `background: transparent` and deliberately never set `color`, which was right while the application was light-themed: the field inherited dark text and sat on a pale document. 13.5 inverted the palette, the field inherited `--text` (`rgb(232,230,242)`), and the template it sits on is `rgb(219,234,230)` — a photograph of a real document, which is not part of any theme and never can be. **The lesson is the one this project keeps relearning about artifacts**: a design system built from tokens cannot reach content that is an image, and the one screen drawing on an image was the one screen 13.5's walkthrough had not covered. The fix is for the overlay to state its own colour rather than inherit one, because what it draws on is not a surface the theme controls. **It has been in production since 13.5 deployed.** | 17 |
| ~~**The primary navigation fails both contrast and touch, on every authenticated screen, and the link rule of 2026-08-25 missed it.**~~ — **both fixed the same day, and every prediction held.** Contrast **4.28 → 13.74** by giving `nav a` its own `--text` rather than inheriting `--brand-bright`, which passes on the page background and fails on the raised surface `nav` paints itself with. Touch: the rule grew to `footer > a, nav a, main p > a, main li > a, main h2 > a`, taking the navigation to 44, the vehicle links from 21 and 27 to 44, and the history screen's document links from 27 to 44. The checkboxes went 18→**24**, the smallest a control may be under WCAG 2.2, with the comfortable 44 coming from the label beside them — `input[type='checkbox'] + label` is now `inline-flex` at `--touch`, and clicking a label activates its control, so that is the real target. **Two honest remainders, named rather than hidden.** The shortest labels are still under 44 in *width* — "Home" at 43, "ITP" at 30 — and forcing a minimum width would space the navigation oddly for a pixel; height is what a thumb misses. And several `label` elements measure 25 tall, which looks like a finding and is not: they label inputs that are themselves 44, and activating one is a convenience rather than a primary target. **The public screens were re-measured and are byte-identical** — wordmark 63×57, footer links 132×44 and 83×44, zero contrast failures — which is what says the rule did not overreach. 258 tests in 47 files, `tsc` and `lint` silent. Original entry: | ~~17~~ |
| **The primary navigation fails both contrast and touch, on every authenticated screen, and the link rule of 2026-08-25 missed it.** `Acasă`, `Garaj` and `Profil` measure **4.28:1** where AA asks 4.5, and **41×25, 37×25, 37×25** pixels where the comfortable minimum is 44. The contrast diagnosis is precise and pretty: the links use `--brand-bright`, which passes on the page background `--surface-sunken` and fails on the raised `--surface` the header sits on — one colour, two backgrounds, and only one of them had ever been measured. The touch failure is the earlier fix being incomplete: `footer > a` and `main p > a` got `--touch`, and the header was excluded on the reasoning that its wordmark already measured 57 pixels. **That reasoning was drawn from the public screens, where the header holds nothing but the wordmark.** The navigation only exists when signed in, so the walkthrough that would have shown it had not been done. Also under 44 and found in the same pass: vehicle links at 21 and 27 pixels tall, the history screen's document links at 30×27 and 39×27, and **the profile's seven notification checkboxes at 18×18**. | 17 |
| ~~**Not a defect, and the third casualty of the machine move: `backend/storage/` did not travel.**~~ — **closed 2026-08-31 on the walkthrough account.** A photograph uploaded to the vehicle created during the registration walkthrough came back and rendered: a `blob:` object URL, `naturalWidth` 1197 by 759, `complete` true, and no error alert on the screen. That is the whole round trip — the fetch carrying the `Authorization` header, the object URL built from the response, and the browser decoding it — and it is the one path no test in either suite can reach, because jsdom provides neither `createObjectURL` nor an image decoder. Verified on this machine for the first time since the move. Original entry: | ~~open~~ |
| **Not a defect, and the third casualty of the machine move: `backend/storage/` did not travel.** The walkthrough vehicle's photograph answers `RESOURCE_NOT_FOUND`, shown as "Resursa nu a fost găsită." The Neon `development` branch still holds the row and its `image_object_key`; the object itself was written to a gitignored directory on `D:\Learning\Claude_Space` and exists only there. Nothing is broken — but **the photograph round trip cannot be verified on this machine until a fresh one is uploaded**, and that round trip is the one thing no test can reach, since it goes through an `Authorization` header and an object URL that jsdom cannot provide. After `application-local.yml` and the specification path, this is the third thing the move left behind, and all three were invisible until something asked for them. | open |
| ~~**The 5 MB vehicle-image limit is reachable today, on mobile web, and nothing downscales anything.**~~ — **closed 2026-08-29 by `images/shrink.ts`, and the shape of it was decided by the certificate rather than by the photograph.** `withinCeiling(file, ceiling)` re-encodes **only when the file exceeds what the server would take**, so anything that was going to be accepted is sent byte-for-byte. That rule is not caution, it is the scan: Document AI cannot read the `I` family today and the recorded trigger for revisiting it is a photograph at *higher* resolution, so shrinking a scan the server would have accepted would close off the only thing that could ever settle it. **Best effort, never a gate** — no `createImageBitmap`, a decoder that refuses, an encoder that returns nothing, or a shrink that would breach the server's own `min-side`, and the original goes as it always did; the server stays the enforcer and answers with a translated code. EXIF orientation is honoured through `imageOrientation: 'from-image'`, without which a phone photograph reaches the OCR rotated ninety degrees. Wired into `scanCertificate` and `uploadVehicleImage`, so every caller is covered by one change. **The ceilings are copies of numbers the backend owns**, which is a second source of truth stated rather than hidden — safe in one direction only, since drifting below what the server allows costs nothing and drifting above merely restores today's honest rejection. 268 tests in 48 files. | ~~open~~ |
| **The 5 MB vehicle-image limit is reachable today, on mobile web, and nothing downscales anything.** Found on 2026-08-25 while deciding the camera question, and separated from it because it is not a phase-17 issue: `grep` finds no canvas, no `createImageBitmap`, no `toBlob` anywhere in `src` — the browser sends whatever file was chosen, unchanged. It does not bite yet because people pick existing pictures, which are usually already compressed, and when it does bite it fails honestly with `IMAGE_TOO_LARGE` translated in both locales. It becomes routine the moment somebody photographs their car from a phone. **The fix is a decision rather than a patch**: downscale on the client before upload, which is a few lines of canvas and applies to the web as much as to the native application, or raise the limit, which costs R2 storage for pictures nobody looks at closely. Deliberately not bundled into the camera decision, because that one waits for Capacitor and this one does not. **Trigger: the first `IMAGE_TOO_LARGE` a real person meets**, or the first photograph taken through the native camera path. | open |

**The first seven rows below came from one pass on 2026-08-25**, read off the code
rather than off this document, asking a single question: what would have to be
undone if phase 17 were started today. They are ordered by how expensive the
undoing would be, not by severity. None of them is a defect in what exists — six
are decisions nobody has had to take yet, and taking them on paper before the
Capacitor project exists is the whole point of the pass.

| Item | Phase |
|---|---|
| ~~**The session model is a browser's, and the Android app cannot use it.**~~ — **the seam landed on 2026-08-25, the plugin did not, and that split was the point.** `api/session/` now holds `SessionChannel` (the interface), `cookieChannel`, `nativeChannel`, `secureStore` and `channel` (the build-time selection); `refresh.ts` and `endpoints/auth.ts` route through it. **Selection is by `import.meta.env.VITE_CLIENT`, not by `Capacitor.isNativePlatform()`, and the deciding argument was the plugin rather than the style**: secure-storage plugins commonly ship a web shim that falls back to `localStorage`, which is the one place this application has decided a credential may never sit. Choosing at build time means the branch not taken is dead code — **verified on the artifact with a positive control**, which is the only version of that check worth running: `Keystore`, `Keychain`, `unchosenSecureStore` and the stub's refusal message are all absent from `dist/`, while `refreshTokenInBody` is present, proving the search was reading the bundle rather than finding nothing. **What remains for 17**: the plugin itself behind `SecureStore` (section 35's deferred choice — Keystore/Keychain backed, never SharedPreferences), and `.env.native` carrying `VITE_CLIENT=native` plus the production API origin, which needs its own `.gitignore` exemption and its own row in `productionEnv.test.ts` or the exemption is unguarded. 258 tests in 47 files. Original entry: | ~~17~~ |
| **The session model is a browser's, and the Android app cannot use it.** `tokenStore.ts` keeps the access token in a module variable and `refresh.ts` sends `body: '{}'` — the cookie channel, with its own comment saying *"Sending a token here would be the native-client path"*. That path does not exist. On Android the cookie is dead by construction: it is `SameSite=Strict`, the WebView's origin is `https://localhost` and the API is `api.cyber-half.com`, which are different **sites**, so it is never attached. **The user would be signed out every time the application is closed.** The backend half has been ready since 4.5b and is correct — `AuthController.refresh` returns the rotated token in the body when the body channel was used, and `LoginRequest.refreshTokenInBody` exists — so what is missing is the frontend half plus the secure-storage plugin section 35 defers to "the mobile phase". Planned rather than skipped, but it touches four files at the centre of authentication (`tokenStore.ts`, `refresh.ts`, `client.ts`, `AuthProvider.tsx`) and every other native decision sits downstream of it. **Decide first, before any Capacitor file exists.** | 17 |
| **CORS does not know the Capacitor origin, and the allowlist is also the CSRF defence.** `application-prod.yml` allows exactly one origin, `https://app.cyber-half.com`. The Android WebView asks from `https://localhost`, so **every request is refused at preflight** and the application is dead on first launch. `CorsTest` cannot catch this: it derives the *methods* from Spring's route table, not the origins. The decision is not the one-line edit it looks like, and `SecurityConfig`'s own comment says why — the allowlist is what protects `/auth/refresh` and `/auth/logout`, so an entry added here is never merely a convenience. Two shapes: **(a)** add `https://localhost`, defensible precisely because the native client uses the body channel and never touches the cookie, though the entry applies to all of `/api/**`; **(b)** use `CapacitorHttp`, which bypasses CORS in the native layer — and whose known weak point is exactly what `apiFetchBlob` needs, reading the vehicle photograph as a blob back across the bridge. **Trigger: before the first `cap sync`.** | 17 |
| ~~**A permanently dead FCM token has no way out of `user_devices`.**~~ — **closed 2026-08-25, and the shape it took was decided by a foreign key nobody had looked at.** `PushTokenRejectedException` is the provider's signal, thrown when the platform reports the token permanently invalid; every other refusal stays an `ApiException` and is still retried. `ReminderDispatcher.deliver` catches it ahead of the general clause — the ordering is the feature, since the subclass would otherwise be swallowed as `PROVIDER_ERROR` — and `dispatch` removes the device and excludes it from the retryable count, so a reminder whose only phone is gone now finishes on the first pass as SENT rather than after three attempts as FAILED. That is the "no device is not a failure" rule arriving one pass late, which is why it reuses the same answer rather than inventing one. **Deliberately not an `ErrorCode`**: that enum's own javadoc says every failed API response carries one of its members and the frontend translates it, and this reaches no client — the frontend's coverage test uses a hand-written list, so adding one would have broken nothing mechanically and made the enum's contract false. 344 tests across 48 classes, and the new class is in the jar. | ~~17~~ |
| **A permanently dead FCM token has no way out of `user_devices`.** `ReminderDispatcher.deliver` catches `RuntimeException` and treats everything as transient — retried to `maxAttempts`, then FAILED — and the device row survives untouched. FCM answers `UNREGISTERED` when the application has been uninstalled, which is permanent by definition. If that is the account's only phone, **every future reminder pays three attempts and ends FAILED, for ever.** The fix is small only if it is designed before the provider: `codeOf` already records `apiException.errorCode().name()`, so a new `ErrorCode` for a token the provider has rejected permanently, plus one branch in `deliver` that deletes the row, is the whole of it. Written afterwards it is a rewrite of `FirebasePushNotificationProvider`'s error contract. **Trigger: before `FirebasePushNotificationProvider` is written, not after it is tested.** | 17 |
| ~~**`notifications_enabled` has no writer.**~~ — **closed 2026-08-25, and the question turned out to be the wrong one.** It was written up as a missing feature; it is a missing *fact*. V9 calls the column "device level, distinct from the account level switch — one phone silenced is not the account silenced", and V1 has no screen listing devices, so there is nowhere in our interface to silence one. **The operating system is that screen**, and the gap was that the backend never learned what it said. `RegisterDeviceRequest` now carries `notificationsEnabled` as a required field and `UserDeviceService.register` writes it on every launch, which costs no extra request because the native client already registers at every start. **The failure this closes is a lie, not an inconvenience**: a permission revoked in Android's settings months after registration leaves the token perfectly valid, so FCM accepts the message, the dispatcher records the reminder SENT, and the person is shown nothing. Required rather than defaulted, because an optional field falling back to true would reintroduce exactly that silent wrong answer. 348 tests across 48 classes. | ~~17~~ |
| **`notifications_enabled` has no writer.** The column exists on `UserDevice`, defaults to `true`, has a setter, and the only caller in the entire project is a test. `RegisterDeviceRequest` does not carry the field and `DeviceController` offers POST and DELETE and nothing else — so today the flag is write-once-true and `findByUserIdAndNotificationsEnabledTrue` filters on something that never varies. Screen 18 is precisely where "the user refused the operating system's permission" has to land, and with the current API the only two moves are *register* (always enabled) or *delete the device*. Deleting on refusal is defensible and may well be the right answer; what it must not be is the answer nobody chose. Anything finer — off on the phone, on on the tablet — needs a PATCH that does not exist. **Trigger: the moment screen 18 is specified.** | 17 |
| ~~**`/auth/refresh` is rate limited by address alone, and phones share addresses.**~~ — **closed 2026-08-25 by removing the limit, which is what the entry below already proposed.** `AuthRateLimit.tokenRefresh`, the `tokenRefresh` component of `RateLimitProperties` and the `token-refresh` block in both `application.yml` files went together — leaving any one behind would have produced configuration that looks live and binds to nothing, the exact shape that hid three files from a jar in 16.3. `AuthController.refresh` no longer takes an `HttpServletRequest` at all, so nothing can quietly start reading an address again without meeting the comment that says why. **A third option was weighed and refused**: keying on the token's family *after* the lookup would keep a bound and fix CGNAT together, and the map would stay bounded because only real families create keys — but it moves rate limiting into the service layer for a threat nobody has articulated, and the runaway client it would tame is our own application with a bug. **Consequence taken with it:** every remaining caller of `enforce` passes an email, so the `email == null` branch became dead and was removed. 349 tests across 48 classes. | ~~17~~ |
| **`/auth/refresh` is rate limited by address alone, and phones share addresses.** `AuthRateLimit.tokenRefresh` passes a null email, so the only key is `token-refresh:ip:<addr>` at 60 per hour. On the web that was the tolerated NAT problem recorded further down this table. A mobile application sharpens it into a real one: **an entire carrier leaves through a handful of CGNAT addresses**, every launch performs a refresh, and a ten-minute access token means roughly six per hour per active user — so ten people on one carrier exit exhaust the budget and the eleventh is met with `RATE_LIMITED` while restoring a session, which reads as a broken application rather than as a limit. The fallback was already written down when the proxy debt was closed: **drop the per-address limit on `/refresh` alone**, since reuse detection already punishes abuse by revoking the whole family. Phase 17 is where that stops being optional. | 17 |
| ~~**Three file inputs and no camera.**~~ — **implemented 2026-08-31, and half the recorded reasoning had expired by then.** `@capacitor/camera` 8.2.4, behind `images/camera.ts`, chosen at build time so the web bundle carries none of it — verified on the artifact, absent along with `registerPlugin` and `takePhoto`, with a positive control. **The entry below argued the camera on *size*: a file input gives no control over what comes back, a phone photograph is 3–6 MB, and the vehicle upload would start answering `IMAGE_TOO_LARGE`. `images/shrink.ts` landed four days later and answered exactly that, for every source including a file input.** So the size argument was already settled by something else, and the design consequence is that the camera sets **no** `targetWidth` or `targetHeight`: two size limiters would be two places for the rules to disagree, and the one that exists is the one the server's limits were written against. What survives is the plain reason — a camera is a camera, and photographing the certificate in the flow beats photographing it in the phone's camera app and then hunting for it in a gallery. **The API had moved too, which is exactly what section 35's "verify against current documentation" is for**: version 8.1.0 deprecated `getPhoto` and its `CameraSource.Prompt` in favour of separate `takePhoto` and `chooseFromGallery`, so the plugin no longer asks "camera or gallery?" itself and the application asks it — two ordinary buttons in the application's own language, where the old dialog would have needed its labels translated through `promptLabel*`. `PhotoChooser` renders those on native and the unchanged file input everywhere else, which also gave the three screens one copy of the `input.value = ''` reset instead of three — the idiom that lets the same photograph be retried after a failed scan, previously explained on one screen and silent on the other two. `VehicleImage`'s `inputRef` went with it: declared, assigned, never read. Original entry: |
| **Three file inputs and no camera — decided 2026-08-25, implemented when Capacitor exists.** There is no `@capacitor` package in the project yet, so the plugin cannot be installed, and building a seam now would mean refactoring three working pages for a consumer that does not exist. **The decision, so phase 17 looks it up rather than re-deriving it: `@capacitor/camera`, with different limits per path.** The argument is not the camera, it is that a file input gives no control over what comes back while our own limits are tight — 5 MB and 40 MP for a vehicle photograph, 10 MB and 40 MP for a scan. A photograph taken on the spot at full resolution is routinely 3–6 MB, so the vehicle upload would start answering `IMAGE_TOO_LARGE` in exactly the flow being introduced. **The two paths want opposite things and must not share a setting.** The vehicle photograph is decorative and should be constrained well under 5 MB, with the source left to the person since picking an existing picture is the ordinary case. The certificate wants the largest image the 10 MB allows: the `I` family is unreadable to Document AI today and the recorded trigger for revisiting that is "a photograph at higher resolution reading `I` at all", so downscaling the scan would close off the one thing that could ever settle it. **What is cheap and was rejected:** `capture="environment"` on the two scan inputs — two attributes, no dependency, no permission, and it works on mobile web too — but it leaves the size problem entirely unsolved. **Not verified and deliberately so:** the plugin's current API and what it requires in the Android manifest, which is section 35's discipline for plugins. | 17 |
| **Three file inputs and no camera.** `CertificatePage`, `AddVehiclePage` and `VehicleImage` all use `<input type="file" accept="image/jpeg,image/png">` with no `capture`. It works inside a WebView, but it opens a document picker — and the application's headline feature is *photograph the certificate*. With an explicit MIME list rather than `image/*`, Android's chooser is inconsistent about offering the camera at all. Either the input stays and the flow is a picker, or `@capacitor/camera` feeds the same upload path and three pages change. **The manifest side travels with it**: `CAMERA` declared and requested at runtime, and on Android 13+ `POST_NOTIFICATIONS` too — which is screen 18's actual mechanism. | 17 |
| **Four small things that only bite on the first day of 17, recorded so they are not rediscovered one at a time.** (a) The `nativeOnly` string in both locales says push notifications arrive in "the phone application, which does not exist yet" — true today, a lie the day the application ships. (b) **The Android hardware back button does nothing** with `BrowserRouter`; the default behaviour closes the application, and it is the first thing any tester notices. (c) `garajul-meu.push.token-key` has no default, so the backend will not start locally until a passphrase is in the gitignored `application-local.yml` — intended behaviour, and still the thing that stops day one. (d) The bundle handed to `cap sync` must come from `npm run build`; a development build falls back to `http://localhost:8080` and the application looks healthy while nothing answers. | 17 |
| **Not a finding, and the reason it is written here is that it saves a day's work.** The verification and password-reset emails carry **codes, not links** — `EmailMessages` contains no URL of any kind. So phase 17 needs no deep links, no Android App Links, no `assetlinks.json` and no intent filters for the authentication flow. Anybody planning the phase from the specification alone would budget for all four. | 17 |
| ~~CSRF protection for the cookie-authenticated paths~~ — **closed 2026-08-23 by 16.2, and the answer was that it already worked.** Section 14 asks for "strict allowed-origin checks and Spring CSRF protection appropriate for the selected cookie flow"; the origin check is Spring's CORS processor refusing actual requests, now asserted rather than assumed, and it is *the* appropriate protection for this flow because a token across sibling subdomains would have to be readable by all of them. `CorsTest` also derives, from the route table, that nothing reads the refresh cookie on a method browsers send without an `Origin` header — a GET endpoint reading that cookie would be forgeable with neither CORS nor SameSite in the way. See the decisions table. Original entry: | ~~16~~ |
| CSRF token protection for the cookie-authenticated `/auth/refresh` and `/auth/logout` paths, per specification section 14. Deferred deliberately on 2026-08-14, with reasoning in the decisions table: `SameSite=Strict` is the working defence today, and a token needs a real frontend and two real origins to be implemented or tested honestly. | 16 |
| ~~**`index.html` carries Vite's generated defaults into production.**~~ — **closed 2026-08-24 by 13.5.** The title is the product's name, the served default is `lang="ro"`, and `i18n/config.ts` rewrites the attribute on every language change plus once at startup, because the event does not fire for the language `init` began with. Original entry: | ~~13.5~~ |
| ~~**The time-zone select offers every IANA zone.**~~ — **closed 2026-08-24 by 13.5**, with `ComboboxField`. See the decisions table. | ~~13.5~~ |
| **`index.html` carries Vite's generated defaults into production.** `<title>frontend</title>` names the build directory in every browser tab, bookmark and shared link; `<html lang="en">` is wrong for an application that defaults to Romanian, and wrong for a screen reader. The title is a one-word fix. `lang` is a small decision rather than a typo: the application switches RO/EN at runtime, so the honest fix sets `document.documentElement.lang` from the i18n state and uses `ro` as the served default. Found on 2026-08-23 by fetching the deployed page, not by any test. | 13.5 |
| **Standing rule, not a dated task: after any phase that adds a production class, list the jar.** `unzip -l target/backend-0.0.1-SNAPSHOT.jar \| grep garajulmeu/<package>` and count. A green suite proves the *test* classpath works — `target/test-classes` precedes `target/classes`, so a production class applied into `src/test/java` compiles, runs, passes, and is absent from the artifact. That happened on 2026-08-23 with three of 16.3's four files and would have failed the first deploy at boot. The frontend has the same shape of hole and the same shape of check: `find src -type f -size -1c` for the empty files that a green suite cannot see. | standing |
| **Standing rule, not a dated task:** `/api/v1/auth/**` is permitted wholesale, which is correct only while every endpoint under it is pre-authentication. All six are, today. Any endpoint added under that prefix which should require a token must be matched individually — check this at the moment of adding, not later. | standing |
| Registration sends the email inside the transaction, so a provider outage rolls the account back. Simple and safe today; revisit if Resend proves flaky. | 4.7 |
| **After a long outage the scheduler delivers a burst of old reminders.** `findDue` has no lower bound on `scheduled_at`, exactly as section 12 requires — "the scheduler queries overdue PENDING work so a short restart does not permanently lose a reminder". The word is "short" and nothing enforces it: after a week down, an account receives the thirty, fourteen and seven-day reminders together, which is the burst section 12 refuses at *generation* time. Not fixed, because the specification asks for no cutoff and inventing one is a product decision — the shapes available are a maximum lateness, or collapsing a document's overdue set to its most urgent. | open |
| **The dispatcher's transaction boundaries are the one thing no test asserts.** `ReminderDispatcherFlowTest` builds the dispatcher by hand to avoid a fifth container, so there is no proxy and the three `@Transactional` boundaries do not exist inside the test — everything joins the test's transaction and rolls back. The state machine is covered; "the claim is committed before the provider is called" is argued for in the code and verified by nobody. A test that could check it would need a committing test and its own context, which is the trade that was refused. | open |
| ~~**Deleting a vehicle, or an account, leaves its photograph in the bucket forever.**~~ — **closed the same day by 12.3b.** `VehicleService.delete` reads the key before the row goes and `UserService.deleteAccount` collects every key in the garage before the cascade takes them; `VehicleImageService.discard` removes the objects and **never throws**, because an exception there would roll the deletion back and make somebody's right to be forgotten depend on a bucket answering. Both halves are asserted on the store rather than on the row. **The account-level test failed first for a reason worth keeping**: rows removed by a database cascade are invisible to the persistence context, so `findById` answered out of the first-level cache and reported a vehicle that was already gone — the test now clears the EntityManager before reading. The original finding, for the record: found on 2026-08-18 while writing up 12.3, not by a test. `VehicleService.delete` removes the row, and the row is the only thing that knew the object key — `image_object_key` goes with it, so nothing can ever find the object again. Account deletion is the same path one level up: the foreign key cascades `users → vehicles` and takes the keys with it. **The metadata is gone and the bytes are not**, which is the wrong way round: section 24 makes deletion mean the data is removed, and a photograph of somebody's car outliving the account that owned it is exactly what that forbids. Not a leak of anything reachable — the object is unlisted and its key is unrecoverable — but it is still their photograph on our storage after they asked us to forget them. **The fix belongs to whoever holds the transaction**: `VehicleService.delete` must read the key before deleting the row and remove the object after the commit, and `UserService` must do the same for every vehicle in the account. Deliberately not bolted on while writing the summary; it needs its own tests, including the one that proves an object survives a rolled-back delete. | 12.4 |
| ~~**The Cloudflare R2 adapter is blocked on a real bucket**~~ — **written 2026-08-23 by 16.4, and it was never as blocked as this entry claimed.** `CloudflareR2FileStorageProvider` speaks the S3 API through the AWS SDK v2 (2.54.2, pinned — the Spring Boot parent manages no AWS version), and seven tests hold the key handling, the content type and the error mapping. **What a bucket was actually needed for is verification, not construction**, and that distinction was worth four months of not noticing: the entry read as "cannot start" when it meant "cannot finish". The decision recorded in `FileStorageProvider`'s javadoc still stands — reads pass through the backend rather than through signed URLs, so the bucket stays private with no expiry machinery, and a `urlFor` arrives the day traffic argues for it. `application-prod.yml` now sets `provider: r2`. Still unproven until the first deploy: the endpoint, the region and the credentials. | ~~12.4~~ |
| **The Cloudflare R2 adapter is blocked on a real bucket**, exactly as the Google adapter was blocked on GCP billing. `FileStorageProvider` is the seam and `LocalFileStorageProvider` carries the feature meanwhile, so nothing above the interface changes when it arrives. What is needed: an R2 bucket, an access key pair and the account id, and the decision — already argued in `FileStorageProvider`'s javadoc — of whether reads keep passing through the backend or move to signed URLs, which would add a field to `VehicleDetails` rather than change `hasImage`. | 12.4 |
| ~~**Two component tests have been seen to fail on a loaded machine.**~~ — **closed on 2026-08-23**, one day early, after it recurred under `--coverage`. `asyncUtilTimeout` is now 5 s and `testTimeout` 15 s; see the decisions table. Original entry: | ~~14.3~~ |
| **Two component tests have been seen to fail on a loaded machine, and CI is a loaded machine by definition.** On 2026-08-22, with Vitest and Playwright fighting over `journey.spec.ts` and the whole run crawling — 64 s, `LoginPage` alone taking 6.4 s where it normally takes under two — `GaragePage` *lists the vehicles* and `VehicleDetailsPage` *shows the identity* both timed out still showing `Se încarcă…`. Both passed immediately on a clean run, so nothing is broken. **The cause is a default nobody chose:** Testing Library's `findBy*` waits one second, which is tuned for a fast desktop, while Vitest's own `testTimeout` is five. Both failures were the first test in their file, which pays the module-loading cost. A shared CI runner is slower than this machine, so the fix is to raise `asyncUtilTimeout` in `src/test/setup.ts` — these tests assert *eventually*, never *within one second*, and none of them measures performance, so a longer wait weakens no assertion. Deliberately not done as a reflex the moment a test went red; it belongs with CI, where it will actually be needed. | 14.3 |
| **Section 31's last line is now half-enforced, and the half matters.** A branch ruleset named *Require status checks to pass before merging* targets the default branch, is Active, restricts deletions, and requires `Backend tests`, `Frontend tests and build` and `Playwright smoke tests`. **The repository owner is on the bypass list**, chosen deliberately on 2026-08-23 after the alternative was put plainly: without bypass, `git push` to `main` stops working and every phase needs a branch and a pull request, because GitHub demands the checks have passed *for that commit* before it may land — and they only run after a push. **What the ruleset still buys**: `main` cannot be deleted or force-pushed, the three jobs still run and are still visible, and any future contributor is gated by them. **What it does not buy** is the thing the specification's sentence is actually about — red code cannot reach `main` from anybody except the one person who pushes to it, which today is everybody who pushes to it. **Trigger to remove the bypass: a second contributor, or the first time a red build reaches `main` unnoticed.** Original entry: | ~~15~~ |
| **Section 31's last line - "deploy only when required checks pass" - is not enforced by anything yet.** The three jobs exist and report, but nothing stops a merge while they are red: that half is a GitHub branch-protection setting, not a file. After the first green run, `main` needs *Require status checks to pass* with `Backend tests`, `Frontend tests and build` and `Playwright smoke tests` selected by name — which means the job names in `ci.yml` are now load-bearing and renaming one silently drops it from the required set. The deploy half arrives with Railway. | 15 |
| **The time-zone select offers every IANA zone, several hundred of them, starting at `Africa/Abidjan`.** Functionally right - it is exactly what `ZoneId.getAvailableZoneIds()` validates against, and a hand-written short list would be a second source of truth, wrong for anybody abroad and stale the first time the tz database moves. Unusable on a phone, and measurably so: on 2026-08-22 the profile screen's accessibility tree was **30,638 characters**, almost all of it options, which truncated three separate attempts to read the page. The fix is a control, not a list - a filter or a typeahead over the same values - which is why it belongs to the design phase rather than to a patch now. | 13.5 |

Development-only by construction - R2 keys are flat strings and have no directories to leave - so the cost is a tidy-looking folder tree on one machine. Not worth code that only ever runs where it does not matter. | won't fix |
| **The design system, which the specification never scheduled.** Section 3 names "custom CSS/design system" in the stack table and section 30's phases never build it; section 35 defers "Final product name, logo, colours and production domain". What exists is one 1432-byte `index.css` and **zero `className` attributes across sixteen pages** — semantic HTML by choice, with the reasoning in the stylesheet's own header. **Blocked on the branding decision, which is the owner's and nobody else's**; tokens built before it exists would be guesses at what they have to support. The constraint it must not break: colour is never the sole signal — `data-tone`, the scan states' line styles and the nine document sentences all exist so a palette can be added without removing meaning. | 13.5 |
| ~~**Renewal creates the new row but reconciles no reminders**~~ — **closed 2026-08-17 by 11.2.** All three writes reconcile now: `POST` and `PATCH` cancel-and-regenerate, `/renew` cancels the superseded record’s set **without** regenerating it, and a preference change reconciles every document in the garage. Writing the three places down in advance is what made it a three-line change rather than a hunt. | done |
| ~~Auth endpoints are not rate limited~~ — **closed 2026-08-14** by `AuthRateLimit` over `InMemoryRateLimiter`. | done |
| ~~**Release-blocking:** `AuthRateLimit` reads the caller's address from `request.getRemoteAddr()`~~ — **closed 2026-08-23 by 15.2.** `application-prod.yml` sets `server.forward-headers-strategy: framework`, which installs Spring's `ForwardedHeaderFilter` so the request is rewritten from `X-Forwarded-For` before any filter of ours reads it. **The failure it prevents is worse than "the limit is inaccurate"**: behind an unconfigured proxy every visitor on earth shares one address key, so the tenth failed login *anywhere* locks out the eleventh person *everywhere* — a per-address defence turned into a global denial of service. Trusting the header is safe only because the application is reachable solely through Railway's proxy; a second, direct route would make it a header any caller can forge to reset their own budget, and the trust would have to move to a known proxy list. Original entry: | ~~15~~ |
| **Release-blocking, not a refinement:** `AuthRateLimit` reads the caller's address from `request.getRemoteAddr()`. Correct locally; behind Railway's proxy it returns the *proxy's* address for everyone, which turns every per-address limit into a global one — `/refresh` at 60/hour would stop the application for all users within minutes. The fix is `server.forward-headers-strategy` in configuration, **never** hand-parsing `X-Forwarded-For`, which a client can forge and which would make per-address limiting bypassable in one line. Must be configured and verified against Railway's actual header behaviour before production. If that behaviour cannot be established with confidence, the safe fallback is to drop the per-address limit on `/refresh` alone: it costs one SHA-256 lookup, and reuse detection already punishes abuse by revoking the family. | 15 |
| ~~`DELETE /users/me` sends the current password in a **request body on a DELETE**.~~ — **closed 2026-08-23 against the real deployment.** A throwaway account was registered and deleted through `app.cyber-half.com`, so Railway's proxy passes the body through. The concern was legitimate and could only ever be settled here: had the body been stripped, the endpoint would have answered `VALIDATION_ERROR` on a field the client demonstrably sent, which is a confusing failure a long way from its cause. If a CDN is ever put in front of the API, this becomes unproven again. Original entry: | ~~15~~ |
| `DELETE /users/me` sends the current password in a **request body on a DELETE**. Legal per RFC 9110, which defines no semantics for it but does not forbid it, and it works locally through Tomcat. Some proxies and CDNs strip DELETE bodies, and if Railway or Cloudflare does, the endpoint fails in production with a validation error on a field the client did send — a confusing failure far from its cause. **Verify against the real deployment before release.** If the body does not survive, the fallbacks are a dedicated header or `POST /me/delete`; a query parameter is not one, because it would put the password in access logs. | 15 |
| ~~CORS has never been exercised by a real browser~~ — **closed 2026-08-15.** Verified against both servers running: `OPTIONS /api/v1/auth/refresh` answered **200**, the credentialed `POST` reached the backend and answered **401**, and the console showed no CORS error. The 401 is the proof: had CORS been wrong the browser would have blocked the request before sending it, and no status would have come back at all. The same run incidentally confirmed `browserLanguage()` against a real `navigator` — the browser reported `en-US` and the page rendered in English. **Neither `CorsTest` nor any frontend test could have established this**: MockMvc speaks HTTP but enforces no same-origin policy, and the frontend tests replace `fetch` outright. | done |
| ~~**No endpoint under `/users/me` is rate limited**~~ — **closed 2026-08-23 by 14.4.** `AccountRateLimit` keys on the account id rather than the network address, because the caller is authenticated and the token's subject is the strongest identity available: an authenticated caller cannot rotate it the way an anonymous one rotates addresses, so the second key the auth limiter needs buys nothing here and would only reintroduce the NAT problem it tolerates out of necessity. That also bounds the map by the number of real accounts. **The key prefixes differ from the auth limiter's on purpose** — sharing `credential-check:` would let a stranger's failed logins lock the owner out of changing their own password. `change-password`, `confirm-email-change` and `DELETE /me` draw on `credentialCheck`; `change-email` draws on `emailDispatch`, the tighter of the two, because it pays a hash *and* sends mail and the scarce resource is the sending reputation. `GET /me` and `PATCH /me` stay unlimited, and a test asserts it so the next person can tell a decision from an oversight. No new policy was invented: the grouping is by what a request costs, and these cost what the auth endpoints cost. Original entry: | ~~standing~~ |
| **No endpoint under `/users/me` is rate limited**, and three of them now cost real work: `change-password` pays two Argon2 operations (a comparison and an encode), `change-email` pays a comparison **and sends an email to a real inbox**, and `confirm-email-change` pays a comparison. Deferred on the reasoning that a valid bearer token is itself a guard — the caller must already hold an unexpired access token for that exact account — and, for the email change specifically, that the code goes to an address the caller may not control, so grinding buys nothing. That is weaker than it sounds if a token is ever stolen: a thief could still spend the account's mail reputation by requesting changes in a loop. The policies to reuse already exist — `credentialCheck` and `emailDispatch` — and the key would be the account id rather than the network address. **Trigger: any authenticated endpoint that sends mail or costs a hash going live without another guard**, or the first evidence of grinding. | standing |
| `spring-boot-configuration-processor` only runs because `maven.compiler.proc=full` is set in `pom.xml`. **JDK 21 requires the option to be set explicitly**; without it the processor is silently skipped and no metadata is generated. | done |
| ~~`HttpStatusEntryPoint` returns a bare 401 with no body~~ — **closed 2026-08-14** by `ApiErrorAuthenticationEntryPoint`. Was scheduled for 4.4, slipped, and was caught by re-reading `SecurityConfig` rather than by any test. | done |
| No `AccessDeniedHandler`, so a 403 from Spring Security itself would still answer with a bare body. **Phase 7 arrived and did not trigger it, as expected but for a sharper reason than the one recorded here**: vehicle ownership is enforced in the repository query and a vehicle somebody else owns answers 404, so no authorization rule reaches the filter chain and no 403 is producible. Every rule is still `anyRequest().authenticated()` with no roles. Building the handler now would mean inventing a rule to test it against. **Trigger unchanged: the first real authorization rule on the chain** — realistically the admin surface, or shared vehicle ownership, neither of which is V1. Both places that take an entry point take a handler too. | standing |
| Surefire now sets `argLine` for the Mockito agent. JaCoCo also writes `argLine`, so when coverage is added the value must become `@{argLine} -javaagent:...` or one plugin will silently overwrite the other. | 14 |
| `eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y` **cannot be installed yet.** Verified against the registry on 2026-08-14: the latest release is 6.10.2 and its peer range is `eslint ^3 \|\| … \|\| ^9`, while this project runs **ESLint 10.8.0**. There is no newer version and no prerelease tag — only a `v5-backport` line. Forcing it past the peer check was rejected: a lint plugin running outside its supported range is the kind of thing that appears to work and silently stops reporting. **Trigger: a jsx-a11y release whose peer range includes ESLint 10.** If that does not arrive by the time the frontend has real forms, revisit the 2026-08-12 choice of ESLint over Oxlint, because the plugin was its entire justification. | 5 |
| ~~Five specification citations in the Java source cite the wrong sections~~ — **closed 2026-08-15.** `EmailProvider` said 22 for the RO/EN email templates (it is 6), `LoggingEmailProvider` said 30 for the ban on logging codes (it is 27), and `GlobalExceptionHandler`, `SecurityConfig` and `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` all cited 30 for "internal detail must not reach the client" — a rule section 30 does not contain, being the development phase order. Corrected in comments only; no behaviour changed. Found by reading the specification directly rather than trusting the notes, which is the only way this class of error surfaces. | done |
| Node version not yet pinned in the repository. Add `.nvmrc` and `engines` so GitHub Actions and Cloudflare Pages resolve the same version. | 14 |
| **Accounts left behind by browser walkthroughs** in the Neon `development` branch: `test-flow@example.com` (2026-08-15) and `walkthrough-8@example.com` (2026-08-16, verified, holding one vehicle — "Dacia Duster", `B 200 ZOOM` — kept on purpose so the next walkthrough has a certificate to open without building one first). Harmless in development and deliberately not cleaned up — a real signed-up account is useful for the next walkthrough. **They must not reach the production branch**, which is a different database; the point of recording them is that nobody later mistakes them for real users while reading the table. A third account was added on 2026-08-17 for the Phase 10 walkthrough, holding one vehicle (`CT 10 DOC`, a Chevrolet Aveo) with an RCA and its renewal, kept on purpose so the document screens have something to show. **That vehicle carries a real VIN**, copied out of habit from the certificate used for OCR calibration — the value is deliberately not written here, because this file is published, and it is one more reason the development branch must never be promoted. The 2026-08-22 walkthrough left three more marks on that same account: an **ITP document expiring 2026-08-23** on that vehicle, a **photograph** on it, and the account's **notification time moved from 09:00 to 14:08** — the last one is the only leftover worth undoing, since it will otherwise send that account's reminders in the middle of the afternoon. A throwaway vehicle created for the deletion test (`B 99 TMP`, invented VIN) was deleted at the end and left nothing behind but an empty directory. | 19 |
| ~~**Push tokens are protected at rest by the database and not by the application.**~~ — **closed 2026-08-23, one phase early, and the trigger written into the entry is what closed it.** It said "before the first real device registers … while the table is still empty so no re-encryption migration is needed", and that condition was still true, so `V11` could delete every row rather than re-encrypt them — a luxury phase 17 would not have had. `PushTokenCipher` (AES-256-GCM), `PushTokenConverter` and the `push_token_hash` blind index; the key is `garajul-meu.push.token-key`, with no default. **One consequence for anybody starting the backend locally**: that key must be added to the gitignored `application-local.yml` or the application refuses to boot, which is the intended behaviour and not a fault. The objection recorded below still stands and was accepted rather than answered — the key does live in the same environment as everything else, so an attacker holding the environment defeats it; what it defends is a database dump leaking on its own, which is the likelier of the two. Original entry: | ~~17~~ |
| **Push tokens are protected at rest by the database and not by the application.** Section 10.7 offers "application/infrastructure level" and V1 takes the second: Neon encrypts at rest, the column is plain text there. The case this does not defend is a database dump leaking on its own — application encryption would, at the cost of a key that would live in the same environment as the Firebase credential, so a compromise of that environment defeats both. **Trigger: before the first real device registers**, which is phase 17, and while the table is still empty so no re-encryption migration is needed. Deferred rather than forgotten, and the classification rules — never logged whole, never in a response — are in force and tested today. | 17 |
| **`RefreshTokenService.sha256` and `common/Sha256Hex` are the same eight lines.** The second was written on 2026-08-23 for the push-token blind index; the first has existed since phase 4. Not merged on the day, because the two have different reasons to exist — one is a storage digest that must never be reversible, the other a search key — and collapsing them would put a shared utility under two unrelated security arguments. **Trigger: a third caller**, at which point the duplication stops being a coincidence. | standing |
| **A renewal entered without a start date does not become the current record**, so the dashboard goes on reporting the policy it replaced — URGENT, EXPIRED, whatever the older row says — until that row's own expiry passes. Section 11's tiebreak prefers the greatest explicit `valid_from` and treats a missing one as already effective, which loses; the implementation matches the specification exactly. **Confirmed in a browser on 2026-08-17**, and shown to disappear the moment the renewal is given a start date. The fix under consideration is **prefilling** the renewal form's start with the day after the superseded record ends — visible and editable, which is why it does not contradict 10.3's refusal to default it silently. **Trigger: the first person who renews and is still told they are about to expire**, or the reminder work in Phase 11, which will read the same rule to decide what to send. | 11 |
| **The `I` family is unreadable to Enterprise Document OCR**, so `registrationDate` (`I`) and `certificateIssueDate` (`I.1`) are never proposed and are always typed. Two documents out of two: on the blank template `I` produced no token and `I.1` was misread as `1.1`; on a filled certificate where both are printed *and* carry values, neither code was recognised at all. Not a mapping defect — the provider did not see them. The `1.1` alias was considered and dropped, because it fixes the sample that does not matter and misses the one that does. **Trigger: a different processor, a newer processor version, or a photograph at higher resolution reading `I` at all.** | standing |
| **The owner's and user's addresses (`C.2.3`, `C.3.3`) propose their first printed line only.** A real address occupies three lines; the mapper reads to the right of a code, not below it. Measured on 2026-08-17 and deliberately left: the address's own lines are 0.036–0.038 apart and the unrelated `C2 = C1` row sits **0.031** below the last of them, so no vertical-gap rule separates them, and a fixed line count or a left-margin test would be fitted to one document rather than measured from it. An address that silently gains `C2 = C1` is worse than one the user completes, because the first is wrong and looks right. **Trigger: a second and third filled certificate to measure**, at which point the rule can be derived rather than guessed. | standing |
| **Rough edge, not a defect:** an identifier typed by hand into `/garage/<not-a-uuid>` gets `VALIDATION_ERROR` from the backend, which the frontend translates as "Verifică datele introduse." — sensible wording for a form, less so for an address. Left alone deliberately: the alternatives are a special case in the frontend for one error code on one screen, or reversing the 7.1 decision that an unconvertible path parameter is a client mistake rather than a missing resource, which was taken for a good reason. It is reachable only by editing the URL. **Trigger: any evidence real users reach it**, or a second endpoint with a path parameter making the wording wrong twice. | standing |

### Open decisions

- **Standing note, not a task: a screen with almost nothing on it is where layout defects hide.** Three of the four defects found on 2026-08-31 were invisible on every screen that had content, and obvious the moment a brand-new account looked at one. The floating footer needed a dashboard with no vehicle to list; the 25-pixel form links needed somebody to look at sign-in rather than at a list; and the collapsed measure was found on the not-found page, where the only content is a heading. **The habit worth keeping is to walk the empty version of a screen, not only the populated one** — and the empty versions are exactly what a first-time user meets.

- ~~**The header language switcher does not stick for a signed-in person.**~~ — **resolved 2026-08-31, the same day it was found, by the first of the three options.** `AuthProvider.chooseLanguage` now changes i18next and then, only when the status is `authenticated`, writes `preferredLanguage` to the account and feeds the answer back through `profileChanged`. **The rule stayed where the rule lives**: the switcher is a control and knows nothing about accounts, and `unknown` is treated as "no account to tell" rather than as signed out. **The interface changes first and the account is told after** — the other order would hold the application on a round trip to redraw one word. **A refused write is swallowed**, which loses durability and not the choice: the language holds for the life of the page, exactly the old behaviour, and the header has no error region worth inventing for a preference. Verified in a real browser rather than only in jsdom: the switch sends `PATCH /api/v1/users/me` → 200, Romanian survives a full reload where it previously reverted to English with `localStorage` rewritten, and screen 15's own language select then reads the same value the header shows. Two tests were added, and **the one that matters asserts the request body, not the screen** — the screen looked right for the whole time this was broken. 272 tests in 48 files. Original entry:
- **The header language switcher does not stick for a signed-in person, by design, and it reads as a broken control.** Measured 2026-08-31 in a real browser: choosing Romanian re-renders the application in Romanian and writes `ro` to `localStorage`; the next page load restores the session, `AuthProvider.applyLanguageOf` applies the account's `preferredLanguage`, and the screen comes back English with `localStorage` rewritten to `en`. Every step is deliberate — section 6 makes the account's preference outrank the device, which is what carries a language from a laptop to a phone — and the switcher is what makes it look wrong, because it offers a choice it then silently discards. Three ways out: **write through to the profile when authenticated**, so the header control means the same thing as the profile screen and section 6 still holds; hide the switcher once signed in, so the choice is only offered where it lasts; or leave it as a per-session override and say so on screen. The first is recommended and is the only one that costs an API call. Not a regression — the old `<select>` behaved identically since Phase 5 — and found only because the flags made someone look at it closely.
- Mac availability for Phase 18 (iOS) not yet confirmed. Blocks nothing before Phase 17.
- Google Document AI processor version must be verified as currently supported at Phase 9; deliberately not frozen in advance.
- ~~Spring Security Argon2 encoder implementation~~ — **resolved 2026-08-13.** `Argon2PasswordEncoder.defaultsForSpringSecurity_v5_8()`, with the reasoning in the `ro.garajulmeu.security` section. Argon2 itself was always the frozen algorithm.

### Carried over from specification section 35

- Final product name, logo, colours and production domain.
- Normalised X/Y/width/height coordinates for certificate overlay fields — calibrated in Phase 8, never guessed.
- Final OCR confidence thresholds — calibrated against representative samples.
- ~~Capacitor secure-storage plugin — selected during the mobile phase against current documentation.~~ — **resolved 2026-08-31: `@aparajita/capacitor-secure-storage` 8.0.0.** Two plugins still exist for Capacitor 8 and both were read rather than recalled. The deciding line was the cipher: this one states AES-GCM with a key from the Android KeyStore, and GCM is authenticated, so a tampered ciphertext is detected rather than decrypted into rubbish. `capacitor-secure-storage-plugin` names neither mode nor construction and keeps a branch for API < 18 that stores base64 — unreachable at `minSdk 24`, and a path that exists in order to be reached one day. **The reading also settled an older argument.** `channel.ts` chose build-time selection over `Capacitor.isNativePlatform()` on the *expectation* that such plugins ship a `localStorage`-backed web shim; both of them do — one unencrypted and saying so, the other base64, which is an encoding and not encryption. The expectation is now evidence.
- Final legal wording, retention periods and privacy notices before release.
